# Sudoku Generator — Design Document

> **Note (one repository).** This document describes the app as built when it was a repository of its own, and it is kept as written. The generator and the player now live in one repository: paths such as `js/core/…`, `docs/…` or `tests/harness.js` are in `core/`, `generator/…`, `player/…` or `docs/` — see [where the old paths went](../docs/architecture.md#where-the-old-paths-went).

> **Document language:** English (translated from the original Vietnamese specification at the user's request; the Vietnamese UI strings quoted in §7.4 and §11 are product copy and stay in Vietnamese).
> **Version:** 1.2 · **Status:** Implemented — all tasks of [`task.md`](./task.md) are done. The sections below describe the implementation as built. Where it differs from the first plan the text says so, and [§18](#18-implementation-notes--deviations) lists every deviation in one table. This repository holds **the generator only**: it was split off a combined project, and the trial-play mode now lives in the sibling repository [sudoku-player](https://github.com/tdat00/sudoku-player), which opens the puzzles this one hands over ([§10.9](#109-hand-off-to-the-player-exchange)).
> **Related documents:** [`task.md`](./task.md) — the task list the agents worked from, with a completion summary (Appendix E) · the user guide, [`USERGUIDE.md`](../USERGUIDE.md) (its Sudoku Generator part; in Vietnamese [`USERGUIDE.vn.md`](../USERGUIDE.vn.md)) — how to use the app · the developer guide, [`DEVELOPERGUIDE.md`](../DEVELOPERGUIDE.md) (in Vietnamese [`DEVELOPERGUIDE.vn.md`](../DEVELOPERGUIDE.vn.md)) — how to run, test and extend · `docs/qa/final-review-v1.md` — final QA report (written before the split and before the UI rework of §10.1, so its screenshots and UI notes are a snapshot).

## 1. Overview & Goals

### 1.1 Product description

A static web app written in **plain HTML + CSS + JavaScript** — no framework, no build step — that lets users:

- Generate Sudoku puzzles in **many different variants**.
- Choose a **target difficulty** and be guaranteed that the generated puzzle lands exactly at that difficulty.
- **Step through** the generation process — this is the key differentiator. The site does not just "spit out" a Sudoku puzzle; it **teaches users how a Sudoku puzzle is made**, one visible change of the board at a time.
- Switch instantly between **English and Vietnamese** across the entire UI and all explanatory content.

### 1.2 Goals

| # | Goal | Measurable criterion |
|---|------|----------------------|
| G1 | Fast generation | 9×9 Normal < 150 ms (p95) on a mid-range desktop |
| G2 | Guaranteed unique solution | 100% of generated puzzles have **exactly one solution** (unique solution) |
| G3 | Guaranteed difficulty | ≥ 95% of generated puzzles fall within the difficulty band the user selected |
| G4 | Support all 15 variants | See [§5](#5-variant-catalogue--mapping) |
| G5 | Visualise the process | Every replay step has: a title, a bilingual description, a visible change on the grid, a score table, and during the dig the running counters (§7.3, §7.5) |
| G6 | Fully bilingual | 0 hard-coded strings; 100% of translation keys have both `en` and `vi` |
| G7 | Never block the UI | Every task > 50 ms runs in a Web Worker or is sliced across frames |
| G8 | Reproducible | Same `seed` + same configuration ⇒ the same Sudoku puzzle, 100% of the time |

**Status:** G1–G8 are met as far as they can be measured in Node (`tests/qualify.js`, rating v2: 4,715 exact hits of 4,720 requests on the offered levels, no invalid or unrated puzzle, docs/difficulty-rating/qualification.md; `tests/perf.js`: p95 inside every budget of §9.1; `tests/fixtures/regression.js`: the same seed gives the same puzzle). G7 holds in worker mode; in main-thread mode it holds up to the granularity limit of one unsplittable piece of work (§9.3). Real-browser timing on the reference machine of §9.1 was not measured.

### 1.3 Non-goals

- No backend, no user accounts, no cloud storage.
- No scoring or leaderboards. Solving the puzzles is the job of the separate **Sudoku Player** app (notes, undo/redo, error checking, technique hints, a timer), which receives a puzzle from this app through the "Play online" hand-off ([§10.9](#109-hand-off-to-the-player-exchange)); this app contains no play mode.
- No support for IE11 or any browser without ES2020.
- No npm/bundler/transpiler in the final product.

---

## 2. Technical Constraints

### 2.1 What "pure HTML/JS/CSS" means

| Constraint | Decision |
|------------|----------|
| No bundler, no transpiler | Use classic `<script>` tags (classic script); **no ES Module syntax** (`import`/`export`) |
| No external libraries | Write everything ourselves: RNG, solver, test harness, i18n, SVG renderer |
| Modularity is still required | Use a **single global namespace `SG`** + one IIFE per file |
| Must run inside a Worker | `importScripts()` can only load classic scripts ⇒ reinforces the decision above |
| Must run when opened via `file://` | `new Worker()` and `fetch()` are blocked on `file://` ⇒ **a fallback mechanism is mandatory** |

> ⚠️ **Key architecture decision (ADR-001):**
> All code uses the following **UMD-lite** pattern, so that the same file runs in 3 environments: the main page, a Web Worker, and Node.js (for headless tests).
>
> ```js
> (function (root) {
>   'use strict';
>   const SG = root.SG = root.SG || {};
>   // ... definitions
>   SG.rng = { /* ... */ };
> })(typeof self !== 'undefined' ? self : globalThis);
> ```
>
> **Strictly forbidden:** `import`, `export`, `require()` in the `js/` directory. Files that hold state a running page must keep (`SG.i18n`, `SG.store`, `SG.app`, `SG.workerClient`, `SG.cache`, …) start with a guard such as `if (SG.i18n && typeof SG.i18n.t === 'function') return;`, so loading a file twice never replaces the live instance.

### 2.2 Environment matrix

| Environment | Worker | Status | Notes |
|-------------|--------|--------|-------|
| Chrome/Edge via `http(s)://` | ✅ | Full | Primary environment |
| Firefox / Safari via `http(s)://` | ✅ | Supported target | Not part of the manual checks: only Chromium-based Edge was run (see §18) |
| Opening `file://index.html` directly | ❌ | Has fallback | Runs on the main thread in cooperative slices of ≈12 ms (`SG.pipeline.runSteps` driven by `SG.workerClient`, yielding through a `MessageChannel` round trip); the pre-generator of `SG.cache` is off |
| Node.js ≥ 18 | — | Tests only | `tests/node-runner.js` loads files with `vm.runInThisContext` |

### 2.3 Target browsers

Chrome/Edge ≥ 100, Firefox ≥ 100, Safari ≥ 15.4. Allowed features: `BigInt`, optional chaining, `Array.prototype.flat`, `structuredClone` (with a small polyfill), CSS `:has()` (with a fallback), CSS Custom Properties, `Intl`. In practice the code uses ES2020 syntax, `Intl` (`NumberFormat`, `PluralRules`), `MessageChannel`, `localStorage`, `<dialog>`, SVG and, for one cosmetic highlight, CSS `:has()`; `structuredClone` is used only where it exists. No polyfill is shipped.

## 3. Overall Architecture

### 3.1 Layering

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  UI LAYER  (js/ui/)                                          │
│  app · controls · board-view(SVG) · step-view · explain      │
│  player · result-view · symbols · i18n · store(URL+storage)  │
│  cache · export · exchange · a11y · theme · help             │
└───────────────▲──────────────────────────────┬───────────────┘
                │ GenerationEvent[]            │ GenerateRequest
                │ (progress/step/done/error)   │ (variant, difficulty, seed…)
┌───────────────┴──────────────────────────────▼───────────────┐
│  ORCHESTRATION LAYER  (js/worker/)                           │
│  worker-client  ──►  gen.worker.js   │  Main-thread fallback │
└───────────────▲──────────────────────────────┬───────────────┘
                │                               │
┌───────────────┴──────────────────────────────▼───────────────┐
│  GENERATION LAYER  (js/core/)                                │
│  pipeline · full-grid · digger · symmetry · difficulty       │
└───────────────▲──────────────────────────────┬───────────────┘
                │                               │
┌───────────────┴──────────────────────────────▼───────────────┐
│  SOLVER LAYER  (js/core/)                                    │
│  dfs-solver (counting) · logical-solver (human techniques)   │
│  tech-basic/subset/fish/wing/chain/cage/ineq                 │
└───────────────▲──────────────────────────────┬───────────────┘
                │                               │
┌───────────────┴──────────────────────────────▼───────────────┐
│  MODEL LAYER  (js/core/ + js/variants/)                      │
│  model (cells/units/constraints) · constraints · bits · rng  │
│  registry + 15 variant builders                              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Dependency rule:** a lower layer **never** knows about a higher layer. The core layers (`js/core/`, `js/variants/`) **must not** touch `document`, `window.location`, or any other DOM API — that is the precondition for headless testing with Node.

### 3.2 Main data flow

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant U as User
    participant UI as app.js
    participant WC as worker-client
    participant W as gen.worker
    participant P as pipeline
    U->>UI: Choose variant + difficulty → click "Generate"
    UI->>WC: generate(config, onEvent)
    WC->>W: postMessage({type:'generate', session, config})
    W->>P: runSteps(config, emit) — in slices of ≈12 ms
    loop Every step / progress milestone
        P-->>W: emit(GenerationEvent)
        W-->>WC: postMessage({type:'events', session, events})  — batches, ≈30 ms
        WC-->>UI: onEvent(event)  → trace buffer (and the app's `generate:progress` event)
        UI-->>U: The page shows the empty board and the Cancel button; no text follows the events
    end
    P-->>W: PuzzleResult
    W-->>WC: postMessage({type:'done', session, result})  — SG.pipeline.toWire
    WC-->>UI: resolve(puzzle)  — SG.pipeline.fromWire rebuilds the model
    UI-->>U: Show the finished puzzle + the Summary tab (score table) + a new entry in the list of generated puzzles
    UI->>WC: replay(result, { onProgress }) — once the puzzle is shown (§7.3, §9.3)
    WC->>W: postMessage({type:'replay', session, result})  — SG.replay.toPlainWire, then SG.replay.prepare in slices
    W-->>WC: postMessage({type:'replayDone', session, analysis})
    WC-->>UI: resolve(analysis)  → the replay steps and their score tables (SG.player.buildViews)
```

In main-thread mode the same `runSteps` generator is driven by the client itself, without `postMessage` ([§9.3](#93-web-worker--fallback-mechanism)); the replay analysis then runs on the page only after the user presses "Prepare score tables" ([§7.3](#73-replay-engine-in-the-ui-layer)).

### 3.3 Separating "real speed" from "viewing speed"

This is the part of the "show every step" requirement that is easiest to get wrong.

> **ADR-002 — Never slow the algorithm down for the sake of animation.**
> The worker runs **at full speed**. Besides its events (the *trace*), the digger keeps a compact **dig log** of the dig that became the puzzle (`PuzzleResult.digLog`, §13.9). The UI shows the finished puzzle at once and builds the **replay** from that log afterwards (its analysis, `SG.replay`, §7.5, rates the board after every move once, in the worker or, in main-thread mode, when the user asks); nothing plays by itself, the user steps through it by hand ([§7.3](#73-replay-engine-in-the-ui-layer)). It replaced a player that played the trace back at a chosen speed (§18).
>
> - Benefit: the actual generation time is unaffected; users can go to any step, forwards or backwards, at their own pace.
> - The replay does not depend on the trace: the dig log is recorded whatever the verbosity, it is the same in a worker and on the main thread and on every board size, and it holds decisions only (a few hundred small entries even on Samurai, about 15 kB as JSON).
> - Mode `trace: 'off' | 'summary' | 'full'` still selects the events. The page sends no `trace` (the "Replay detail" option is gone), so the pipeline's default `summary` applies; its step events go to the trace buffer and to the `generate:progress` event of `SG.app` (the page does not show them while a run goes on). `full` (every `micro` event) is for direct callers and the tests. `SG.pipeline` downgrades `full` to `summary` for boards with more than 300 cells (`SG.pipeline.FULL_TRACE_MAX_CELLS`; warning `trace.downgraded`, event `buildModel.traceDowngraded`), which among the 15 variants is the case for Samurai (369 cells) only (Sohei, 288, keeps `full`). Full recording costs less than 35% of the generation time (asserted by `tests/manual/perf.test.js` for whole generations and by `pipeline.test.js` for digging).
> - Memory limit: the trace is down-sampled when it exceeds `maxTraceEvents` (default 20 000, `SG.events.capTrace` / `createTraceBuffer`); every non-`micro` event is kept, the `micro` events are thinned evenly, and what the dropped ones changed is carried by synthesised `trace.coalesced` events, so applying a capped trace still ends on the same board. The replay of the page does not read the trace, so the limit no longer shapes what it shows.

---

## 4. Unified Puzzle Model

> **This is the heart of the design.** Every variant — from Mini 4×4 to 5-grid Samurai, from Killer to Greater-Than — is reduced to **a single data structure**. That is what lets us get by with **one** solver and **one** generator.

### 4.1 Core idea

Any Sudoku puzzle = **a constraint satisfaction problem (CSP)** consisting of:

1. A set of **cells** numbered `0 … N-1`.
2. Each cell has a **domain** — a subset of `{1 … D}`.
3. A set of **units (all-different groups)** — each unit is a list of cell indices that must hold pairwise distinct values.
4. A set of **extra constraints** — sum cages, inequalities, …
5. **Display (layout)** information — the drawing coordinates of each cell, region borders, background colours.

**Important consequences:**

- *Rows, columns, 3×3 boxes, Jigsaw regions, diagonals (Sudoku X), the 4 windows (Hyper)* → are all just **units**. No special-case code is needed.
- *Samurai / Butterfly / Flower / Sohei* → overlapping grids that **share cell indices**. A cell in an overlap region belongs to the units of both grids. There is no need for a separate "multi-grid solver" — it is simply a larger CSP.
- *Wordoku / Color / Alphabet Sudoku* → differ only in the **displayed symbol set**. The core always works with the integers `1…D`.

### 4.2 Structure definitions

```js
/**
 * @typedef {Object} PuzzleModel  — immutable once built
 * @property {string} variantId        'classic9' | 'jigsaw9' | 'samurai' | ...
 * @property {number} cellCount        N — total number of unique cells
 * @property {number} digits           D — number of values (4, 6, 9, 12, 16)
 * @property {Int32Array[]} units      units[u] = array of the cell indices of unit u
 * @property {Int32Array[]} cellUnits  cellUnits[c] = array of the indices of the units containing cell c
 * @property {Int32Array[]} peers      peers[c] = array of "neighbour" cells (sharing ≥1 unit)
 * @property {UnitMeta[]} unitMeta     { kind:'row'|'col'|'box'|'region'|'diag'|'window'|'cage', gridIndex, label, partial? }
 * @property {Cage[]} cages            Killer — [] if unused
 * @property {Ineq[]} inequalities     Greater-Than — [] if unused
 * @property {Layout} layout           Drawing information
 * @property {SymbolSet} symbols       { mode, labels[] } — labels[v-1] is the text drawn for value v
 * @property {Int32Array} fixedCells   One entry per cell: 0 = free, v > 0 = forced to v (e.g. the Wordoku line); used by the full-grid generator only
 */

/** @typedef {Object} Cage   @property {Int32Array} cells @property {number} sum @property {boolean} distinct */
/** @typedef {Object} Ineq   @property {number} a @property {number} b  // constraint value[a] < value[b] */

/**
 * @typedef {Object} Layout
 * @property {number} rows, cols            Size of the drawing canvas (e.g. 21×21 for Samurai)
 * @property {Int32Array} cellRow, cellCol  Canvas coordinates of each cell
 * @property {Int32Array} cellRegion        The cell's main region (box/region) → used for drawing borders & colouring
 * @property {Array<{cells:Int32Array,kind:string}>} overlays  Hyper windows, X diagonals…
 * @property {Array<{gridIndex:number,originRow:number,originCol:number}>} subGrids  Multi-grid
 */
```

### 4.3 Representing the solve state

Separate the **model (immutable)** from the **state (mutable)** so that copying/restoring is cheap:

```js
/**
 * @typedef {Object} SolveState
 * @property {Int32Array} candidates  candidates[c] = bitmask of the values still possible
 *                                     bit (v-1) = 1 ⇒ value v is still possible
 * @property {Int32Array} value       value[c] = the value already fixed, 0 if not yet
 * @property {number} unsolvedCount
 */
```

> **Why `Int32Array` bitmasks:** `D ≤ 16`, so every value domain fits in the low 16 bits of an `int32`. Intersection/union/count (`popcount`) run as bit operations — roughly 20–50× faster than `Set`/`Array` — and an `Int32Array` is copied by a single memcpy. Note the allocation rule in §9.2 R5: `.slice()` is fine for one-off copies, but inside hot loops copy with `.set()` onto a pre-allocated scratch buffer so the copy allocates nothing.

### 4.4 Why `peers` is precomputed

`peers[c]` = the set of cells from which a value must be eliminated when cell `c` is fixed. Computing it once when the model is built turns constraint propagation into a linear loop over a flat array — there is no need to walk the units again.

### 4.5 Model validation

Every variant builder **must** pass `SG.model.validate(model)`:

| Check | Description |
|-------|-------------|
| V1 | Every unit has exactly `D` cells (except special units marked `partial`) |
| V2 | Every cell index in the units lies within `[0, cellCount)` |
| V3 | Every cell belongs to ≥ 1 unit |
| V4 | `peers` is symmetric: `b ∈ peers[a] ⟺ a ∈ peers[b]` |
| V5 | `cellRow`/`cellCol` are pairwise unique (no two cells are drawn on top of each other) |
| V6 | Cages: no cell shared between two cages; feasible sum (`minSum ≤ sum ≤ maxSum`) |
| V7 | Inequalities: `a ≠ b`, no contradictory cycle (`a<b` and `b<a`) |
| V8 | At least one complete solution exists (trial-run `dfs.solve` with a small budget: 50 000 nodes / 500 ms; it only runs when V1–V7 pass, and running out of budget is a warning, not an error) |

`validate` returns `{ ok, errors, warnings }`; `validate(model, { solve: false })` skips V8. A Killer cage with at least two cells is also registered as a `partial` all-different unit (`kind: 'cage'`, label `K<n>`), which is how the engine makes the cells of a cage peers of each other.

## 5. Variant Catalogue & Mapping

### 5.1 Summary table

| ID | Name (VI) | Name (EN) | D | Cells | Extra units | Extra constraints | Complexity |
|----|-----------|-----------|---|-------|-------------|-------------------|------------|
| `classic4` | Sudoku Cổ điển 4×4 | Classic Sudoku 4×4 | 4 | 16 | — | — | ⭐ |
| `classic6` | Sudoku Cổ điển 6×6 | Classic Sudoku 6×6 | 6 | 36 | 2×3 boxes | — | ⭐ |
| `classic9` | Sudoku Cổ điển 9×9 | Classic Sudoku 9×9 | 9 | 81 | 3×3 boxes | — | ⭐ |
| `jigsaw9` | Sudoku Ghép hình | Jigsaw Sudoku | 9 | 81 | 9 irregular regions | — | ⭐⭐⭐ |
| `mega12` | Sudoku Khổng lồ 12×12 | Mega Sudoku 12×12 | 12 | 144 | 3×4 boxes | — | ⭐⭐ |
| `mega16` | Sudoku Quái vật 16×16 | Monster Sudoku 16×16 | 16 | 256 | 4×4 boxes | — | ⭐⭐⭐ |
| `diagonalX` | Sudoku Đường chéo (X) | Diagonal Sudoku (X) | 9 | 81 | 2 diagonals | — | ⭐⭐ |
| `hyper` | Sudoku Siêu vùng | Hyper Sudoku (Windoku) | 9 | 81 | 4 windows (3×3) | — | ⭐⭐ |
| `killer` | Sudoku Sát thủ | Killer Sudoku | 9 | 81 | — | Cage (sum + all-different) | ⭐⭐⭐⭐ |
| `greaterThan` | Sudoku So sánh | Greater-Than Sudoku | 9 | 81 | — | Inequalities between adjacent cells | ⭐⭐⭐⭐ |
| `samurai` | Sudoku Samurai | Samurai Sudoku | 9 | 369 | 5 overlapping grids | — | ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ |
| `butterfly` | Sudoku Cánh bướm | Butterfly Sudoku | 9 | 144 | 4 overlapping grids | — | ⭐⭐⭐⭐ |
| `flower` | Sudoku Bông hoa | Flower Sudoku | 9 | 189 | 5 overlapping grids | — | ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ |
| `sohei` | Sudoku Sohei | Sohei Sudoku | 9 | 288 | 4 grids in a cross layout | — | ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ |
| `wordoku` | Sudoku Chữ | Wordoku | 9 | 81 | (optional diagonal) | Cells fixed by a word | ⭐⭐⭐ |

**Presentation-only layer, applied on top of any variant** (`js/ui/symbols.js`; switching it never regenerates the puzzle):

| Symbol mode | VI | EN | Notes |
|-------------|----|----|-------|
| `digits` | Chữ số | Digits | Default. The variant's own labels: Mega 16×16 draws `0`–`F` |
| `letters` | Chữ cái | Letters | A, B, C, … |
| `colors` | Màu sắc | Colours | A 16-entry palette (`--sg-board-sym-1..16`) **plus** a small geometric symbol so that colour is never the only carrier of the value; the symbol can be switched off in Settings (`colorAuxSymbol`) |
| `shapes` | Hình dạng | Shapes | 16 distinct geometric shapes (circle, square, triangle, diamond, star, plus, hexagon, heart, …) |
| `word` | Chữ của từ khóa | Word letters | Wordoku only (its default); the letters of the key word |

Which modes a variant offers is its `symbolModes`: `digits`, `letters`, `colors`, `shapes` for every variant except Mega (`digits` only — its symbol set is the variant option `symbolSet`, see [§8.6](#86-mega-1212--1616)) and Wordoku (`word`, `digits`).

**Levels offered** (`difficultySupport`; the UI only lists these, and `SG.pipeline` refuses the others with `difficulty.unsupportedLevel`). Rating v2: the levels each variant passed in the qualification (`node tests/qualify.js`, protocol and table in [docs/difficulty-rating/qualification.md](../docs/difficulty-rating/qualification.md)):

| Variant | Levels |
|---------|--------|
| `classic4` | veryEasy (its puzzles need nothing beyond singles) |
| `classic6` | veryEasy, hard (a list with a gap: easy and normal are never reached, expert … nightmare only by about half of the requests) |
| `classic9`, `wordoku` | veryEasy … hell1 (hell2 is rarely reached under the default symmetry) |
| `mega12`, `mega16`, `diagonalX`, `hyper`, `jigsaw9`, `killer`, `greaterThan`, `samurai`, `sohei`, `butterfly`, `flower` | all nine, veryEasy … hell2 |

### 5.2 Multi-grid geometry

Each sub-grid is a standard 9×9 grid placed at an origin `(originRow, originCol)` on a shared canvas. Cells at coinciding canvas positions are **the same cell** (they share an index).

| Variant | Canvas | Sub-grid origins | Overlap region | Unique cells |
|---------|--------|------------------|----------------|--------------|
| `samurai` | 21×21 | (0,0) (0,12) (12,0) (12,12) (6,6) | 4 boxes (3×3) | 369 |
| `butterfly` | 12×12 | (0,0) (0,3) (3,0) (3,3) | 6×6 central region | 144 |
| `flower` | 15×15 | (3,3)=centre, (0,3) (6,3) (3,0) (3,6) | each petal overlaps the centre in a 6×9 area | 189 |
| `sohei` | 21×21 | (0,6) (6,0) (6,12) (12,6) | 4 boxes (3×3), empty middle | 288 |

> ⚠️ **Validation requirement:** the builder must self-check that every overlap region is **aligned with the 3×3 box boundaries** (origin offset divisible by 3) and that it computes exactly the number of unique cells listed in the table. On a mismatch ⇒ throw an error while building the model; never skip it silently.

### 5.3 Rules for adding a new variant

A new variant only needs to register a definition with `SG.variants.register`:

```js
SG.variants.register({
  id: 'diagonalX',
  i18nKey: 'variant.diagonalX',
  group: 'extraRegions',            // UI grouping; 'group.<id>' must exist in the locale packs
  digits: 9,
  difficultySupport: ['veryEasy','easy','normal','hard','expert','master','nightmare','hell1','hell2'],
  defaultSymbolMode: 'digits',
  symbolModes: ['digits','letters','colors','shapes'],
  options: [ /* schema of variant-specific options, used to auto-generate the UI */ ],
  build: function (opts, rng) { /* → PuzzleModel */ },
  // Optional: hooks into the pipeline
  postGenerate: null,   // e.g. Killer generates cages once the full grid exists (a twin `postGenerate.steps` makes it resumable)
  digStrategy: null,    // e.g. Greater-Than digs "signs" instead of "cells" (a strategy id registered with SG.digger.registerStrategy)
  fastSeed: null,       // e.g. pre-fill the diagonal boxes before the full-grid search
  // Optional: tuning
  restartThreshold: undefined,                       // failed guesses per full-grid attempt (Mega: 300 / 400)
  clueFloor: undefined,                              // own clue floor per level, see §6.7
  clueFloorScale: { normal: 0.83, hard: 0.8, expert: 0.8, master: 0.8 }   // calibration of the derived floor, see §6.7
});
```

`difficultySupport` lists the levels the variant offers: exactly those that pass the qualification of `node tests/qualify.js --variant=<id>` ([docs/difficulty-rating/qualification.md](../docs/difficulty-rating/qualification.md)); a list may have gaps.

`register` validates the definition (a typo fails when the file loads, not in the middle of a run) and rejects a duplicate `id`. The UI never reads `options` directly: `SG.variants.optionsOf(id)` returns them in one normalised shape (§13.8). The worked example of a complete new variant is in the developer guide, [`DEVELOPERGUIDE.md`](../DEVELOPERGUIDE.md#adding-a-variant).

You **must not** add `if (variant === 'x')` anywhere in `js/core/`. Every difference must go through the registry.

---

## 6. Core Algorithms

### 6.1 Seeded RNG

Mandatory for reproducibility (G8) and for stable tests.

- Algorithm: **mulberry32** (32-bit, a single state word, period 2³², good distribution, ~3 lines of code).
- Initialisation: `seed` is a `uint32`. If the user does not supply one, use `Date.now() ^ (Math.random()*2**32)`.
- API: `next()` → `[0,1)`, `int(n)` → `[0,n)`, `pick(arr)`, `shuffle(arr)` (Fisher–Yates), `fork(tag)` → an independent child RNG (so that a change in a later step does not alter the result of an earlier step).

> **Why `fork` matters:** if one shared RNG is used for both "full grid generation" and "hole digging", modifying the digging algorithm would also change the full grid, rendering regression tests useless. Each phase takes its own child stream. The names are a stability contract (same seed + configuration ⇒ same puzzle, guarded by the hashes of `tests/fixtures/regression.js`): `model` (variant `build`), `grid#A` (full grid of attempt A), `extra#A` (`postGenerate`), `dig#A.T` (dig sample T of attempt A). `fork` derives the child from `(seed, tag)` with a 32-bit FNV-1a string hash. The UI hashes a free-text seed with the same FNV-1a over the NFC-normalised, trimmed text (§14.2).

### 6.2 Step 1 — Full grid generation

**Goal:** produce a complete, valid solution with a uniformly random distribution.

**Algorithm: randomised DFS + constraint propagation + MRV.**

```
function generateFullGrid(model, rng, budget):
    state ← initState(model)              # every cell has all D candidates
    applyFixedCells(state, model)          # Wordoku: the diagonal is predetermined
    if not propagate(state): fail
    return dfsFill(state, rng, budget)

function dfsFill(state, rng, budget):
    if budget.exceeded(): throw TimeoutError
    c ← the unassigned cell with the smallest popcount(candidates[c])   # MRV
    if no unassigned cell remains: return state                         # done
    if popcount(candidates[c]) == 0: return FAIL
    for v in shuffle(candidatesOf(c), rng):
        snapshot ← state.cloneInto(scratch[depth])              # .set() into pooled buffer, no alloc
        assign(state, c, v)
        if propagate(state) and dfsFill(state, rng, budget) ≠ FAIL:
            return state
        state ← snapshot                                        # backtrack
    return FAIL
```

**Optimisation specific to `classic9`:** fill the 3 boxes on the main diagonal first (they are independent of one another ⇒ only 3 random permutations of 1…9 are needed), then run DFS on the rest. This cuts ~40% of the time. It is an **optional** optimisation registered through the registry (`fastSeed`), not a special case inside the core. Mega 12/16 pre-fill the diagonal boxes of their box grid the same way, and the multi-grid boards use `fastSeed` to decide the cells that several grids share (`fastSeedOverlaps`) before the search starts.

**For large / multi-grid boards** (`mega16`, `samurai`, `flower`, `sohei`): plain DFS can wander for a long time. Apply a **budgeted restart**: if the number of failed guesses exceeds `restartThreshold` (default `cellCount × 50`; a variant may declare its own — Mega 12/16 use 300 / 400, which `SG.fullGrid.generate` reads from the variant definition unless its caller passes the option), abandon the attempt and start over with an independent child stream. After 50 restarts the last attempt runs without a limit. This strategy (a Las Vegas restart) is in practice much faster than persisting on a bad branch.

### 6.3 Constraint propagation

Runs to a fixpoint, using a worklist to avoid rescanning everything:

| Rule | Applies to | Description |
|------|------------|-------------|
| P1 — Peer elimination | All variants | Fixing `c=v` ⇒ clear bit `v` from the `candidates` of every `peers[c]` |
| P2 — Naked single | All variants | `popcount(candidates[c]) == 1` ⇒ fix it immediately |
| P3 — Hidden single | All variants | Within a unit, if value `v` is still possible in only one cell ⇒ fix that cell |
| P4 — Cage sum bounds | Killer | For a cage: `sum - maxRemaining ≤ cellValue ≤ sum - minRemaining`; combined with the sum-combination lookup table |
| P5 — Inequality propagation | Greater-Than | `a<b` ⇒ `max(cand[a]) < max(cand[b])`, `min(cand[b]) > min(cand[a])`; two-way pruning |
| P6 — Contradiction detection | All variants | A cell with 0 candidates, or a unit with no room left for some value ⇒ FAIL immediately |

> **P4 — cage sum combination table:** precomputed when `combo-table.js` loads; `SG.comboTable.table[size][sum]` = array of bitmasks of the feasible value sets (512 masks in all, a few KB, built for digits 1–9 only). It makes Killer affordable: a cage is reduced to a handful of digit sets and O(1) lookups. P4 and P5 are not built into `constraints.js`: they are **propagation extensions** registered through `SG.constraints.registerExtension({ id, prepare, propagate })` — P4 by `SG.comboTable.enablePropagation(variantId)`, P5 by `SG.ineq.ensureExtension()` — and they register **lazily** (on the first Killer / Greater-Than build), so a plain page load does not touch the engine. A model that is built by any other route (`SG.pipeline.fromWire`, `SG.cache` deserialisation) must call the same functions before `SG.dfs` is used on it, otherwise the DFS silently ignores cage sums and signs.

### 6.4 DFS solver that counts solutions (`dfs-solver`)

```
solutionCount(model, givens, limit = 2) → 0 | 1 | 2    // 2 means "≥ 2"
```

- Stop early as soon as the `limit`-th solution is found — **never** count them all.
- Shares `propagate` with the grid generator.
- Takes a `budget` (max nodes + a time limit) and a cancellation token.

### 6.5 ⭐ Unique-solution check — the optimised version

This is **performance hotspot #1**: it is called hundreds of times during hole digging.

**Naive approach:** remove the cell → `solutionCount(puzzle) == 1`? → cost = re-solving the whole puzzle from scratch every single time.

**Optimised approach (used in this project):**

> **Applied theorem:** Suppose puzzle `P` currently has a unique solution and we remove the clue at cell `c` (true value `v`), producing `P'`. Then **`P'` still has a unique solution if and only if, for every `w ∈ candidates_{P'}(c), w ≠ v`, the puzzle `P' ∪ {c = w}` has no solution.**
>
> **Why:** every solution of `P'` assigns some value to `c`. The original solution assigns `v`. A second solution exists ⟺ it assigns some `w ≠ v` to `c` (if it assigned `v`, it would also be a solution of `P`, and `P` is unique ⇒ it coincides with the original solution).

```
function stillUnique(model, givens, c, v):
    cands ← candidatesAfterPropagate(model, givens, c)   # usually only 2–4 values remain
    for w in cands where w ≠ v:
        if hasSolution(model, givens ∪ {c = w}):
            return false          # found a second solution → cell c must be put back
    return true
```

**Why it is much faster:**

| | Naive | Optimised |
|---|-------|-----------|
| Number of solves | 1 solve, which must find both solutions | `k-1` solves (k = number of candidates, usually 2–4) |
| Cost of each solve | Find solution 1 (easy), then turn the whole search space upside down looking for solution 2 (very expensive when none exists) | Each solve is a puzzle that has been **forced wrong** ⇒ constraint propagation usually detects the contradiction within a few steps |
| Measured | ~1.0× | **≈ 3.7–4×** on the dig-time calls where the removal keeps the puzzle unique (`solver.test.js` asserts ≥ 3×), but only ≈ 1.8× over a whole dig, because refused removals cost both approaches about the same. The ~5–12× of the first estimate was too optimistic; the narrower reading of the criterion was accepted |

Two additional minor optimisations:
- **Reuse propagation results:** keep the `state` after propagating `P'` once; each `w` only clones from that state.
- **Order `w` heuristically:** try the `w` with the least support first — it usually hits a contradiction earliest.

### 6.6 Logical solver based on human-player techniques (`logical-solver`)

This solver is both the **difficulty yardstick** and the **difficulty filter** during hole digging.

```js
SG.logicalSolver.solve(model, givens, {
  allowedTechniques: ['nakedSingle','hiddenSingleBox', ...],  // null = every registered technique that applies to the variant
  collectSteps: true,
  assumeUniqueness: true,   // false: skip techniques that rely on "one solution" (uniqueRectangle)
  stopOnStuck: true,
  budget
})
// → { solved, status: 'solved'|'stuck'|'contradiction', steps: [...], techniqueCounts: {...},
//     hardestTechnique, score, state, solution? }
```

The loop: repeatedly **try the techniques in a fixed technique-priority order** (ascending `order`; from rating v3 on the order follows the weights of the score: they are nondecreasing along it, ties allowed, and `tests/suites/technique-weights.test.js` asserts it over the registry as it is), apply the first finding of the first technique in that order that has an effect, and start again from the top, until the puzzle is solved or the solver is stuck (stuck is a normal result, never an error). Techniques are plain registry entries (`SG.techniques.register({ id, group, baseCost, order, i18nKey, variantScope, assumesUniqueness, find })`; the contract is the header of `js/core/techniques.js`); the solver never changes when one is added. A finder is a function of the model, the state and the options of its call: the budget checks of the subset finder, for instance, are spaced by search nodes counted from the start of each call, so earlier calls cannot move them.

> **Singles are techniques.** The solver deliberately never calls `SG.constraints.propagate`: that engine places naked and hidden singles on its own, so they would be consumed silently — neither attributed nor scored — and `allowedTechniques: ['nakedSingle']` would still solve puzzles that need hidden singles. It starts from a peer-consistent grid and applies every finding with `assign` / `eliminate` only. Cage sums and inequalities are likewise left to their techniques (`tech-cage.js`, `tech-ineq.js`) instead of the propagation extensions.

#### Technique ladder

40 techniques are registered: **34 classic** (the 20 rows of the first plan, with Hidden Single split by unit kind and the two Quads split into Naked / Hidden, and, since rating v3, the skyscraper, the two-string kite, the remote pairs, the empty rectangle, the finned X-Wing, the finned Swordfish, the WXYZ-Wing, the long forcing chain, the deep Nishio, BUG+1, the restricted XY-chain and, since MS15b, the ALS-XZ and, since MS16, Sue de Coq), **1 Jigsaw** (the Law of Leftovers, group `locked`, scoped to Jigsaw 9, since MS14b), **3 Killer** (`cage` group) and **2 Greater-Than** (`ineq` group). `order` is the position in the loop (×10 spacing, so a technique can be inserted); the *design score* is the first plan's `baseCost` (what the technique registers, and what the solver's own raw score adds up), the *weight* is what `SG.difficulty` scores with (`SG.difficulty.WEIGHTS`, rating v2, see below).

| `order` | `id` | Technique (EN) | Group | Design score | Weight |
|--------:|------|----------------|-------|-------------:|----------------:|
| 10 | `nakedSingle` | Naked Single | `basic` | 1 | 1 |
| 20 | `hiddenSingleBox` | Hidden Single (box/region) | `basic` | 2 | 1.25 |
| 30 | `hiddenSingleLine` | Hidden Single (row/col) | `basic` | 3 | 1.5 |
| 40 | `pointing` | Pointing Pair/Triple | `locked` | 7 | 40 |

| 50 | `boxLine` | Box/Line Reduction | `locked` | 7 | 50 |
| 60 | `nakedPair` | Naked Pair | `subset` | 9 | 120 |

| 194 | `alsXz` | ALS-XZ (two disjoint almost locked sets of 1 to 4 cells, a restricted common digit X, and a digit Z in both: Z leaves every other cell that sees all the Z cells of both sets; MS15b) | `chain` | 106 | 1250 |
| 196 | `sueDeCoq` | Sue de Coq (a row or column crossing a box or region: two or three open cells I of the crossing, an almost locked set A in the line outside the box and an almost locked set B in the box outside the line, their digits disjoint and inside the digits C of I with \|C\| = \|I\| + \|A\| + \|B\|; the digits of A leave the other cells of the line, those of B the other cells of the box, the left-over digit of I both; MS16) | `chain` | 112 | 1300 |
| 198 | `forcingChainLong` | Long Forcing Chain (shortest proof of C + 1 to 12 links) | `chain` | 118 | 1400 |

| 202 | `nishioDeep` | Deep Nishio (depth 2; 3 only when the caller asks for it) | `chain` | 210 | 2600 |

`cage` techniques have `variantScope: ['killer']` and `ineq` techniques `['greaterThan']`: the solver and the digger only use a technique whose scope contains the model's variant. The Vietnamese names of the techniques live in the locale pack (`technique.<id>`), which is authoritative; the first plan's Vietnamese names were not kept.

**Order and weights (rating v3).** The first ladder had three inversions between the loop order and the weights: `cageCombo` (weight 40) after `boxLine` (50), `ineqChain` (40) after `nakedPair` (120) and `jellyfish` (520) after `xyWing` (650) and `xyzWing` (750). Rating v3 changes the orders, not the weights: `cageCombo` 55 → 44, `ineqChain` 65 → 46, `jellyfish` 160 → 137. Along the registry the weights are now nondecreasing (equal weights are allowed), so the floor of a level is always a prefix of the ladder of a variant (§6.7). Three ties of weight keep their calibrated outcome through the larger `order`: `ineqChain` over `pointing`, `nakedQuad` over `ruleOf45` and `jellyfish` over `cageSplit` (the hardest technique of a rating). The test fixtures keep the rating v2 positions as history (`SG.fixtures.techniques.historyV2`, with the v2 `ladder`) next to the current order (`registryOrder`).

**The chain search seam (rating v3, MS07a).** `core/js/engine/tech-chain.js` exports two parameterised search kernels: `SG.chainSearch.forcing(model, state, ctx, { id, minLength, maxLength, workLimit, edges })` and `SG.chainSearch.nishio(model, state, ctx, { id, minDepth, maxDepth, workLimit })`. They are the searches of `forcingChain` and `nishio` with the id, the Finding's `technique` and the prefix of its `i18nKey` (`technique.<id>.step[.kind]`) as parameters; MS07a registered nothing on them, and since MS07b the four chain techniques are their wrappers (next paragraph; the fifth, `xyChain`, is the MS12 wrapper of the restricted mode, below). `edges` (forcing only, MS12) is the explicit edge mode of the shared graph builder: `'general'` (the default, the graph and the three proofs described here, unchanged) or `'restricted'` (the XY-chain graph and its only proof, "The XY-chain" below); any other value is `solver.invalidOption`, and no general registered finder reads it. The inclusive range is applied to the **shortest** proof of the whole search: the search starts at length (depth) 1, runs up to the maximum and keeps the minimum (ties: the first in the scan order of the registered finder; iterative deepening for Nishio), and a minimum below the requested minimum gives `null`. A longer or deeper technique called alone therefore never relabels a proof that a shorter one owns, and nothing is searched from the minimum up. `length` counts edges; for the two chains of a `contradiction` or `forced` proof it is the longer one, not the sum (the two differ by an odd number: every edge changes the polarity of the literal and the proof ends in both polarities of one node). The seam clamps nothing, so that tests can use chains of more than 12 edges; a registered wrapper intersects its own range with the explicit maximum of its caller. **Work (R10).** A forcing unit is one edge expansion of the breadth-first search and a Nishio unit one trial; the counter is reset per call; an absent `workLimit` means the default 50,000 and `null` or `Infinity` means unbounded (the seam normalises both; `forcingChain` and `nishio` pass it explicitly). **A bounded call never lets more than 256 work units pass between two checks of the parent budget (IR52, MS07b):** it checks at entry and before every root of a forcing scan, remembers the work done at its last check and checks again before an expansion that would carry the gap over 256; an expansion of more than 256 edges is split into pieces of at most 256, every edge visited and counted once and in the order of the search (also for a Finding in the middle of a piece, a cut by the work limit or a budget that runs out). An unbounded call keeps the cadence it always had (no entry check and no periodic check; the root checks of the forcing scan and the trial checks of Nishio were always there). A spent limit ends the call with `null`, never with the best proof so far (a proof is published only when its minimum is established); a spent budget is "nothing found" as for the registered finders, so the record of such a call has `capHit: null` exactly like a complete scan, and a caller checks its own budget after the call; cancellation and every other error propagate. The optional observer `ctx.options.onPatternWork(record)` (it passes through the solver options like any unknown key) gets a fresh `{ id, work, limit (null: unbounded), capHit (null | 'work'), retained (null) }` per call and never selects a result. The cost of the finders on their fixtures is measured with `tests/pattern-cost.js` (§15.4).

**The registered windows (rating v3, MS07b; the XY-chain row is MS12).** Each of the five chain techniques is a wrapper that hands the seam its id, both bounds and its work limit explicitly (no kernel default ever stands in), so every proof belongs to exactly one id:

| `id` | shortest proof | work limit | caller option that can only narrow it |
|------|----------------|------------|---------------------------------------|
| `xyChain` | 1 to 12 edges over the restricted edges (3, 5 to 11 occur) | 50,000 | `maxChainLength` |
| `forcingChain` | 1 to C links | none (the accepted unbounded policy; the parent budget still applies) | `maxChainLength` |
| `forcingChainLong` | C + 1 to 12 links | 50,000 | `maxChainLength` |
| `nishio` | depth 1 | none (as `forcingChain`) | `maxNishioDepth` |
| `nishioDeep` | depth 2 (default maximum 2, at most 3) | 50,000 | `maxNishioDepth` |

C is `SG.chainSearch.SHORT_MAX`, **4** (selected by task MS07c, below), read at every call so that a test or a tool can change it. The options only narrow: absent, non-numeric or `NaN` is the id's own default maximum, a number is floored and clamped to the id's ceiling (`Infinity` therefore reaches 3 for `nishioDeep`, not its default 2), and `0`, a negative number or a maximum below the id's minimum is an empty window (`null`, never a raised maximum). The weights are 1400 for `forcingChainLong` and 2600 for `nishioDeep` (the technique of the Hell 1 tier: the tier's help lists it, so the level is no longer vacuous); depth-2 proofs that were `nishio` are now `nishioDeep`, and proofs of 5 to 12 links that were `forcingChain` are `forcingChainLong`.

**The selected cutoff (rating v3, MS07c).** The owner's rule (R9) was fixed beforehand: measure C = 4 and C = 6 on fresh generation requests, take the cutoff with **more exact hits in total**, and C = 6 on a tie. The experiment is the three Nightmare cells that carry chains (classic9, diagonalX and Jigsaw 9), 12 requests each from the soak base seed 20260101 (36 requests per cutoff, 72 in all), every other default fixed (the shallow allowance 256, the attempts of the board, the weights, the bands), a hit being the generator's own `exact` flag and every error or timeout a miss (there was none). C = 4 has **36** exact hits (classic9 12, diagonalX 12, Jigsaw 9 12) and C = 6 has **24** (2, 10 and 12), so the default is 4. The command seconds of the six cells were 12.3 s at C = 4 against 121.8 s at C = 6, the sampled references and the final paths agree with the exact flags in all 72 rows, and no request failed an invariant; the cells, the ledgers and the other measurements are in [docs/difficulty-v3/chain-window-2026-11-15.md](../docs/difficulty-v3/chain-window-2026-11-15.md). `forcingChainLong` (a Nightmare-tier technique, 1.25 × 1400 below 2400) now owns every shortest proof of 5 or more links, so a puzzle whose proof needs one is exact at Nightmare, and a chain of 5 or 6 links no longer counts as the Master technique `forcingChain` (1.25 × 1100 below 1500). The only production change is the literal `INITIAL_SHORT_MAX` of `core/js/engine/tech-chain.js`; the long ceiling 12, the depths, the weights, the work limits and the orders are unchanged.

**The skyscraper (rating v3, MS08a).** `skyscraper` (group `fish`, order 112, base cost 33, weight 330, unscoped, `assumesUniqueness: false`) names the smallest single-digit pattern that Simple Colouring or a chain used to prove. For a digit *d*, take two base lines of one orientation (rows or columns): complete units, disjoint (no cell of either lies in another line of its kind, so overlapping sub-grids give no skyscraper, never a wrong one), each with *d* in exactly two cells and no *d* placed. Each line holds *d* once, so one of its two cells is *d*. Every one of the four cells has exactly one cover line (the owner line of the other orientation); exactly **one** cover line holds a cell of each base line. Those two cells, the *roots*, see each other and cannot both be *d*, so at least one of the other two cells, the *ends*, is *d*, and *d* leaves every other cell that sees both ends (real peers, so Jigsaw regions and extra regions count; a root or an end is never a target). Two shared cover lines are an X-Wing, which `xWing` owns, and none is no pattern. The search takes the digits in order, rows then columns as base lines, then the pairs of lines in line order, and returns the first pattern that removes something. Finding: `{ technique: 'skyscraper', cells (the four cells, ascending), placements: [], eliminations (ascending), i18nKey: 'technique.skyscraper.step', i18nArgs: { digit, lines (the two base lines), ends (the two ends, ascending) } }`; the arrays are the call's own and the state is only read. **Work (R10):** one unit is one tested pair of base lines, with a limit of 50,000 per call (`SG.fishSearch.SKYSCRAPER_WORK_LIMIT`; a classic 9×9 call tests at most 648 pairs, a 16×16 board 3,840, and the measured cost on the four fixtures is 3 to 74 pairs, so the limit binds on no real board); the counter is local to the call, the parent budget is checked at entry and before the pair that would carry the work since the last check over 256 units, a spent limit ends the call with `null` (a lost deduction, never an invented one), a spent budget is "nothing found" as for the chain finders, and cancellation and every other error propagate. The observer `ctx.options.onPatternWork` gets `{ id, work, limit, capHit (null | 'work'), retained (null) }` per call, and `SG.fishSearch.skyscraper(model, state, ctx, { workLimit })` is the unregistered seam the tests use for an explicit limit. Its weight puts it in the tier of Hard (1.25 × 330 is above Normal's maximum and below Hard's) and, as a technique below Expert's weights, in the floor of Expert and above; no tier or floor rule is changed. **Effect on the references (MS08a):** the 26 labelled reference puzzles keep every level and every `v3Expected` entry (three of the Hard ones are rated by a skyscraper now: scores 560, 563 and 554 become 475, 521 and 515; `constructed-nightmare-3` goes from 2,642 to 2,653); of the 520 regression hashes 66 change, all in the cells whose level admits the skyscraper (`classic6/hard`, `classic9/hard` to `nightmare`, `diagonalX/nightmare`, `hyper/hard`, `jigsaw9/master`, `greaterThan/hard`); of the 14 replay goldens six analyses change (G01, G04, G06, G11, G12, G14) and three wires (G09, G11, G12), and 5 of the 28 existing help examples are made again: four from their own sources (`pointing`, `swordfish` and `xyWing` only change the number of hints applied before their hint, `simpleColouring` its snapshot) and `xyzWing` from another real source of the catalogue because its old puzzle no longer needs one; `skyscraper` is new (the source of the `simpleColouring` example, Hell 1 seed 214). The old rating-v2 positions of the fixtures stay as history (`historyV2`); the new fixtures name their positions with explicit lists, and the chain positions now include `skyscraper` in their `after` lists.

**The two-string kite (rating v3, MS08b).** `twoStringKite` (group `fish`, order 114, base cost 34, weight 340, unscoped, `assumesUniqueness: false`) names the other small single-digit pattern that Simple Colouring or a chain used to prove: a row and a column that each hold a digit *d* in exactly two cells, linked through one box (or jigsaw region). For a digit *d*, take a row and a column that are complete units, disjoint from the other lines of their kind (as for the skyscraper, so overlapping sub-grids give no kite, never a wrong one), each with *d* in exactly two cells and no *d* placed, and four distinct cells. Each line holds *d* once. If one cell of the row and one cell of the column lie in the same box (a *box-like* unit: a complete unit of kind `box` or `region`, the region being the box of a Jigsaw board, that is the only box-like unit owning the cell, counted over both kinds; an overlapped box of a multi-grid board owns no cell uniquely, and a diagonal or a window is no box), these two cells, the *bridge*, see each other and cannot both be *d*, so at least one of the other two cells, the *ends*, is *d*, and *d* leaves every other cell that sees both ends (real peers; a bridge cell or an end is never a target). The search takes the digits in order, then the rows, then the columns (both in line order), then the bridge's row cell and its column cell in ascending cell order, and returns the first pattern that removes something. Finding: `{ technique: 'twoStringKite', cells (the four cells, ascending), placements: [], eliminations (ascending), i18nKey: 'technique.twoStringKite.step', i18nArgs: { digit, lines (the row and the column, in unit order), bridge (the two bridge cells, ascending), ends (the two ends, ascending) } }`; the arrays are the call's own and the state is only read. The ownership of the lines is the whole-line disjointness of the skyscraper (never weaker than the fish search's `findFish`), so on a board whose rows or columns overlap only the lines that no other line of their kind touches take part, and only a cell with a single box-like owner can bridge: the pattern can fire on the Samurai, Sohei and Flower (27, 18 and 6 disjoint rows and as many columns), never on the Butterfly (no disjoint line); on a Jigsaw board (nine disjoint rows and columns, its nine regions are the boxes) it fires as on a classic board, and a board without any complete box-like unit has no kite and costs no work; the tests hold a negative for an overlapped box (alone, and as a box and a region over the same cells), a negative through an overlapped box of the Samurai next to a kite in the middle band of its center grid, and compare with an independent enumeration on all four multi-grid boards and on the irregular jigsaw board and its transpose. **Work (R10):** one unit is one tested pair of a row and a column that both hold *d* twice, with a limit of 50,000 per call (`SG.fishSearch.KITE_WORK_LIMIT`); the counter is local to the call, the parent budget is checked at entry and before the pair that would carry the work since the last check over 256 units, a spent limit ends the call with `null` (a lost deduction, never an invented one), a spent budget is "nothing found", and cancellation and every other error propagate; the observer `ctx.options.onPatternWork` gets `{ id, work, limit, capHit (null | 'work'), retained (null) }` per call and `SG.fishSearch.twoStringKite(model, state, ctx, { workLimit })` is the unregistered seam of the tests. **Structural maximum:** at most one unit per digit, disjoint row and disjoint column: 729 on every 9×9 board (Classic, Diagonal, Hyper, Jigsaw, Killer, Greater-Than, Wordoku), 216 on 6×6, 64 on 4×4, 1,728 on the 12-digit and 4,096 on the 16-digit board, 6,561 on the Samurai (the largest, 13 % of the limit; 27 disjoint rows and columns), 2,916 on the Sohei, 324 on the Flower, 0 on the Butterfly (and 0 on any board without a complete box-like unit); the limit therefore binds on no board, and the tests reach it with an explicit `workLimit`. The measured cost on the five fixtures is 7 to 98 units (11 and 22 on the two positives). Its weight puts it in the tier of Hard (1.25 × 340 is above Normal's maximum and below Hard's); no tier or floor rule is changed, and the skyscraper helpers (`endEliminations`, `findBounded`, `seamOf`) are shared, which leaves the skyscraper's result unchanged (the skyscraper of the base commit and the one of the tree give the same Finding and the same observer record on 2,336 states of every agreement setup, also under explicit limits of 0, 1 and 3; its 155 tests are unchanged). **Effect on the references (MS08b):** the 26 labelled reference puzzles keep every level and every `v3Expected` entry; four ratings change by 1 to 13 points (`escargot` 5,280 to 5,279, `worlds-hardest-2012` 5,605 to 5,592, `constructed-nightmare-2` 2,662 to 2,661 and `-3` 2,653 to 2,652); of the 520 regression hashes 18 change (`classic9/hard` seed 89, `classic9/expert` seeds 8, 377 and 10,946, `classic9/master` seeds 5 and 377, `classic9/nightmare` seed 377, `diagonalX/nightmare` seeds 1, 5, 21, 55, 144 and 610, `hyper/hard` seed 5, `jigsaw9/master` seeds 5, 13, 144 and 233; `classic6/hard`, `killer/hard` and `greaterThan/hard` admit the technique and stay unchanged, as do the other 16 seeds of `jigsaw9/master`); of the 14 replay goldens six analyses change (G01, G05, G06, G07, G11, G14: the rise of G01 is now the Two-String Kite step at R2C4, 47 to 593; G06 and G07 are Jigsaw requests: G06 ends at 2,327 instead of 2,329, and G07 digs another puzzle, 2,391 instead of 1,797, whose jump comes one step later) and two wires (G10, G11); of the help examples `twoStringKite` is new (Hell 1 seed 214, step 36), `hiddenTriple` and `xWing` are made again from their own sources (only the number of hints applied before their hint changes) and `simpleColouring` from another real source of the catalogue (Nightmare seed 648) because its old puzzle now needs a kite instead.

**The empty rectangle (rating v3, MS08c, restricted form).** `emptyRectangle` (group `fish`, order 124, base cost 38, weight 380, unscoped, `assumesUniqueness: false`) names the grouped implication of a box that holds a digit *d* in two arms, with a conjugate pair outside it. For a digit *d* take a *box-like* unit *U*: a complete unit of kind `box` or `region` (the region being the box of a Jigsaw board, as for the kite; the owners are counted over both kinds; a diagonal or a window is no box) that does not hold *d* yet. Its candidates (the open cells that can still be *d*) must split into a **row arm**, the candidates in one row *r*, and a **column arm**, those in one column *c*, both nonempty, with no candidate at the cell where *r* and *c* meet (the *empty intersection*); a candidate in a third line, or at the intersection, gives no rectangle (that is the general rectangle, which the restricted form does not claim; the real negative `ER_GENERAL` of the fixtures has one). Every candidate needs a single owner, as in the candidate ownership of `findFish` (never weaker than it): *U* is the only box-like unit that holds it, and it has exactly one complete row and one complete column, so an overlapped box of a multi-grid board gives no rectangle (whole-line disjointness is not needed, the lines are used only through the candidates). *U* holds *d* once. An **external conjugate pair** is any complete unit *V* in which *d* is not placed and exactly two cells *p* and *q* can still be *d* (the strong links of Simple Colouring: a row, a column, a box, a region, a diagonal or a window, never a partial unit), with *p* and *q* both outside *U*: exactly one of them is *d*. Call *A* an arm whose every cell *p* sees (real peers) and *B* the other arm. If *p* is *d*, *d* leaves all of *A*, so a cell of *B* is *d*; if *q* is *d*, *q* is. So *B* or *q* holds *d*, and *d* is removed from every cell that sees every cell of *B* and sees *q*; a candidate of *U*, *p* and *q* are never targets. Both arms are tried as *A* and both cells of the pair as *p* (the roles are explicit in the search and in the independent enumeration of the tests, `referenceRectangles`, and are not encoded in the arrays of the Finding). The search takes the digits in order, then the boxes (unit order), then the conjugate units (unit order), then the one or two rectangles of the box in (row, column) unit order (two only for a pair of candidates in different rows and columns, both with the same arms), then the row arm and the column arm as *A*, then the lower and the higher cell of the pair as *p*, and returns the first pattern that removes something; **that order is the documented first-pattern order, and it is not changed to put a particular witness first**: the vector of the brief (digit 5, box 1, *A* = {R2C1, R2C3}, *B* = {R1C2, R3C2}, the pair R2C5 and R7C5 of column C5, R7C2 loses the 5) holds a second rectangle when its seven cells are all there (R7 is a pair, R7C2 and R7C5, and its rectangle removes R2C5), and the first in order is that one; the tests pin both deductions on the bare vector and the intended R7C2 witness on the vector with a decoy 5 at R7C9 and on its transpose. Finding: `{ technique: 'emptyRectangle', cells (the candidates of the box and the two cells of the pair, ascending), placements: [], eliminations (ascending), i18nKey: 'technique.emptyRectangle.step', i18nArgs: { digit, box (the label of the box-like unit), lines (the row and the column of the arms, in unit order), ends (the two cells of the pair, in cell order) } }`; the arrays are the call's own, the state is only read, and they are **not positional encodings** of *A*/*B* or *p*/*q* (the step text names the roles by description, "the end that sees every digit of one part"). The pattern can fire on every board with complete box-like units: all 9×9 boards (Jigsaw included, where a region is the box), the 4, 6, 12 and 16 digit boards, and on the Samurai, the Sohei, the Flower and the Butterfly only through boxes whose cells have a single box, row and column (37 of the 45 boxes of the Samurai, 28 of 36 of the Sohei, 12 of 45 of the Flower and 4 of 36 of the Butterfly); a board without a complete box-like unit has no rectangle and costs no work. **Work (R10):** one unit is one tested `(digit, box, conjugate unit)` combination, a legitimate combination extension counted before the rejection checks (the ends inside the box, the arms, the visibility); it contains at most eight trials (two rectangles of the box, two choices of *A*, two choices of *p*), and the counter is neither a count of individual targets nor of visibility tests. The limit is 50,000 per call (`SG.fishSearch.EMPTY_RECTANGLE_WORK_LIMIT`); the counter is local to the call, the parent budget is checked at entry and before the unit that would carry the work since the last check over 256 units, a spent limit ends the call with `null` (a lost deduction, never an invented one), a spent budget is "nothing found", cancellation and every other error propagate, the observer `ctx.options.onPatternWork` gets `{ id, work, limit, capHit (null | 'work'), retained (null) }` per call, and `SG.fishSearch.emptyRectangle(model, state, ctx, { workLimit })` is the unregistered seam of the tests. **Structural maximum** (units per call, digits × boxes that can have single owners × complete units, every unit a possible pair): 192 on 4×4, 648 on 6×6, 2,187 on every 9×9 board with the nine rows, columns and boxes (Classic, Jigsaw, Killer, Greater-Than, Wordoku), 2,349 on Diagonal, 2,511 on Hyper, 5,184 on the 12-digit and 12,288 on the 16-digit board, 14,580 on the Flower, 27,216 on the Sohei, 3,888 on the Butterfly and 44,955 on the Samurai (the largest, 90 % of the limit); counted as eight units per combination instead, the Samurai, the Sohei, the Flower and the 16-digit board would exceed the limit, which is why the unit is the combination. The measured cost on the five fixtures is 22 and 63 (positives) and 102, 46 and 20 (negatives) of the 50,000 units, nothing capped, and the largest work of any call in the states of the agreement setups is 313 (the Samurai); the limit was not adjusted. Its weight puts it in the tier of Hard (1.25 × 380 is above Normal's maximum and below Hard's); no tier or floor rule is changed, the helpers shared with the skyscraper and the kite (`findBounded`, `seamOf`, `spend`, `findStrongLinks`) are unchanged, and the skyscraper and the kite of the base commit and the ones of the tree give the same Finding and the same observer record on every one of 18,688 lines of the agreement states (also under explicit limits of 0, 1 and 3). **Effect on the references (MS08c):** the 26 labelled reference puzzles keep every level and every `v3Expected` entry; two ratings change (`platinum-blonde` 5,676 to 5,665 and `constructed-nightmare-2` 2,661 to 2,637, one rectangle step each) and `golden-nugget` uses it once at the same score (5,689); of the 520 regression hashes 29 change (`classic9/hard` seed 4,181; `classic9/expert` seeds 8, 610, 1,597 and 10,946; `classic9/master` seed 13; `classic9/nightmare` seeds 13 and 55; `diagonalX/nightmare` seeds 3, 5, 21, 34, 55, 89, 144, 987 and 10,946; `hyper/hard` seed 2; `jigsaw9/master` seeds 1, 3, 5, 34, 55, 144, 233, 610, 987 and 10,946; `greaterThan/hard` seed 34), all in cells whose level admits it and none below Hard (`classic6/hard`, `killer/hard` and 19 of the 20 seeds of `greaterThan/hard` admit it and stay unchanged); of the five replay wires G09 changes only in the counters of its local search (Killer Expert 3,514,204,290: 318 to 346 ratings) and G11 and G12 (Greater-Than) are other puzzles, and of the 14 analyses G07 (Jigsaw Nightmare 3,962,167,687: 2,391 to 2,310), G11 (Greater-Than Expert 42: 994 to 963, 8 compact and 110 detail steps), G12 (Greater-Than Hard 3,115,968,914: 594 to 647, whose hardest technique is now the empty rectangle) and G14 (Monster Hell 2 seed 12: its jump 1,646 to 1,685, the final score 6,470 unchanged) change; the example of the Generation Steps guides (G01) is unchanged. Of the help examples `emptyRectangle` is new (Hell 1 seed 214, step 23: digit 8, box 5, the pair of column C7), `skyscraper` is made again from another real source (Hard seed 18, step 26; its old puzzle needs a rectangle first), `twoStringKite`, `hiddenSingleBox` and `hiddenTriple` from their own sources (step 36 to 37, 58 to 50 and 34 to 31).

**The finned X-Wing (rating v3, MS09a, sashimi included).** `finnedXWing` (group `fish`, order 126, base cost 40, weight 400, unscoped, `assumesUniqueness: false`) names an X-Wing whose base lines hold extra candidates, the *fins*, all in one box. For a digit *d* take two **base lines**, complete rows (or columns) in which *d* is not placed and at least two cells can still be *d*, and two **cover lines**, complete lines of the other kind. A candidate of a base line is a **body cell** when it lies on a cover and a **fin** when it lies on neither. Every base candidate, fins included, needs a single owner as in the candidate ownership of `findFish` (never weaker): its base line is the only line of its kind that holds it and it has exactly one complete line of the other kind; whole-line disjointness is not required and not added. The pattern needs **a body cell in every base line**, **at least one fin**, and **all fins in one box-like unit**: a complete unit of kind `box` or `region` (the region being the box of a Jigsaw board; the owners are counted over both kinds; a diagonal or a window is no box), the only box-like unit that holds the fin, so an overlapped box of a multi-grid board has no owner and gives no pattern; a board without a complete box-like unit has no finned fish and costs no work. A base line of fins only is a box/line reduction (`boxLine`), and a selection without a fin is an ordinary X-Wing (`xWing`); neither is relabelled. The covers are chosen among the cover lines of the base candidates (a cover without one would have no body cell; the tests confirm this by trying every complete line). The **targets** are the open cells of the covers that can still be *d*, outside both base lines, that see **every fin** through their real peers (row, column, box, region, diagonal, window, extra regions, not membership in the fin box); body cells and fins are never targets. **Why it is sound:** if a fin is *d*, every target sees it and loses *d*; if no fin is *d*, the *d* of each base line is a body cell, the two lie on different covers (a cover holds *d* once), so both covers have their *d* in a base line and no target, which lies outside the base lines, is *d*. **Sashimi** is not a separate pattern, flag, id or key: at n = 2, one body cell in a base line gives the missing-corner sashimi form (the apparent fish corner is not a candidate), and at n = 3 sparse bodies are also permitted by the same proof (an ordinary Swordfish can already have two body cells per base line); a base line with no body cell and a selection with no fin are not sashimi and are excluded. The search takes the digits in order, then rows before columns as base lines, then every pair of base lines in line order, then every pair of covers in unit order, and returns the first pattern that removes something; **that order is the documented first-pattern order, and it is not changed to put a particular witness first**: the six cells of the vector of the brief (digit 5, R1 {C2, C8} and R4 {C2, C8, C9}, the fin R4C9 in box 6, R5C8 loses the 5) alone also form the columns C2 and C8 on the covers R1 and R4 with the fin R5C8, which would remove R4C9, and the row reading comes first; the tests pin both readings on the bare vector, and the intended witness on the vector with a decoy 5 at R9C2 (which ends the second reading) and on its transpose (the vector establishes a witness, not the exclusive ownership of every deduction). Finding: `{ technique: 'finnedXWing', cells (every candidate of the base lines, ascending), placements: [], eliminations (ascending), i18nKey: 'technique.finnedXWing.step', i18nArgs: { digit, bases (the base lines, in unit order), covers (the covers, in unit order), fins (the fins, in cell order) } }`; the arrays are the call's own and the state is only read. The pattern can fire on every board with complete box-like units: all 9×9 boards (Jigsaw included, where a region is the box), the 4, 6, 12 and 16 digit boards, and on the Samurai, the Sohei and the Flower, where every base candidate needs its single row, column and box (9, 7 and 6 of the 12 sampled states of the agreement setup hold a finned X-Wing); the Butterfly, whose 36 rows hold 324 cell places over its 144 cells, held none in 300 sampled states and is covered for agreement only. **Work (R10):** one unit is one tested combination of two base lines, counted before the rejection checks; it contains at most one trial per pair of covers (6 on 4×4, 15 on 6×6, 36 on every 9×9 board, 66 and 120 on the 12 and 16 digit boards, 153 on the multi-grid boards, where the two base lines can have candidates in up to 18 different covers), so the counter is neither a count of individual cover tests nor of targets. The limit is 50,000 per call (`SG.fishSearch.FINNED_X_WING_WORK_LIMIT`); the counter is local to the call, the parent budget is checked at entry and before the unit that would carry the work since the last check over 256 units, a spent limit ends the call with `null` (a lost deduction, never an invented one), a spent budget is "nothing found", cancellation and every other error propagate, the observer `ctx.options.onPatternWork` gets `{ id, work, limit, capHit (null | 'work'), retained (null) }` per call, and `SG.fishSearch.finnedXWing(model, state, ctx, { workLimit })` is the unregistered seam of the tests. **Structural maximum** (units per call, digits × 2 orientations × pairs of complete lines, every pair possible): 48 on 4×4, 180 on 6×6, 648 on every 9×9 board, 1,584 on the 12-digit and 3,840 on the 16-digit board, 11,340 on the Sohei and the Butterfly and 17,820 on the Samurai and the Flower (the largest, 36 % of the limit), so the limit is never reached by a structure; the measured cost on the five fixtures is 66 and 131 (positives) and 92, 196 and 216 (negatives) of the 50,000 units, nothing capped, and the largest work of any call in the states of the agreement setups is 1,901 (the Samurai); the limit was not adjusted. The enumeration is shared with a size parameter (`SG.fishSearch.finnedFish(model, state, ctx, { size: 2 | 3, workLimit })`): size 3 is the finned Swordfish of MS09b, with a limit of its own, and the finned X-Wing's limit and behaviour are unchanged by it. Its weight puts it in the tier of Hard (1.25 × 400 is above Normal's maximum and below Hard's); no tier or floor rule is changed, the helpers shared with the other bounded patterns (`findBounded`, `seamOf`, `spend`, `findStrongLinks`) behave as before, and the X-Wing, the Swordfish, the Jellyfish, the skyscraper, the kite and the empty rectangle of the base commit and the ones of the tree give the same Finding and the same observer record on every one of 35,040 lines of the agreement states (also under explicit limits of 0, 1 and 3 on the seams). **Effect on the references (MS09a):** the 26 labelled reference puzzles keep every level and every `v3Expected` entry; four ratings change (`worlds-hardest-2012` 5,592 to 5,544, `platinum-blonde` 5,665 to 5,675, `constructed-nightmare-1` 2,704 to 2,727 and `constructed-nightmare-2` 2,637 to 2,652, the finned X-Wing being used once, once, twice and twice there; levels and hardest techniques unchanged); of the 520 regression hashes 23 change (`classic9/expert` seeds 13, 55, 233 and 377; `classic9/master` seeds 2, 13, 1,597 and 4,181; `diagonalX/nightmare` seeds 8, 21, 89, 144 and 987; `jigsaw9/master` seeds 1, 3, 5, 144, 233, 610, 987, 4,181 and 10,946; `greaterThan/hard` seed 10,946), none below Hard; of the five replay wires G09 (Killer Expert 3,514,204,290) and G11 (Greater-Than Expert 42) are other puzzles (G09's hardest technique is now the finned X-Wing, final score 968 to 809), G10 (Killer Nightmare 42) changes only in the counters of its local search and G12 and G13 are unchanged; of the 14 analyses G04, G05, G06, G09, G11, G12 and G14 change (5, 11, 70, 80, 170, 4 and 9 fields) and the example of the Generation Steps guides (G01) is unchanged. Of the help examples `finnedXWing` is new (Nightmare seed 1156, step 32: digit 5, base lines R5 and R9, covers C5 and C7, the fin R9C6, R8C5 loses the 5) and `hiddenSingleBox`, `xyzWing`, `wWing`, `uniqueRectangle`, `forcingChain` and `nishio` are made again from their own sources, which only moves their step numbers (50 to 52, 32 to 33, 43 to 44, 45 to 47, 48 to 49 and 17 to 18).

**The finned Swordfish (rating v3, MS09b).** `finnedSwordfish` (group `fish`, order 134, base cost 48, weight 480, unscoped, `assumesUniqueness: false`) is the three-line version of the same proof: a Swordfish whose base lines hold extra candidates, the *fins*, all in one box. For a digit *d* take three **base lines** (complete rows, or columns, in which *d* is not placed and at least two cells can still be *d*) and three **cover lines**, complete lines of the other kind. A candidate of a base line is a **body cell** when it lies on a cover and a **fin** when it lies on neither. The preconditions are those of the finned X-Wing, with three lines instead of two: every base candidate, fins included, has the candidate ownership of `findFish` (its base line is the only line of its kind that holds it and it has exactly one complete line of the other kind; never weaker, and whole-line disjointness is not required), there is **a body cell in every base line and on every cover**, **at least one fin**, and **all fins lie in one box-like unit** (a complete unit of kind `box` or `region`, the only box-like unit that holds each fin, counted over both kinds). The **targets** are the open cells of the covers that can still be *d*, outside the three base lines, that see **every fin** through their real peers; body cells and fins are never targets. **Why it is sound:** if a fin is *d*, every target sees it and loses *d*; if no fin is *d*, the *d* of each of the three base lines is a body cell, the three lie on three different covers (a cover holds *d* once), so every cover has its *d* in a base line and no target, which lies outside the base lines, is *d*. **Sparse bodies:** at n = 2, one body cell in a base line gives the missing-corner sashimi form; at n = 3, sparse bodies are also permitted by the same proof (an ordinary Swordfish can already have two body cells per base line), so a base line with fewer than three body cells is not called sashimi, and there is no separate id, key or Finding field for it; a base line with no body cell, a selection with no fin, two base lines and four base lines are not finned Swordfish. The shared enumeration of `finnedXWing` runs with three lines (`SG.fishSearch.finnedFish(model, state, ctx, { size: 3 })`), and neither technique relabels the other: a finned X-Wing inside a position stays `finnedXWing`'s (order 126, earlier in the loop), an ordinary Swordfish `swordfish`'s, a line of fins only `boxLine`'s. The search takes the digits in order, then rows before columns as base lines, then every combination of three base lines in line order, then every combination of three covers in unit order (the covers that hold a candidate of the base lines), and returns the first pattern that removes something; the order is the documented first-pattern order and is not changed to put a witness first (the eight cells of the vector of the brief, digit 5, R1 {C2, C5}, R4 {C5, C8, C9} and R7 {C2, C8}, the fin R4C9 in box 6, R5C8 loses the 5, also hold the columns C2, C5 and C8 on the covers R1, R4 and R7 with the fin R5C8, which would remove R4C9, and the row reading comes first; the tests pin both readings and the intended witness with a decoy 5 at R9C2 and on the transpose). Finding: `{ technique: 'finnedSwordfish', cells (every candidate of the three base lines, ascending), placements: [], eliminations (ascending), i18nKey: 'technique.finnedSwordfish.step', i18nArgs: { digit, bases (the base lines, in unit order), covers (the covers, in unit order), fins (the fins, in cell order) } }`; the arrays are the call's own and the state is only read. The pattern can fire on every board with complete box-like units (all 9×9 boards, Jigsaw included, the 4, 6, 12 and 16 digit boards) and on the Samurai, the Sohei and the Flower through the base candidates that have a single row, column and box. **The Butterfly cannot fire in a satisfiable state, by structure and not only by sampling:** only 36 of its 144 cells have a single row and column, nine in each of four corner boxes, every row and column that holds such cells holds three of them in one corner box, and the lines of two corners never meet; an eligible base line has two or three candidates in the three cells of one corner box, two such lines of one orientation and digit in one box would put the digit twice in it (no solution), so the three base lines lie in three different corners, only one of them can supply fins (all fins lie in one box), the other two need two covers each, and the finned one a body cover of its own, **at least 2 + 2 + 1 = 5 covers, never three**. A test pins the topology from the model's units and the same shape in a state without a solution (two base lines of one corner box and one of another), which does fire; 120 sampled states of the Butterfly give nothing, as the argument says.

**Work and truncation of the finned Swordfish (R10, MS09b).** One unit is one tested complete combination of three eligible base lines of one digit and orientation, counted before the rejection checks; all the cover choices of that combination are tried inside the unit, in the order above, so the counter is neither a count of cover trials (up to 2,925 on a multi-grid board) nor of visibility checks or targets. The limit is **50,000 per call, its own constant** `SG.fishSearch.FINNED_SWORDFISH_WORK_LIMIT`: the registered finder and the `size: 3` seam take their default from it, never from `FINNED_X_WING_WORK_LIMIT`, which keeps its own value and the behaviour of the finned X-Wing (a test evaluates a copy of the engine with one literal changed to show that neither moves the other); no limit depends on the board and none grows to a structural maximum. The counter is local to the call, the parent budget is checked at entry and before the unit that would carry the work since the last check over 256 units, a spent budget is "nothing found" with `capHit: null`, cancellation and every other error propagate, and the observer `ctx.options.onPatternWork` gets `{ id, work, limit, capHit (null | 'work'), retained (null) }` per call. **A spent limit is a deterministic truncation:** the call returns `null` with `capHit: 'work'`, the searched prefix being the first *limit* combinations of base lines in the order above (and every cover choice inside them); it never proves that no finned Swordfish exists, a finned Swordfish found before the limit is spent is complete and returned as always, and the same state and limit give the same prefix and the same answer.
