The pins that follow from these records are listed with their before and after values in `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS12/evidence.md`: the jump transitions of `replay` (15 to 16 distinct, because G03's compact and details jumps are now different transitions), the unrecorded-choice list of `replay` (G03 step 5 is `combined`), and the "together" specimen of `steps` (G03 no longer has one; G04's jump, "45 and 46, the whole group 915", takes its place, and G05 is unchanged). `digger-target`, `cache`, `worker`, `pipeline`, `mega` and `player` hold. The help catalogue holds **37 examples**: `xyChain` is new (Hell 1 seed 8, step 28: digit 1, cells R3C1, R6C1, R6C6, R8C1 and R8C6, R4C1 loses the 1), `forcingChain` (Nightmare 648, step 30), `nakedTriple` (Hell 2 escargot, step 30) and `jellyfish` (a corpus puzzle, see `player/design.md`) are made again, and the other 33 are unchanged on their own sources.

**The grouped chain graph (rating v3, MS13a, R16; inactive when it was written, read by `forcingChain` and `forcingChainLong` since MS13c).** `SG.chainGraph.build(model, state, ctx[, { groupLimit, workLimit }])` (`core/js/engine/tech-chain.js`) builds the canonical grouped graph of a position and returns `{ nodes, weak, strong, complete, work, capHit }`. **At MS13a it was inactive** (historical: no registered finder, no seam call and no rating read it, so it was not a technique and changed no Finding, observer record, hash, wire, golden or help example). MS13c activated it for `forcingChain` and `forcingChainLong`, **not** for the XY-chain (the paragraph on the active grouped forcing below); `SG.chainSearch.forcing` and the singleton graph with its arithmetic node decoding are unchanged and are what the short ID's fallback runs. **Nodes.** A node is `{ cells, digit }`: `cells` ascending, distinct and non-empty, each an open cell that still holds the 1-based `digit`; a node is true when at least one listed cell holds the digit, so a true group is no permission to place one arbitrary member, and a group node needs this explicit representation and its own decoder, never the `cell * digits + digit - 1` arithmetic of the atomic graph. `nodes` lists the singletons first (every open candidate, ascending by cell and digit), then the **groups** sorted by the numeric key `[digit, ...cells]` element by element with a shorter prefix first (not by decimal string). A group is the set of open cells that hold a digit in the intersection of a complete unit of kind `box` or `region` with a complete unit of kind `row` or `col` that shares at least two cells with it, when at least two of them hold it; identical groups are kept once. Windows (Hyper), diagonals and every other complete unit generate **no** group of their own (SA2's ruling of this task: the family of the MS08b box and region reading) but still give strong links and, through `model.peers`, weak links. **Edges.** *Weak* (at most one is true): the same digit with the nodes disjoint and **every** cross pair of their cells seeing each other (`model.peers`, partial units included; never one convenient pair, never overlapping nodes), or two singletons of one cell. *Strong* (at least one is true): the same digit with two disjoint nodes whose cells are exactly all the open occurrences of the digit in one complete unit where it is not placed (`ctx.completeUnits`; one more occurrence, or a partial unit, gives none), or the two singletons of a bivalue cell. **Work and limits.** One unit is one tested combination: every candidate group (a box or region, a row or column and a digit, charged before the deduplication), every pair of candidates of one open cell and **every** pair of nodes of one digit, charged before the test that their first cells see each other (that prefilter is sound, but the pair scan is quadratic, so it is work and not free setup). The limits are 4,096 additional nodes and 50,000 units, frozen in `SG.chainGraph.LIMITS`; the optional fourth argument replaces either with a finite number of at least 0 (floored, anything else is `solver.invalidOption`; there is no unbounded mode; the allowance that MS13c's grouped attempt shares is explained with the census below). The quota counts distinct groups and is enforced **as they are collected**, so an excess group is never retained: a duplicate costs work and no slot, and exactly the limit is allowed. The parent budget is checked at entry, after the model-only set-up (the peer matrix and the box-line intersections, cached per model, so that a cold and a warm call account the same), once per 256 steps of the uncharged per-call scans and so that no more than 256 charged units pass between two checks. **The census** (`docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS13a/census-all/`, 15 models of the test kit, SA1's review; labelled by population). The **structural maximum** of a board is the blank state (every candidate in every cell gives the most nodes, group candidates and same-digit pairs; recounted by the independent checker): **84,807 units on every 9 x 9 board but Jigsaw 9 (1.7 times the limit; 78,822 on the test kit's Jigsaw 9, 1.6), 321,048 on 12 digits (6.4), 1,209,344 on 16 digits (24.2), 268,704 on Butterfly (5.4), 459,297 on Flower (9.2), 1,047,384 on Sohei (20.9) and 1,715,391 on Samurai (34.3)**; only 6 x 6 (13,590, 0.27) and 4 x 4 (2,144, 0.04) fit. The **positions of the ladder solves** of 6 seeded puzzles per model (test-kit puzzles dug from random solutions, not published puzzles; every position at which `xyChain` is tried because every earlier technique found nothing; Killer and Greater-Than are the test kit's uncaged and uninequalitied models) show that **the limit is reached on the large boards**: Samurai 38 of 48 positions over 50,000 (median 85,352, largest 138,529), Sohei 43 of 51 (median 109,951, largest 124,066), Flower 17 of 44 (median 44,057, largest 70,583) and 16 digits 20 of 321 (median 23,349, largest 57,089), whereas classic9 (5 positions, largest 6,117), Killer and Greater-Than (the same 5), 12 digits (32, 13,644), Butterfly (15, 31,008), Diagonal (84, 16,419), Hyper (67, 15,387) and Jigsaw 9 (26, 8,693) never reach it (4 x 4, 6 x 6 and Wordoku never reach the chain group in the 6 puzzles). Of the real named puzzles only the stuck positions of six classic9 evil puzzles were measured (7,536 to 12,086 units, all complete, `work-census-final.json`). So the default 50,000 returns an incomplete graph (`capHit: 'work'`) at many positions of the large boards; the limit was not raised (SA2's ruling 2), the tests build large graphs with finite overrides, and the policy for such positions was left to MS13c, where the owner decided it (GC-01 option A, S-OD11; the active grouped forcing below). At MS13a the grouped attempt was planned to share one work allowance between construction and search, starting at 50,000, without resetting it between stages, and no whole-call policy for `forcingChain` was decided: GC-01 awaited the owner's choice of a singleton fallback with unbounded local work or a cap on the whole call (the owner chose the fallback, option A). Parent budgets and cancellation remain in force. **Incomplete graphs.** A graph that cannot be completed is `complete: false` with **empty** `nodes`, `weak` and `strong`, never a partial graph, and `capHit` says why: `'work'` (the unit limit; `work` equals it), `'nodes'` (more additional nodes than the quota; the tag of the existing observer vocabulary, which MS13c reuses) or `null` (the parent budget ran out, and the caller's own check fails after the call). `complete: false` is decisive: an empty array never means that no work was done or that no proof exists. Cancellation and every other error propagate and the builder publishes no observer record (IR72 C2). **The independent checker** `SG.chainProofs` (`core/tests/fixtures/chain-proofs.js`) recomputes the expected nodes (the group family included), both edge relations, the exact work and the scan steps from the model and the state alone and compares them with a graph (with the known solution it also reports a weak edge between two true nodes and a strong edge between two false ones); it checks a chain proof for the ascending union of its cells, the legacy ordering, singleton starts for forced and contradiction proofs, the exact endpoint targets and the exact root conclusions. It does not forbid a legitimately placed singleton that also occurs in a group inside its proof (R16 forbids choosing an *arbitrary* member from a group's truth, not forcing a singleton through two contradictory paths; SA2's reproducer is a positive test), while a group start and an arbitrary member stay rejected. **Inactivity (MS13a, historical)** was shown on the same inputs: a differential of 6,370 calls (2,306 with a Finding) of the five registered finders, the seam in both modes and 135 complete ratings (6,129 steps), with their observer records, has the SHA-256 `8c0f69aa5c7c22acb3ec85be9af1907030916f97aa9c7d9450012a7e35b042e3` for the tree and for the base commit `8345e89` (`legacy-diff-tree-final.txt`, `legacy-diff-base-final.txt`); the complete E on the final source (all five wires, all 14 analyses and all 520 hashes, recorded and merge-checked against the unchanged canonical outputs, nothing written) and the 37 help examples are unchanged. **Mutation analysis:** 78 in-memory mutants of the builder and of the checker, 77 detected and 1 equivalent. The equivalent one is the completeness guard of the strong-link cover test: the occurrence table is written only for complete units, so a partial unit has the count 0, and two nonempty nodes have at least two occurrences, so the count test alone already refuses it; the guard stays as an explicit soundness guard. The pack, the evidence and the rulings are in `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS13a/evidence.md`.

**Reading and displaying version-2 group proofs (rating v3, MS13b, R16).** The consumers of a chain Finding's text read `i18nArgs = { proofVersion: 2, kind, length, chains }` whose steps are nodes `{ cells, digit, truth }` (`truth` 1 for true, 0 for false), and still read the legacy triples `[cell, digit, truth]`; no finder emitted a node at that task (the grouped graph above was inactive; the grouped forcing of MS13c emits them since), a pure singleton Finding keeps its triples, and one path may mix the two forms. **Text.** `formatChains` of both apps' `js/ui/i18n.js` keeps the `⇒` between the steps and the `|` between the two paths. A triple, and a node with **one** cell, are written `R1C2=1` or `R1C2≠1` (a disjunction of one member is that member). A node with several cells is written in brackets with **every member labelled**: a **true group is the disjunction of its members, `(R1C1=5 ∨ R1C2=5)`, and a false group is every member false, `(R1C1≠5 ∧ R1C2≠5)`**; it is never a disjunction of inequalities and never one guessed member (a true group does not say which member holds the digit). The cells of a node may be ids (labelled by the cell labeler, as before) or labels already written, and its digit a raw 1-based value (the Generator writes it as it always wrote the digit of a triple) or a symbol already written. **The Player** (`SG.play.localizeArgs`, used by `describeHint`) returns a copy of the arguments and never writes the Finding's: the cells of every node become labels and its digit the symbol that the board draws (a hexadecimal value 1 is `0`, a Wordoku value its letter), in a node and in a triple alike; a step it does not know is copied. **Highlights stay the Finding's `cells`**: `buildHint` copies it, `describeHint` lists it and the board marking uses it, so MS13c has to emit the full sorted union of the nodes' cells (the independent checker `SG.chainProofs` checks it); the UI derives no second set from `chains` and repairs no missing cell. The DOM and SVG marking was read, not run. **Wording.** The locale texts of the two forcing IDs changed in both languages and both apps (32 entries; the exact old and new texts are in `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS13b/locale-delta.md`): the two descriptions say, conditionally ("If a proof step represents a group ..."), what a group is and how it is written, that a candidate removed by the endpoint rule must **conflict with every candidate represented by each end** (different candidates of one cell conflict too, which "seeing cells" does not say) and that chains are searched up to a limited length, with no claim of a work cap; the endpoints, contradiction and forced explanations speak of an *end* and of "a statement about a candidate or group" being both true and false, so that they stay true when an end or an inner node is a group (the root of a forced or contradiction proof stays one candidate). The keys, their prefixes through `{chains}. ` and their placeholders are unchanged. **Still inactive (MS13b, historical).** The Findings, observer records and ratings of the base commit are reproduced byte for byte, and with the new language packs the old formatter and the new one write the same text for every legacy Finding of the same populations; the five wires, the 14 analyses, the 520 hashes and the 37 help examples were unchanged (`docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS13b/evidence.md`). GC-01 was still open at that task, which added no cap, fallback or limit; the owner decided it afterwards (option A, S-OD11) and MS13c implemented it.

**The active grouped forcing (rating v3, MS13c, R16; owner decision GC-01 option A, S-OD11).** `forcingChain` (1 to C links) and `forcingChainLong` (C + 1 to 12) search the canonical grouped graph above and publish the shortest proof **of that graph** from a bounded attempt: at most 4,096 additional nodes and **one** work allowance of 50,000 units shared by the construction and the search (the search starts at the units that the construction spent and is never reset). R10 allowed one measured adjustment of these two limits, common to every board; the census below led to none, so they stay as MS13a froze them. A Finding is published only from a complete graph in which the minimum of the window is proved; an incomplete graph or search is never a Finding and never the best proof seen so far. **Only `forcingChain`**, after a **local** cap of its attempt (`construction-work`, `construction-nodes` or `search-work`) and a passed check of the parent budget, falls back to the accepted complete singleton search, which has **no local work limit** (IR54's policy; the parent budget, cancellation and the caller's window still apply, and its work is part of the call's). There is no fallback after a spent parent budget, cancellation or an error, a completed negative (a proof below the window included) or an empty window, and `forcingChainLong` has none: a cap is its null, as before. A fallback result certifies only the **singleton-graph** minimum (a shorter proof with groups may exist), a complete grouped search the minimum of the grouped graph. **Proofs.** Contradiction and forced proofs start at a **singleton** (a true group is no permission to place an arbitrary member, so a group has no polarity proof and is never the start of one); a group starts only as a false literal of the endpoint proof, whose target set is exactly the singleton candidates weakly linked to both ends (each conflicts with every member of an end group); groups are inner nodes and ends. The order of the links of a singleton is the singleton search's own, so a proof without a group is byte for byte the legacy Finding (triples, the legacy first-appearance `cells`, no `proofVersion`); a proof with a group is `proofVersion` 2 with `cells` the ascending union of all its nodes; every array of a Finding is a copy. **Seam and record.** `SG.chainSearch.groupedForcing(model, state, ctx, { id, minLength, maxLength, groupLimit, workLimit, fallback })` (finite limits, no unbounded mode) is what the two finders call; `SG.chainSearch.forcing` and the other IDs are unchanged. The observer record of the two IDs keeps `{ id, work, limit, capHit, retained }` (`work` is now every stage: construction + search + fallback; `limit` is null for `forcingChain` and 50,000 for `forcingChainLong`, with its `capHit`) and adds `domain` (`'grouped'`, `'singleton'` or null), `grouped: { construction, search, additional, graph, minimum, stop }` (`stop`: null, `'empty'`, `'construction-work'`, `'construction-nodes'`, `'search-work'` or `'parent-budget'`) and `fallback` (null or `{ entered, work, outcome }`); it is published only on a normal return, never on cancellation or an error, and no more than 256 units pass between two checks of the parent budget across both stages. **Cost and caps (S-AV36, `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS13c/census/`).** On 710 activation-ladder positions of 12 models (labelled populations kept apart in the tables) the attempt of `forcingChain` completed at 400 and stopped at a local cap at 310 (99 construction work, 0 node quota, 211 search work); all 310 ran the fallback and 39 of them found a proof; `forcingChainLong` stopped at 313 (99 and 214). Every cap is on a large board (16 digits, Samurai, Sohei, Flower, and a few on Butterfly, 12 digits and the diagonal board); no call ended at a spent parent budget (none was set). In **pre-freeze per-call measurements** a grouped call cost about 0.6 ms on Classic and 2 to 2.3 ms on the large boards, 5 to 25 times a singleton call (fixed positions, not final-source or generation-time figures). The one candidate, a common allowance of 200,000, was measured on the same 710 positions: short and long caps **310 / 313 to 2 / 1**, Findings **213 / 115 to 323 / 254**, and **16 % more** warm finder latency (1,990 to 2,305 ms; about twice on Sohei and Samurai). Only the short ID keeps an uncapped fallback (while its parent budget permits); the long ID loses its capped deductions and has no such protection. The candidate was not adopted: the approved starting bound is retained, nothing required an adjustment merely to remove caps, and the cost of re-recording E and the quick sets is no selection criterion; its whole-generation effect is unmeasured, and no optimality, preserved long coverage or unchanged wall-budget availability is claimed. S-AV36 stays open for the final cost audit. **Generator cost (S-AV38; partial intermediate record, local single-machine observations against the accepted MS14a chain engine, not `main`; a same seed is not the same delivered puzzle).** Of the 50 cells of the performance table whose level admits the two IDs, 21 have a complete 50-run record (seed 7000, `perf.js --gate`): 15 meet their p95 target and six miss (classic9, diagonalX, wordoku, mega12 and butterfly at Nightmare, killer at Hell 2). For the five of them that were also run with the MS14a engine at 50 runs the p95 (ms, base to candidate) is classic9/nightmare 3,061 to 4,902, diagonalX/nightmare 7,693 to 16,534, wordoku/nightmare 1,624 to 3,371 and mega12/nightmare 5,485 to 8,396 (the first four already missed their target) and killer/hell2 8,806 to 14,528 (from meeting to missing); butterfly/nightmare is 24,335 against 8,000 with no paired baseline. Sixteen cells have only a complete two-run pilot (diagnostic maxima, not p95 estimates and no GP verdict; for example hyper/nightmare 658 to 19,543 and jigsaw9/nightmare 4,272 to 22,283 ms) and 13 (the Samurai, Sohei and Flower cells and mega16/hell1) have no complete pilot, so their cost is unknown. One G13 wire request (Samurai Nightmare 42) went from about 11.0 to 94.2 s (about 8.6 times; the puzzle and the attempts differ), above the 30 s qualification reference for that request and not a qualification verdict. This is not a complete or passing GP matrix, and no target, guard, offer or S-OD11 policy changed (`docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS13c/gp-table.md`). **Effect on the canonical outputs (E, fresh on this source).** Of the five wires two change (G10 keeps its puzzle and differs in 10 work counters of its generation; G13 is another puzzle), of the 14 analyses seven (G03, G04, G06, G07, G12, G13, G14) and of the 520 hashes 26 (classic9/nightmare seeds 3, 8, 21, 89, 144, 377, 1597, 4181 and 6765, and 17 seeds of diagonalX/nightmare); regenerated with the accepted base engine in one process, every one of these outputs equals the saved one, so the cause is the activation alone: a grouped step replaces another step of the registered ladder, which changes the rating of a candidate puzzle and so which candidate the digger accepts. Of the 26 labelled references none changes level, requiredness or hardest technique and six change score (escargot 5,499 to 5,543, worlds-hardest-2012 5,544 to 5,567, platinum-blonde 5,688 to 5,814, golden-nugget 5,681 to 5,701, constructed-nightmare-1 2,572 to 2,578 and constructed-nightmare-2 2,661 to 2,702). The coverage is **bounded** (the grouped graph within its allowance, the singleton graph where `forcingChain` fell back); it is not an exhaustive search for Medusa, alternating inference chains or X-cycles. The independent checker `SG.chainProofs` validates every member, edge, end and target of the Findings that the tests publish. The evidence and the rulings are in `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS13c/evidence.md`.

**The Law of Leftovers kernel (rating v3, MS14a, R17; inactive when it was written, registered by MS14b).** `SG.leftoverPatterns.find(model, state, ctx[, { workLimit }])` (`core/js/engine/tech-subset.js`, after the subset finders) returns the first Finding of `lawOfLeftovers` or null, and the frozen `SG.leftoverPatterns.LIMITS` is `{ units: 2, cells: 4, work: 50000 }`. **At MS14a it was inactive** (historical): it was not registered (no technique id, weight, locale text or help example existed; MS14b added them, see the paragraph below), no registered finder, seam, solve, rating or generation called it, and it shared no scratch with the subset finders, so it changed no Finding, observer record, hash, wire, golden or help example. GC-01, which the two paragraphs above call open, was resolved by the owner after MS13b (S-OD11, option A); its implementation is MS13c and not this task. **The idea.** In a Jigsaw, *n* complete rows (or columns) that share no cell hold every digit exactly *n* times, and so do *n* complete regions that share no cell. Take both with the same *n* (1 or 2) and set aside the cells that the two choices share: the cells each side has left over (A on the line side, B on the region side, 1 to 4 cells each, the same number on both) hold **the same multiset** of digits, with repeats and fixed (placed) digits counted. **What is computed.** For one choice of units each side is enumerated completely: a fixed cell takes its value, an open cell one of its candidates, and cells of a side that see each other take different digits. An assignment is summed up by its **signature**, the count of every digit (a set of the digits would lose the repeats), and for each signature the digits each cell takes are kept. Only when **both** enumerations are complete are the signatures intersected; an open-cell candidate that appears in no assignment of a signature both sides have is removed, and nothing else is. Ignoring the cells outside the side and the rules between the sides only adds assignments, so a removed candidate is in no completion of the supplied candidates that satisfies the full units. **Scope and order.** Jigsaw 9 only (`model.variantId === 'jigsaw9'`; another model returns null with work 0, still with a record); only complete units of kind `row`, `col` (line side) and `region` (region side) take part, the units of one side share no cell, and a choice with no open cell left over is skipped. The choices are scanned for *n* = 1, then 2, rows and then columns as the line side, the units of a side by unit index and every combination once, and for each line side every region side; the first choice that removes something gives the Finding. **An empty intersection of the signatures** (both enumerations complete, no signature in common) proves the **supplied candidate state** inconsistent (the complete tables over-approximate every completion, so it need not prove the original givens inconsistent); the whole call then returns null at that first evaluated choice, never "no candidate has support" and never a Finding of a later choice (SA2's ruling); an enumeration that was cut short proves nothing of the kind. **Work and limits.** One unit is one tested extension: a unit tried as the next of a side (only those that can still complete the side), or a digit tried for the next cell of a side, rejected or not. The limit of 50,000 counts the whole call (the counter is local to it) and a spent limit returns null with `capHit: 'work'` (an incomplete enumeration is never read as "no support"); the parent budget is checked at entry and every 256 units, a spent parent budget returns null (the caller re-checks its own budget), and cancellation and every other error propagate. The seam `{ workLimit }` replaces the limit with a finite number of at least 0 (floored; anything else is `solver.invalidOption`; there is no unbounded mode). `ctx.options.onPatternWork` gets `{ id, work, limit, capHit, retained: null }` of every call that **returns** and nothing on an error. **The limit is reached on irregular layouts of the test kit**: the blank state of the test kit's staggered layout needs 98,530 units and the wavy one 81,664, so the default limit cuts both with `capHit: 'work'` (the mixed one needs 33,784, the crossed board 6,469 and the 3 x 3 boxes 3,436); the limit was not raised. **Finding.** `technique` `lawOfLeftovers`, key `technique.lawOfLeftovers.step` (its text is MS14b's), no placements; `cells` are the distinct cells left over on both sides, ascending, fixed cells and cells with no removal included and the cancelled shared cells not; `eliminations` ascending by cell, then digit; `i18nArgs` `{ lines, regions, cells, digits }`: the labels of the chosen lines and regions (each list by unit index, never by label text), of the left-over cells (the same order) and the **distinct removed digits** (ascending; not a signature, and not removed from every listed cell, which a consumer must not imply). Every array is the caller's own, and the model, the state and the context are only read. **Tests.** The 40 new tests of `tech-subset.test.js` (named `law of leftovers kernel`, seven groups) use a new core fixture `SG.fixtures.jigsawModels` (`core/tests/fixtures/jigsaw-models.js`: the layout check, the builder, a transpose, a restarting solver and six named layouts: the 3 x 3 boxes, `staggered`, `wavy` and `mixed` for the choices of one and two units on irregular regions, `ordered` for the order in which choices are tried and `crossed`, whose only choices have two units on each side) and an independent enumeration written from the units, `unitMeta` and the cells alone (sorted digit lists as signatures); positive and negative vectors are checked against it, positions with known solutions satisfy the full units, inconsistent positions appear only as negative tests, and the work is recounted by hand and by the enumeration. **Inactivity (MS14a, historical)** was shown by the same 6,370-call differential as MS13a (SHA-256 `8c0f69aa5c7c22acb3ec85be9af1907030916f97aa9c7d9450012a7e35b042e3` for the tree and for the base `bc3450c`), by a second one of the six registered subset techniques (the Finding and the number of budget checks of every call) and the registry on 526 states (240 Jigsaw), with and without calls of the kernel interleaved between them (`bfce0d4c32d4464aa4fd893a30015c4ce4641c092a398259b1b55bf709994359` all three times), and by the complete E on the final source (five wires, 14 analyses and 520 hashes recorded and merge-checked against the unchanged canonical outputs, nothing written) and the 37 help examples. **Mutation analysis:** 92 in-memory mutants of the kernel (geometry, signatures and multisets, fixed digits, support intersection, budget and cancellation, output order and shared scratch), 91 detected and 1 equivalent: counting each digit with radix 4 instead of 5 cannot change a signature, because the digits of one unit are pairwise different, so a side of *n* ≤ 2 units holds a digit at most twice. The pack, the evidence and the rulings are in `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS14a/evidence.md`.

**The active Law of Leftovers (rating v3, MS14b, R17).** MS14b registers the kernel above as the technique `lawOfLeftovers`: group `locked`, `order` 87 (after `minMaxForcing`, before `hiddenTriple`), base cost 20, weight 200, `variantScope: ['jigsaw9']`, `assumesUniqueness: false`; the registered finder is `SG.leftoverPatterns.find` with its own default limits (a repeated load of `tech-subset.js` keeps the first definition), and the finder keeps its own `model.variantId === 'jigsaw9'` check (another board returns null with no work). A technique of 200 points adds at least 250 to a score, so on Jigsaw 9 it can first appear at Normal and is in the floor of Hard and above; no other variant lists it and the weight table of every other board is unchanged. **Limits and S-AV37.** The defaults (2 units, 4 cells, 50,000 work units) are kept. Measured on this source (`docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS14b/s-av37.json`): the real Jigsaw puzzles (the 12 requests of the discovery batch, `jigsaw9` hard seeds 1 to 12, and the two puzzles of the Player's existing help sources: 14 in all, every call of the finder along their canonical solves, 38 calls, 5 uses of the technique in 4 puzzles) have no capped call and a largest work of 3,845 (7.7 % of the limit); 60 seeded dug puzzles of the test layouts have 30 positions with a Finding, none capped, largest work 5,842 and largest call 0.8 ms; only the **constructed blank states** reach the limit (the staggered layout and the wavy one, 2.4 and 2.2 ms; the mixed layout takes 33,784, the crossed one 6,469 and the 3 x 3 boxes 3,436). Every call costs at least 3,436 units when no choice qualifies, because every row/region pair of a layout is scanned; that is a property of the scan, not of a layout. The limit was therefore **not adjusted**; no whole-generation cost of the technique is claimed beyond the pattern-cost record (7 fixture cases, 20 runs each, none capped: Findings cost 278 to 1,901 units and the three negatives 3,436 to 5,401, p50 0.07 to 0.9 ms). **Effect on the canonical outputs (E, fresh on this source; `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS14b/`).** The five wires and 13 of the 14 analyses are unchanged (G07, the other Jigsaw analysis, included); **G06** (Jigsaw Nightmare 42) changes: the puzzle goes from 24 to 22 clues and its score from 2,369 to 2,374 (one use of the technique), with 9 compact rows (8 before) and 33 detail rows (34). Of the 520 hashes **22** change: Jigsaw 9 Normal seeds 1, 5, 8, 21, 55, 89, 144, 233, 377, 610, 987, 1597, 4181 and 10946 and Jigsaw 9 Master seeds 2, 34, 144, 233, 610, 1597, 4181 and 10946; the other 24 cells and the other 18 Jigsaw keys are unchanged (five of those 18 puzzles use the technique, and the digger's choices there are the same). Regenerated with the base engine (`7eddb15`, which has no registered Law of Leftovers) in one process, all 40 Jigsaw hashes and G06 and G07 equal the saved fixtures, and regenerated with this tree they equal the merged ledgers, with the same region map for every key: the cause is the registration alone. Each of the 22 new puzzles uses the technique (1 to 3 times) in its canonical rating and is rated differently by the base engine (the 14 Normal puzzles are above Normal there, 12 Hard, one Expert and one Nightmare; four of the eight Master puzzles are Nightmare and four keep the level with another score); a registered step replaces another step of the Jigsaw ladder, which changes the rating of a candidate puzzle and so which candidate the digger accepts. Of the 26 labelled references (all Classic 9 x 9, where the technique does not exist) none changes rating, level, requiredness or hardest technique. The pins that followed from recorded outputs: the rated score of G06 (2,369 to 2,374) and its details jump (position 29, R4C6, 138 to 1,293 with the other single at 138, to position 27, R2C4, 109 to 538, the other single at 112) in `replay.test.js`; the Generator help data (`H.ladder()`) now lists the technique under *Techniques for specific variants* in its group `locked`, for Jigsaw Sudoku (`help.test.js`: the variant groups `cage`, `ineq` and `locked`; the page itself was not rendered). **Cost adapter.** `tests/fixtures/pattern-cost.js` accepts a scoped technique only when its scope is exactly one board that `SCOPED_FIXTURES` knows (`jigsaw9` to `SG.fixtures.jigsawTechniques`, `core/tests/fixtures/jigsaw-techniques.js`) and the fixture file belongs to that board; a record of such a case is built on its own region map (`model`, `cellRegion` and the givens), a classic case is unchanged, and the identity of a record (`fixtureHash`, `toolHash`) now covers the scoped fixtures, the Jigsaw builder and the helper. **Tests and mutation analysis.** `tech-subset` 140 to 167 tests (registration, scope, order, the real row/column positives on their own region maps, the default limit and a seeded population of 60 dug puzzles), `techniques` 267 to 268 (seven Jigsaw supplements join the scoped soundness sweep with at least one firing), `technique-weights` 15 to 16, `pattern-cost-tools` 32 to 44, `i18n` +1 and `ui-contract` +1 (every fixture file is listed in `tests.html`), so the Generator quick set is 4,526 tests. 86 in-memory mutants (registration 17, scope 6, weight and order 7, the cost adapter 13, record identity 3, help integration 40): three survived the first run (a registration guard left out of the filtered run, a second board accepted by the adapter and two cells of the saved example's map swapped) and were killed by new tests, so all 86 are detected and none is equivalent. The example of the guide is in `player/design.md`; the pack, the evidence and the rulings are in `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS14b/evidence.md`.

**The two Killer cage interactions (rating v3, MS14c, R17; tests only, no technique added).** The ordinary required digit locked to a unit and the two-cell Killer pair are both the pointing step of `cageCombo` (`pointOutOfCage` in `core/js/engine/tech-cage.js`), so there is no `cageLocked` technique: the ladder above, the ids, orders, weights, scopes, locale texts, help examples (38), ratings and every canonical output are unchanged, and no engine file was edited. **Rule.** For one cage `cageCombo` keeps the *surviving combinations*: the digit sets of `SG.comboTable` with the size and the sum left after the placed cells of the cage, without a digit already placed in it, inside the union of the candidates of its open cells, and with every open cell holding at least one digit of the set (**no assignment matching**: the filter does not ask whether the sets can be dealt out to the cells; that stronger test is not implemented and was not approved). A digit that every surviving set contains is *needed*. With one open cell that can hold it, it is a placement. With two or more, the cage must put it in one of them, so it is removed from every unsolved cell **outside the cage** that can hold it and lies in **every** unit of the model that contains all those places (a row and a box can both apply; an eliminated cell is listed once). A two-cell cage whose surviving combination is one pair needs both digits in both cells, so both leave the other cells of the units that the two cells share: the two-cell Killer pair is the same rule, not a second one. The Finding is the ordinary one of `cageCombo`: `cells` the whole cage, `i18nArgs { sum, size, cells, digits (of the surviving sets), forced (needed by every set) }`. **Never a target:** a cell of the cage, a solved cell, a cell without the candidate, or a cell outside the units common to all the places of the digit; and nothing is locked when a surviving set lacks the digit, or when one place that can still hold it lies outside the units of the others. **Tests (MS14c).** `core/tests/suites/tech-cage.test.js` (33 tests, run by both apps) builds its boards with `SG.model.create` from the 27 row, column and box units and one explicit cage, never from a Generator variant (the cage propagation is switched on for the variant id `tech-cage-core`), and checks 12 positions and 4 near misses, each as given and transposed, against the eliminations written out by hand, a brute-force oracle built from `model.units` and the digit subsets (never from the combination table or the finder's tables), and the exact solver (no solution has an eliminated digit in its cell), with the true solution kept. **Mutation analysis:** 36 in-memory mutants of the pointing, the needed digits, the surviving-combination filter, the Finding fields and the registration; 35 detected and 1 equivalent: the guard `value[x] === 0` of the pointing cannot matter, because a solved cell holds its own digit alone and every state is peer-consistent (`assign` removes a placed digit from all its peers), so a solved cell is never both in a unit common to the places of a digit and a holder of that digit. The evidence is in `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS14c/evidence.md`.

**The bounded almost-locked-set enumerator (rating v3, MS15a, R18; inactive until MS15b).** `core/js/engine/tech-als.js` (a new module, listed after `tech-chain.js` in all eight source lists) defines `SG.alsPatterns`: `enumerate(model, state, ctx, work[, limits])`, `createWork([limit])`, `spend(work, ctx)` and the frozen `LIMITS` `{ cells: 4, records: 4096, work: 50000 }`. **It was inactive when it was written** (MS15b registers `alsXz` on it, in the next paragraph; this paragraph describes the enumerator and what MS15a showed): no technique id (`alsXz` and `sueDeCoq` belonged to MS15b and MS16), weight, locale text or help example existed, no registered finder, seam, solve, rating or generation called it, and it shared no state with any finder, so no Finding, observer record, hash, wire, golden or help example changed (the catalogue stayed at 38 examples). **What it lists.** An almost-locked set (ALS) is *k* open cells (1 to 4) of one all-different unit whose candidates are exactly *k* + 1 digits. The *k* cells hold *k* different digits among those *k* + 1, so exactly one of the digits is missing from the set; the unit does not have to be complete, so a partial unit (a Killer cage), a diagonal, a window, a Jigsaw region and the units of any grid of a multi-grid board all count, and a bivalue cell is a 1-cell ALS. A solved cell is never part of a set; the candidates are read as they are (no propagation, no consistency check: an open cell without candidates simply adds no digit, so a position with an empty open cell is a structural input of the enumeration, not an example of a valid puzzle). Unions use masks of digits only, never a mask of board cells. **Records.** Each is plain data `{ cells, digits, occurrences }`: the ascending cell indexes, the ascending 1-based digits (*k* + 1) and, for each digit, the ascending cells of the set that still hold it. A record is complete and valid on its own, one per distinct cell set (a set that two units share is listed once). The order is by cell count, then lexicographically by the cells, and the records kept are always a **prefix** of that order (at most 4,096). **The result** `{ records, complete, capHit }`: `complete` is true when every ALS of at most `LIMITS.cells` cells (the effective cell bound, default 4) was listed: larger ALSs may exist and are never claimed absent, and `complete` does not cover the later pair or target searches of a finder. Otherwise `capHit` says why it stopped and **no case asserts that another ALS does not exist**: `'records'` (4,096 kept and a further set exists; exactly 4,096 are allowed: the limit refuses the next distinct set; the records are the first 4,096 and may support a constructive witness while the guard has work left, never an "absent" claim), `'work'` (the guard refused a unit: the records are those of the sizes finished before the cut, possibly none, because part of a size is not a prefix; the guard has no unit left, so an enclosing finder has no allowance for a pair scan it did not count) and `null` with `complete` false (the parent budget was spent: answered as "nothing found", the caller re-checks its own budget). Cancellation and every other error propagate. **The shared work guard.** `work = { limit, used, checkedAt, capped }` from `createWork` (starting limit 50,000, a whole number of at least 0 replaces it; no unbounded guard) is the one counter of an enclosing finder's call: the enumeration and the later pair scan of MS15b and MS16 spend from it with `spend(work, ctx)` (true: one unit charged; false: the limit is spent, `capped` is set, nothing is charged), and nothing resets it at a change of phase (`enumerate` never lowers `used` or `limit` and never clears `capped`). `enumerate` needs the guard (a missing or invalid one is `solver.invalidOption`, so a lost shared guard is never replaced by a fresh one). The parent budget is checked at entry and before the unit that would carry the gap since the last check over 256 units. The enumerator publishes **no** `onPatternWork` record: the enclosing finder is the one call of the solver and publishes one record for the whole of it. **Work.** One unit is one tested extension: a cell of a unit tried as the next member of a combination of its open cells (ascending), whether the bound "at most *k* + 1 digits in the union" then rejects it or not; sizes 1 to 4 are searched one after the other (so that a cut leaves a prefix), unit by unit in index order, and a set that two units share is tested in both; a size with *k* + 1 more than the digits of the board has no possible set and is not searched. The probe that tells whether a record beyond the limit exists stops at the first one. The seam `limits = { cells, records }` of the tests lowers either limit (a whole number of at least 0, floored; anything else is `solver.invalidOption`); nothing in the solver passes it. **Ownership.** Every call allocates its own tables, the records are the caller's own arrays, the model, the state and the context are only read, and nothing is cached (so no stale list can survive a change of the same state object). **Limits.** 4 cells, 4,096 records and 50,000 units are the starting values of R10; MS15b owns the once-only measured adjustment of its own new limit, and no earlier finder's unused allowance transfers. A blank 9 x 9 board costs 810 units and a 16 x 16 board in which every cell is {1, 2} 154,032 (the default limit cuts it inside the fourth size, keeping the 256 bivalue cells); seeded 16 x 16 positions with two candidates per cell reach the 4,096-record limit inside the third size, within the work limit. **Tests (MS15a).** `core/tests/suites/tech-als.test.js` (58 tests, run by both apps; it is in `core-shared`): the vectors (two cells {1,2}/{2,3} form a 2-cell set, a union of 2 or 4 digits does not, 3 cells with 4 digits and 4 cells with 5 do, 5 cells with 6 are outside the limit, cells that share no single unit are no set, solved cells, duplicated geometry, a partial cage, diagonal, window and Jigsaw units), the canonical order and its independence of the order of the units and of the cells of a unit, an independent enumeration of the records (all subsets of the open cells of each unit and the union of their digits, written from `model.units`, the state and sorted digit lists only, with no mask, no pruning and no search order of the engine) and, apart from it, a recount of the work and probe units that re-implements the engine's walk (the same order of cells, union bound and size bound) and so pins the stated count rather than discovers it, on 84 seeded positions of classic 4, 6 and 9 digits, 12 and 16 digits, diagonal, windows, Killer-style cages, every named Jigsaw layout and its transpose and a Samurai, the record limit (every lowered limit is a prefix, exactly the limit is allowed, the probe, the real limit of 4,096), the guard (every work limit from 0 to the total gives the prefix of the finished sizes, the cadence with the exact positions of the 256-unit checks, a guard that arrives partly spent, a spent budget, cancellation and other errors, a thrown null, a plain sentinel), and ownership (arrays of its own, read-only inputs under the debug checks, no cache, no interference with the registered finders (all but the two Nishio ones) on 10 seeded positions). **Mutation analysis:** 131 in-memory mutants of the module (cardinality, union bound, search, order, records, limits, the probe, the guard and its cadence, option checks, which units, ownership); 130 detected and 1 equivalent: the mutant that ignores the digits of the board in the size bound was first detected only by non-termination of the 10^12-cell call of a test and is now detected finitely (nine allowed cells against eight on a 9-digit board), and the equivalent one (remembering the records of a call in a list that nothing reads cannot change any result, so it can only change memory) is the survivor. **Inactivity** was shown against the base commit `8bd2487` by the 6,370-call chain differential (SHA-256 `bc0d73d2e75497541343fc02456c3ea3278a76830d6f10175522ba2635862c2a` for the tree and for the base, 135 complete ratings), by a second differential of all registered finders on 242 states (registry `25cc089a...`, weights `fd35856d...`, finder digest `c93fdd7a...`, equal for tree and base, and equal again with 15,064 enumerator calls interleaved; the keys of `SG` differ only by `alsPatterns`), and by the complete E on the final source (five wires, 14 analyses and 520 hashes recorded and merge-checked against the unchanged canonical outputs, nothing written) and the 38 help examples. The pack, the evidence and the rulings are in `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS15a/evidence.md`.

**The active ALS-XZ (rating v3, MS15b, R18).** MS15b registers `alsXz` in `core/js/engine/tech-als.js`, on the enumerator above: group `chain`, `order` 194 (after `forcingChain` 190, before `forcingChainLong` 198, checked by a test), base cost 106, weight 1,250 (1.25 x 1,250 = 1,562.5 is above Master's 1,500 and below Nightmare's 2,400, so it is a Nightmare-tier technique), unscoped to every variant, `assumesUniqueness: false`; the registered `find` takes no options. **The deduction.** Take two almost locked sets A and B of the retained records that share no cell, and a digit x that both hold and that is *restricted common*: every cell of A that holds x sees every cell of B that holds x (tested pair by pair on `model.peers`; disjointness is no shortcut). Two cells that see each other cannot both hold x, so one of the two sets holds no x, and a set of *k* cells with *k* + 1 digits that holds no x holds each of its other *k* digits exactly once. So for every other digit z that both sets hold, at least one cell of A or B holds z, and a candidate z of any other open cell that sees **every** cell of A and B that holds z is removed. The sets may lie in different units, grids or partial units (a Killer cage): the proof needs only that each set lies in one all-different unit and that the sets are disjoint. **Order.** The retained records in their order, A before B by record index; for a pair x ascending over its restricted-common digits, then z ascending over the other common digits (another restricted-common digit may be z); the first (A, B, x, z) that has a target gives the Finding, whatever a later one would remove. The pivot is the first cell of A that holds z (`A.occurrences[z][0]`); its peers are visited ascending, and an open cell of them that holds z and sees every cell of A and B that holds z is a target (a cell of the sets never is). The scan of one (x, z) is finished before its Finding is made. **Finding:** `technique: 'alsXz'`, `i18nKey: 'technique.alsXz.step'`, `cells` the cells of A and B ascending, `eliminations` `[target, z]` ascending by cell, no placements, `i18nArgs { digitX, digitY, cells }` (the labels of `cells`); the locale packs of both apps carry `technique.alsXz`, `.desc` and `.step`. **Work and stops.** One guard per call, the enumeration's, never reset: one unit is charged before each pair of records tested, before each (x, z) whose target scan is opened and before **every** peer of the pivot that is visited, rejected or not (no prefilter, no free scan). The parent budget is checked at entry, every 256 units, after the enumeration and before anything is published; a unit that the guard refuses, or a stop of the parent budget (even one seen once), gives **no Finding** and the work is not continued, and after a work cut the guard is spent, so a consumer reads a cap, never "nothing found". One observer record `{ id: 'alsXz', work, limit, capHit, retained }` is published for each call that returns (none for an error or a cancellation, which propagate; an observer that throws propagates too), after the search: `capHit` is `'work'` when the guard refused a unit, otherwise `'records'` when the record quota really cut the enumeration (the pairs of the retained prefix are still searched, a Finding being a constructive proof), otherwise `null`; a stop of the parent alone is `null`, as for every registered finder, and never erases a cap reported before it. `SG.alsPatterns.findXz(model, state, ctx, { workLimit, limits })` is the seam of the tests; the registered finder passes no options, so production runs with `LIMITS`. **Limits (R10, once-only, S-AV39).** The starting values 4 cells, 4,096 records and 50,000 units are **retained**; the one allowed adjustment is not used, and MS16 inherits them (it cannot retune them because it adds a second consumer). Whole-call measurements, production limits, kept apart by population: 15 blank boards of every layout cost 324 to 4,050 units; a 9 x 9 flood of {1,2} costs 10,309 and a 12 x 12 flood 39,198 (both complete, with a Finding); the 16 x 16 flood is **cut at 50,000** (complete at 154,073 with the diagnostic lifted limit, where it finds a Finding; 100,000 would still cut it, 200,000 would not); 344 calls on 22 seeded groups (classic 4, 6 and 9 digits, 12 and 16 digits, Samurai, diagonal, hyper and the 120 states of the `techniques` sweep) reached **no work cap** and 4 record caps (12 digits 1 of 8, a sparse Samurai 3 of 4) with the same Finding under the lifted limits; 254 calls on 58 real puzzles already in the repository found 20 Findings and met **3 work caps, all in the `xWing` help example's solve** (50,000 units; the lifted completions at 50,158, 52,575 and 63,271 find no Finding either), with a latency of at most 7.9 ms per call. No deduction was lost at a cap in any measured population, so an adjustment would only have turned three negative answers into complete negatives. **Not measured (named gaps):** the cap rate on generated hell-level puzzles and the cost of a whole generation (the GP cells). **Tests (MS15b).** `core/tests/suites/tech-als.test.js` is now 111 tests (the 58 of MS15a, with the "inactive" test retargeted to "registers ALS-XZ only" and the API pin extended, and 53 new in six groups: the registered technique and its seam; the proof by hand on vectors (two cells {1,2}/{2,3} against two cells {1,4}/{3,4}, one target, overlapping sets, a missing cross-peer, a z cell the target cannot see, a Killer cage, the pivot); agreement with an independent oracle (shared unit by the definition, witnesses and Findings written from the state only) on the real fixture positions and on seeded positions of 4, 6, 9, 12 and 16 digits, diagonal, hyper, cage, Samurai and Jigsaw layouts, and the exact solver `SG.dfs.searchFrom` showing that forcing an eliminated digit leaves no completion; the one guard, the work limit and the record quota; the parent budget, the errors and the observer record; ownership). **Mutation analysis:** 73 in-memory mutants of `findXz` and its registration (registration, seam, disjointness, common digits, restricted common, digit choice and order, targets and pivot, pair order, Finding shape, work and caps, budget and observer): 72 detected and 1 equivalent (a pair with one common digit has no z, so the z loop never runs and nothing is charged); MS15a's 131 enumerator mutants were re-run on the final bytes (130 detected, the same equivalent one). The predecessor-finder specimens of `tech-chain` that `alsXz` displaces (a long-chain window test and two helpers) are run on a literal list of the 29 ids before `forcingChainLong` at the accepted MS15a commit (SA2's ruling A, 2026-11-18; their puzzles, limits, thresholds and assertions are unchanged, and the ladder lists, `idsBefore()` and the production registry are not). **Effects of the activation (E, recorded fresh on the final source, the accepted MS15a engine as comparator).** The wires G10 and G13 (Killer and Samurai Nightmare 42), seven analysis goldens (G05, G06, G07, G10, G12, G13, G14) and 24 hashes (`classic9/nightmare` 7 of 20, `diagonalX/nightmare` 17 of 20) changed, and nothing else (the other 3 wires, 7 goldens and 496 hashes are identical); each was explained by regeneration (the base engine reproduces the saved output, the tree reproduces the ledger) and by its mechanism (a candidate that the base rates above the requested level is rated inside it when ALS-XZ is available, so the digger keeps it). Of the 26 reference puzzles of `rated.js` five change score (hell-level references and one constructed Nightmare), none changes level, requiredness or hardest technique. The help catalogue is 39: the new real example is Classic 9 x 9 Hell 1 seed 644 at step 18, and `forcingChainLong` (a new source, Hell 1 seed 214), `xWing`, `remotePairs` and `xyChain` (their step moved by one) were refreshed. The suites that pin the goldens (`replay`, `steps`, `player`) were refreshed from the recorded outputs with their premises kept; one real-golden check of a placement-only first use has no specimen left (no first use of any of the 15 jumps of the 14 goldens places a digit), and a controlled case through `SG.replay.firstUse` replaces it (named item MS15b-Q6, for MS18e). The evidence and the rulings are in `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS15b/evidence.md`.

**The active Sue de Coq (rating v3, MS16, R19).** MS16 registers `sueDeCoq` in the same file, on the same enumerator: group `chain`, `order` 196 (right after `alsXz` 194, before `forcingChainLong` 198, checked by a test), base cost 112, weight 1,300 (1.25 x 1,300 = 1,625, a Nightmare-tier technique), unscoped, `assumesUniqueness: false`; the registered `find` takes no options. **Geometry.** A *line* is a unit whose `unitMeta.kind` is `row` or `col`, a *box* a unit of kind `box` or `region`; no diagonal, window or cage is either, and no completeness of the unit is asked (the proof uses all-different units only). Take a line U and a box V that share at least two open cells (S, ascending), and as I every 2-subset and every 3-subset of S (not only S itself; the other cells of S are ordinary cells of U and V). C is the union of the candidates of I; A is an almost locked set of p cells in U minus V, B one of q cells in V minus U, the digits of A and of B are subsets of C and disjoint, and |C| = |I| + p + q, so Z, the digits of C that neither A nor B holds, has |I| - 2 digits (none for a 2-cell I, one for a 3-cell I). **The deduction.** The cells of A and I lie in U and A holds p of its p + 1 digits, so I holds none of those p; likewise B in V; I holds |I| different digits, exactly the |C| - p - q = |I| left: Z, the digit of A that A misses and the digit of B that B misses. So every digit of A is held by a cell of A or I in U, every digit of B by a cell of B or I in V, every digit of Z by a cell of I (in U and in V): a candidate of a digit of A leaves the other cells of U (outside I and A), one of B the other cells of V, one of Z both (outside I, A and B); nothing is removed from a cell of I, A or B, from a solved cell, or outside U and V. A record has no unit of its own for this test (it was found in some unit, which says nothing about this pair), so it is classified by its cells, by `model.units` and `model.cellUnits`. **Order and Finding.** Pairs by line index then box index; in a pair I of 2 cells before 3, the tuples ascending lexicographically; then A, then B, in the retained records' order; the first (I, A, B) whose finished target scan finds a target gives the Finding (the canonical first witness, all its targets, the largest set is not chosen). `technique: 'sueDeCoq'`, `cells` I, A and B ascending, `eliminations` `[cell, digit]` ascending by cell then digit, no placements, `i18nArgs { digits, partDigits, forced, line, box }` (the digits of A, of B, of Z, the labels of U and V), `i18nKey` `technique.sueDeCoq.step` when Z is empty and `technique.sueDeCoq.step.extra` otherwise (both locale packs of both apps carry `technique.sueDeCoq`, `.desc`, `.step` and `.step.extra`). **Work and stops.** One guard of its own per call, `SDC_WORK_LIMIT` (50,000), not the spent guard of an earlier call and not `LIMITS.work`; one unit is charged before each of nine operations (a box cell visited in the geometry, solved cells included; an extension of the enumeration, the same guard; a record classified; a (line, box) pair opened; a record of a home list tested for lying outside the other unit; an I opened; a record of a side tested for its digits inside C; an (A, B) tested; a cell of U and V visited for the targets), and nothing else is charged. The parent budget is checked at entry, every 256 units, at the end of the geometry, of the enumeration and of the classification, and before publishing; with no pair of two open shared cells the call ends in the geometry (no enumeration, `retained` 0, no cap). A refused unit gives no Finding (a partial target scan is dropped whole) and a stop of the parent budget gives none even if seen once; one observer record `{ id: 'sueDeCoq', work, limit, capHit, retained }` is published for each call that returns, outside the error handling, with the same `capHit` rule as ALS-XZ. **Seam.** `SG.alsPatterns.findSueDeCoq(model, state, ctx, { workLimit, limits })` is the seam of the tests; an absent `workLimit` is the private constant, `opts` must be an object (the shared seam also refuses an array now, for ALS-XZ too: `solver.invalidOption`), and an explicit `limits` is validated even when the geometry ends the call before the enumeration. **Limits (R10, once-only, S-AV39).** The own allowance 50,000 is **retained**; the one delegated adjustment is not used (the shared 4 cells, 4,096 records and 50,000 units stay as they are). Whole-call measurements, production limits, kept apart: 15 blank boards cost 390 to 5,277 units; a 9 x 9 flood of {1,2} costs 22,059 (no cap, no Finding), 12 x 12 and 16 x 16 floods are **cut at 50,000** (complete at 89,916 and 375,088 with the lifted diagnostic limit, and with no Finding either); 488 calls on 40 seeded groups found 115 Findings and met **2 work caps** (one lost a Finding: a sparse 16-digit call that needs 101,791 units, which 100,000 does not recover and 200,000 does) and 3 record caps with the same Findings as the lifted limits, at most 5.986 ms a call; on 134 real puzzles already in the repository 23 reach Sue de Coq (301 calls) with **no Finding and no cap** (at most 3,326 units, 0.69 ms). **Not measured (named gaps):** the cap rate on generated hell-level positions of the big boards and the cost of a whole generation (the GP cells, MS18c to MS18e); S-AV39 stays open. **Tests (MS16).** `core/tests/suites/tech-als.test.js` is now 174 tests (the 111 of MS15a and MS15b, with the registration, scope and seam pins extended, and 63 new in six groups: the registered technique and the seam; the proof by hand on vectors in rows and columns, both forms of Z, a proper subset of the intersection, the near misses and the wrong roles of a unit (window, diagonal, cage); agreement with an independent oracle written from `model.units` and the state on 482 seeded and real positions of every kind of board with the exact solver; the guard, the nine charge points, the caps and the record quota; the parent budget with every check, the errors and the observer record; ownership, immutability and no cache). **Mutation analysis:** 139 in-memory mutants of `findSueDeCoq`, its registration, its weight and its texts (131 detected on the first run; the 8 survivors were 6 equivalent mutants, each with a non-circular argument, and 2 killed by two tests added afterwards), MS15b's 71 ALS-XZ mutants re-run on the final bytes (70 detected, 1 equivalent) and MS15a's 131 enumerator mutants (130 detected, 1 equivalent). **Effects of the activation (E, recorded fresh on the final source, the accepted MS15b engine as comparator).** No wire and no analysis golden changed (G01 to G14 match), and **one hash of 520 changed, `classic9/nightmare` seed 377**, explained by regeneration (the base engine reproduces the saved hash, the tree reproduces the ledger; the tree's puzzle has 33 clues and uses `sueDeCoq` once, the base rates it hell1/2576 and so rejects that candidate); the 26 reference puzzles of `rated.js` are unchanged, none uses `sueDeCoq`. Sue de Coq is rare: in the test census of the suite every position where it fires also has an ALS-XZ deduction, and it is reached in the ladder only where ALS-XZ has nothing; the one real generated puzzle found for it, `classic9/nightmare#377`, is such a case (its canonical rating uses it once). The help catalogue is 40: the new real example is Classic 9 x 9 Nightmare seed 377 (the one changed regression key) at step 19, and none of the 39 earlier examples moved. The evidence and the rulings are in `docs/difficulty-v3/evidence/MS16/evidence.md`.

> The scores of the first plan were **starting values**; the calibration of task `T-2.10` replaced them with calibrated costs (`SG.difficulty.COSTS`, rating v1), and rating v2 replaced those with the weights above ([docs/difficulty-rating/report-detailed.md](../docs/difficulty-rating/report-detailed.md) §5.2: singles stay close together so that their order does not matter, cage combinations and inequality chains start at the locked-candidate level, the rule of 45 and cage splitting sit among strong subsets and fish). The weights are engineering priors, not measurements of human solvers. Task `T-6.3` of the first plan referred to pre-generation, not calibration.

#### Difficulty scoring

Rating v2 (the joint report [docs/difficulty-rating/report-detailed.md](../docs/difficulty-rating/report-detailed.md) §5 is the specification):

```
precondition  a complete logical proof (rating.solved); otherwise the puzzle is unrated: no score, no level
E     = empty cells at the start                     (rating.empties)
n_t   = uses of technique t                          (rating.techniqueCounts)
w_t   = SG.difficulty.WEIGHTS[t]                     a technique without a weight is an error, never a guess
P     = max { w_t : n_t > 0 }                        the peak: the hardest technique, counted once in full
W     = Σ w_t · n_t                                  the work
V     = 80 · E / (E + 40)                            empty cells: bounded by 80, 40 at E = 40
R     = 0.25 · P · log₂(1 + W / P)                   the rest of the work, on a logarithmic scale (0 when P = 0)
score = Math.round(V + P + R)                        one rounding of the unrounded sum
level = the level whose band [min, max) contains score
```

The only inputs are `E` and the technique counts: not the requested or achieved level, the variant, the board size, the time or the symmetry, so the same `E` and counts give the same score in every variant. The score never decreases when a count, `E` or the weight of the hardest technique grows, and it does not depend on the order of the steps; it is **not** monotone under clue removal (the proof changes). The digger does treat a removal it refused (stuck or too hard) as refused for the rest of that dig: a measured search heuristic, not a property of the score (§6.8). One expensive step counts in full on any board (through `P`), while a long proof of cheap steps grows only logarithmically, so a singles-only proof stays below about 77 on every board up to 369 cells. There is no technique floor any more: the level is derived from the score, never the other way round. Because `R ≥ 0.25·P` whenever a technique is used, every puzzle that uses technique `t` scores at least `1.25·w_t`; that is why the oracle of a level allows exactly the techniques with `1.25·w_t` below the band maximum (§6.7).

The logical solver is deliberately not told about the weights: it reports `techniqueCounts`, and `SG.difficulty.classify(report, model)` scores from them, so `report.score` of the solver is the design score — always use `rating.score` (`rating.rawScore` equals it and is kept for the formats). `rating.ratingVersion` is 2.

`rating.hardestTechnique` is the used technique with the largest weight — the one that provides `P` (ties: the larger registration `order`, then the id in ascending code-unit order); `rating.hardestGroup` is its group. Both are metadata only. `rating.confidence` is the margin of the score to the nearest edge of its band that borders another level. Worked example (classic9 *hard*): `P = 320` (xWing), `W = 29 + 19·1.25 + 5·1.5 + 5·40 + 120 + 320 = 700.25`, `V = 80·53/93 = 45.59`, `R = 80·log₂(1 + 700.25/320) = 133.82`, score `round(499.41) = 499`.

The Difficulty score table (§10.5) shows this sum: each technique row has its uses and its share `R·w_t·n_t/W` of the work (the row of the hardest technique also carries `P`), the Empty cells row shows `V`, and one `roundToTotal` makes whole numbers that add up to the score (`SG.difficulty.scoreTable`).

> **An extremely useful corollary:** if `logicalSolver` solves a puzzle **entirely with sound inference techniques**, that puzzle **certainly has a unique solution** — there is no need to call `dfs-solver` again. This is exploited in [§6.8](#68--optimal-hole-digging-strategy).

### 6.7 Difficulty definitions

Nine levels (rating v2). The band is the same for every variant; the oracle of a level allows exactly the techniques whose `1.25 × weight` is below the band maximum (the column lists those that become available at that level; all of them at `hell2`).

| Level | VI | EN | Score band | Techniques that become available | Clue floor `minClues` (classic9) | `minCluesPerUnit` |
|-------|----|----|-----------|----------------------------------|---------------------------------:|------------------:|
| `veryEasy` | Rất dễ | Very easy | [0, 45) | singles | 40 | 2 |
| `easy` | Dễ | Easy | [45, 180) | pointing, box/line, naked pair; cage combination, inequality chain | 34 | 2 |
| `normal` | Trung bình | Normal | [180, 400) | hidden pair, triples, quads; rule of 45, min/max forcing | 29 | 1 |
| `hard` | Khó | Hard | [400, 700) | X-Wing, Skyscraper, Two-String Kite, Remote Pairs, simple colouring, Empty Rectangle, Finned X-Wing, Swordfish, Finned Swordfish, Jellyfish; cage splitting | 25 | 1 |
| `expert` | Chuyên gia | Expert | [700, 1000) | XY-Wing, XYZ-Wing | 25 | 1 |
| `master` | Bậc thầy | Master | [1000, 1500) | WXYZ-Wing, W-Wing, Unique Rectangle, forcing chain (up to 4 links) | 22 | 0 |
| `nightmare` | Ác mộng | Nightmare | [1500, 2400) | long forcing chain (5 to 12 links), Nishio (depth 1) | 17 | 0 |
| `hell1` | Địa ngục 1 | Hell 1 | [2400, 3600) | deep Nishio (depth 2) | 17 | 0 |
| `hell2` | Địa ngục 2 | Hell 2 | ≥ 3600 | — | 17 | 0 |

Bands are half-open, `[min, next min)`, so every score belongs to exactly one level; the top level has no upper bound. The canonical names and descriptions of the levels (both apps use them verbatim) are in [docs/difficulty-rating/report-detailed.md](../docs/difficulty-rating/report-detailed.md) §5.3; the level descriptions say what a level *typically* uses, because the level comes from the score. `expert` takes the clue floor and unit minimum of `hard`, and the hell tiers those of `nightmare`. `maxClues` is informative only. `SG.difficulty.BASE_GROUPS` names the classic technique families in ladder order; it no longer defines the levels. No further tiers in this release: no generated puzzle reached 5,400 (report §5.3).

**Floor and tier.** Which techniques a level can use follows one rule, `allowed(i)`: the registered, weighted techniques that apply to the variant (all of them when there is no model) with `1.25 × weight` below the band maximum of level `i` (every one at `hell2`), in registry order. The **floor** of a level is `allowed` of the **previous global level** — empty for `veryEasy` — even when the variant does not offer that level; its **tier** is `allowed` of the level without its floor. The table above lists the tiers; `spec` returns both sets next to `allowedTechniques` (`floorTechniques`, `tierTechniques`) and `SG.difficulty.floorTechniques` / `tierTechniques` return them for any valid level. Nothing is cached: the sets are read from the registry and `WEIGHTS` on every call, so a changed weight or registration shows at once. A puzzle *needs* the tier of its level when the floor techniques alone do not solve it; a score inside the band does not prove that. `veryEasy` (its tier is the three singles) and a level whose tier is empty (`hell2` with today's weights; `hell1` was one until `nishioDeep` joined it in MS07b) are **vacuous**: the certificate is `{ required: true, vacuous: true, floorLevel: null }` and takes no solve. `easy` is not vacuous: a puzzle of singles only that scores 46 is a shallow Easy. `SG.difficulty.certifyFloor` ([§13.7](#137-sgdifficulty)) is the independent reference check, and `SG.difficulty.requiredOf` derives the same verdict from the technique counts of a complete canonical rating, without a second solve: because the floor is a prefix of the ladder of the variant (§6.6), the canonical solve takes the same steps as the floor solve until it first needs a technique outside the floor, so a rating that used any such technique is exactly the one that needed the tier. The two agree on every constructed and published puzzle and on small Classic, Killer, Jigsaw and Greater-Than samples (the tests of `difficulty-floor.test.js`). Generation accepts a sample by this verdict: it is exact only with a complete canonical proof in the band **and** `required` (§6.8); the soak, the qualification and the manual suites count a hit by that `exact` flag alone and never by comparing level names (§15.3, §15.4).

**Names of the levels.** The levels were renamed after rating v2 had been built, for names that are easier to understand: `medium` → `normal`, `veryHard` → `expert`, `expert` → `master`, `evil` → `nightmare`, `nightmare1` → `hell1`, `nightmare2` → `hell2` (VI: `normal` keeps Trung bình, `expert` is Chuyên gia, `master` Bậc thầy, `nightmare` Ác mộng, `hell1` / `hell2` Địa ngục 1 / 2). Only the names changed (ids in URLs, exports and storage, locale keys, code, documents); the bands, the weights and the generation did not. The documents of the two versions (`docs/qa/final-review-v1.md`, `docs/difficulty-rating/`, `task.md`) keep the names of their time; the mapping above reads them. A saved link, history entry or export that carries an old id is not translated: `d=expert` now means the level once called `veryHard`.

**Variant scaling rule.** `SG.difficulty.spec(level, model)` starts from the classic9 row and multiplies the clue floor by `cellCount / 81`; the score band is never scaled (rating v2). A variant whose board does not follow that proportion then adjusts the floor in its definition — this is the calibration knob, and it replaced the first plan's single scaling rule:

1. `clueFloorScale: { level: factor }` multiplies the derived floor of that level (a missing level has factor 1). Rating v2 gives `expert` the factor of `hard` and `hell1` / `hell2` that of `nightmare` wherever a variant declares them.
2. `clueFloor` — a table `{ level: clues }` (a missing level: no floor) or a function `(level, model, derivedFloor) => clues` — replaces it.

The result, `spec(level, model).minClues`, is the **single number** that the digger, the pipeline, `tests/soak.js` and the variants sweep (invariant **I6**) all use. The calibration of task `T-7.7` (rating v1) measured, per variant, how often ONE dug puzzle lands on the requested level and set:

| Variant | `clueFloorScale` | Effect on the share of one dug puzzle landing on the level (normal / hard / master) |
|---------|------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| `diagonalX`, `hyper`, `jigsaw9`, `greaterThan` | normal 0.83 · hard 0.80 · master 0.80 | normal 1–49 % → 32–76 % (`hyper` 1 → 32, `jigsaw9` 8 → 50) |
| `samurai`, `sohei`, `butterfly` | normal 0.60 · hard 0.60 | normal 2–8 % → 38–49 % |
| `flower` | easy 0.80 · normal 0.60 · hard 0.60 | normal 9 → 49 %; `normal` is now offered |
| `killer` | `clueFloor: { veryEasy: 40 }` | the other levels dig down to a handful of clues: classic Killer shows no digits and the cages carry the information |

(A 9×9 board keeps its floor above the natural stop of the dig on purpose, so that the mean clue count still falls with the level.) Classic, Mega and Wordoku are untouched. Exact-level hit rate over 150 puzzles per level before → after: butterfly normal 95 → 100 %, hyper normal 89 → 100 %, jigsaw9 normal 99 → 100 %; dug puzzles per normal puzzle: hyper 38 → 3.2, butterfly 31 → 2.1. The numbers and the reasoning are in the header of `js/core/difficulty.js`.

**Known minimum clue count:** `classic9` = 17 (proven). Other variants have no tight bound known ⇒ it must not be hard-coded; let the digger stop on its own when no further cell can be dug. `DifficultySpec.extraPasses` is kept for the contract of §13.7 but is not used: the guided stage runs until nothing can be removed, so there is no pass to add.

### 6.8 ⭐ Optimal hole-digging strategy

The user's requirement describes a naive loop: *"remove a cell → check it is still solvable → repeat"*. Below is the **optimal approach** and the reasons for it.

#### Problems with the naive approach

1. Picking random cells with repetition ⇒ the same cell that was already rejected is tried again and again.
2. Checking for a unique solution by counting all the way to 2 solutions ⇒ very expensive (§6.5).
3. It only checks *"solvable"*, not *"exactly as hard as intended"* ⇒ difficulty lands at random, and the whole puzzle often has to be thrown away and redone.
4. It scores difficulty after **every** dig ⇒ the logical solver, the most expensive component, is called the most often.

#### The optimal approach — 5 improvements

**① Pre-shuffled digging sequence**
Shuffle the cell list **once** and walk through it sequentially ⇒ each cell is considered exactly once, with no waste. Several sequence styles are supported, affecting both speed and the "feel" of the puzzle:
`random` (default) · `rowByRow` · `snake` (S-shaped) · `spiral` · `jumpOne`.

**② Symmetry handled in groups**
When symmetry is enabled, mirrored cells are **dug/restored together as one unit**, not cell by cell. Styles (`SG.symmetry.KINDS`): `none`, `rotational180` (default; the most attractive and common), `rotational90`, `mirrorH`, `mirrorV`, `mirrorBoth`, `diagonal`, `antiDiagonal`. Groups are computed on **canvas** coordinates, so a multi-grid board mirrors about the centre of the whole canvas, and a group simply shrinks where the mirrored position holds no cell.
*Trade-off:* the stricter the symmetry, the higher the minimum clue count ⇒ the harder it is to reach the top levels. Symmetries whose groups have more than two cells (`rotational90`, `mirrorBoth`) dig in coarser steps and miss a level more often, so the generator allows 30 dig samples per grid instead of 12 for them, and the controls warn from `master` upwards, the hell levels included (`SG.controls.STRICT_SYMMETRY`).

**③ Cheap pruning before expensive checks (cheap pre-filters)**
Before calling any solver, immediately reject candidates that violate:
- The remaining clue count would drop below the level's `minClues` (the variant's own floor, §6.7).
- That row/column/region would be left with fewer than `minCluesPerUnit` clues (2 / 2 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 0 / 0 / 0 / 0 for the nine levels on a 9-digit board, scaled by `digits / 9`; Killer uses 0) — this helps the puzzle "look balanced".
- The cell was already rejected in a previous pass (the `rejected` bitmap; Killer also puts there the clues its layout search rated inside the band, §8.2). Only a lost uniqueness is final in principle: a removal refused because the puzzle got stuck or too hard can become acceptable once another removal changes the proof. Treating every refusal as final for the rest of the dig is a measured search heuristic (rating v2 qualification: re-checking those refusals after every accepted removal made generation 4.6 times slower and hid seven top levels for time, with the same share of exact hits).

**④ ⭐ The technique-bounded oracle** — use a logical solver *limited to the allowed techniques* as the oracle
Instead of asking *"does the puzzle still have a unique solution?"* and only afterwards *"how hard is it?"*, we ask **one single question**:

> *"After digging, can the puzzle still be solved **using only the technique set allowed for this difficulty level**?"*

- If **yes** ⇒ accept the dig. (And because all techniques are sound, **a unique solution is guaranteed for free** — no DFS needed. The oracle runs with `assumeUniqueness: false`, so `uniqueRectangle`, the one technique that presupposes uniqueness, is never used to certify it.)
- If **no** ⇒ put the cell back.

Rating v2 adds the band's upper edge to the question: a removal is also put back when the score of the bounded solve would reach the band maximum (`maxScore`), so the easy levels stop in time on large boards, where many empty cells alone would otherwise push a singles-only puzzle past its band.

Result: the dig never leaves the chosen band from above, and every puzzle it keeps has a complete proof with the techniques of the level. Whether it reaches the band from below is decided by the full rating (⑤), which is what makes a sample *exact*: a complete canonical proof, a level equal to the requested one **and** a technique of that level's tier (`SG.difficulty.requiredOf`, §6.7; nothing is required of `veryEasy`). A complete proof in the band that needs no such technique is *shallow*: it is a candidate, but it is never exact.

*Applies to:* every level (rating v2).
*Top tiers (`nightmare`, `hell1`, `hell2`):* every other sample is also a fast **proposal**: stage A with `dfs stillUnique` (§6.5) as the oracle, stage B towards puzzles the techniques below the chain group cannot finish; each uniqueness check of a removal is guarded at 200,000 search nodes (a removal that needs more is refused). The proposal is rated in full; when it is above the band it is **refined downward**: empty symmetry groups of the solution are added back one at a time in a seeded order (at most 64 full ratings) — an addition that is completely rated with a score ≥ the band minimum is kept (and the refinement stops when the score is also below the band maximum), any other addition is undone.
*Safety net (risk R3):* whatever oracle dug it, every puzzle is re-verified with `SG.dfs.countSolutions(…, 2) === 1` before it leaves the digger. If a technique bug ever made one ambiguous, it is replaced by a plain DFS-dug puzzle and the result carries the warning `digger.unsoundTechnique`.
