A candidate that the searches, the refinement or a variant's strategy offer as the closest puzzle of the run (the keeper, §6.8) does not go through the dig step, so the keeper counts its solutions itself (within the 200,000-node guard) before it keeps it, whatever its proof claims: a candidate that is not verified unique is never kept, the closest verified one stays, and a complete proof contradicted by a second solution adds the same warning.

**⑤ Deferred & banded rating**
The full rating runs once per dug puzzle (step 6), not after every cell. Inside the guided stage (below) the oracle's own score is the objective, so the expensive rater is still called once per sample (the proposals and the local search rate their candidates in full, counted against the caps of ⑥).

#### Complete algorithm (as built)

Stage A is `SG.digger.dig`/`digStage` (the engine, usable on its own); stage B is the guided dig; `SG.digger.generate` is the restart loop; `SG.pipeline` wraps all of it in the seven steps and owns the events. The restart loop lives in the digger so that a puzzle is the same whether it is generated through the pipeline or by direct calls.

```
function generate(model, level, rng, maxAttempts = defaultMaxAttempts(model.cellCount), digTries = 12):   # digTries = 30 for coarse symmetries
    spec ← difficultySpec(level, model)                                    # §6.7, incl. the variant's floor
    for attempt in 1..maxAttempts:
        caps     ← { ratings: 1024, proposals: 32768 }                     # per attempt, counted, never timed
        solution ← generateFullGrid(model, rng.fork('grid#'+attempt))      # step 3
        model'   ← variant.postGenerate(...) or "nothing to do"            # step 4 (Killer: cages + layout search)
        for sample in 1..digTries:                                         # the same grid, a fresh dig each time
            # ── step 5: dig ─────────────────────────────────────────────
            if level is a top tier and sample is odd:                      # the fast proposal (④)
                givens ← proposalDig(...); rating ← rate(givens)
                if rating.score ≥ spec.max: refineDown(...)                # ≤ 64 ratings, symmetric groups
            else:
                givens ← copy(solution)
                order  ← symmetryGroups(sequence(allCells, order, rng), symmetry)
                # Stage A: greedy dig along the pre-shuffled sequence
                for group in order:
                    if clues − |group| < minClues or violatesUnitMinimum(group): continue
                    remove(group)
                    ok ← solvableWithin(allowedTechniques) and score < spec.max
                    if not ok: restore(group)
                    (stage B takes over at ≈ 47 % of the cells as clues)
                # Stage B (every level): the guided dig, "dig deeper", towards the sample's target score
                repeat: rate up to 32 candidate removals with the objective (the bounded solve's score,
                        refusing a removal that is stuck, ambiguous or at/above the band's upper bound);
                        take the one that suits the target best (tier first, then nearest, else the highest);
                        apply it only where the policy allows (§6.8); stop when nothing can be removed
            safety net: countSolutions(givens, 2) == 1, else DFS fallback + 'digger.unsoundTechnique'
            # ── step 6: rate ────────────────────────────────────────────
            rating ← SG.difficulty.rate(model', givens)                    # one full rating
            # ── step 7: tune ────────────────────────────────────────────
            exact ← rating.solved and rating.level == level and requiredOf(rating, level, model').required
            if exact: return puzzle                                         # tune.hit (required: true)
            remember the best puzzle: exact 0, shallow (in the band, tier not needed) 0.5, else the distance
                                                                           # tune.shallow / tune.miss → next sample
            if caps are used up: break
        if no sample was exact and caps remain:
            local search from the closest sample of the attempt            # symmetric groups: remove / swap /
                                                                           # add (Greater-Than: remove signs)
        emit retry.difficulty (attempt, willRetry)
    if no sample at all had a complete proof: throw 'digger.noRatedPuzzle'
    return the best completely rated puzzle, with the warning 'difficulty.approximate'
           (and 'difficulty.shallow' when it is in the band but needs no technique of the tier)
```

Why stage B is *guided* and not "dig further with a relaxed oracle" as the first plan sketched: a puzzle that stage A has finished is minimal (every remaining clue is needed for uniqueness itself), so a relaxed oracle finds nothing more to remove — and random minimal puzzles need nothing beyond singles about three times in four, whatever technique set the oracle allows, so the hard levels would be hit far too rarely. Choosing the removal that suits the target at each step finds the puzzles that need more (the hardest one, where the level has no upper bound and so no target). The guided stage never goes past the band; clues are given back only by the downward refinement of the top tiers' proposals and by the local search (rating v2), both logged as `reveal` entries of the dig log, so the replay shows them.

> **Pipeline seams.** `digLevel` runs the three steps `dig` ("dig holes and check each one": the dig strategy, default `digStage`, then the safety net), `rate` (one rating) and `tune` (the band check: `tune.hit` / `tune.shallow` / `tune.miss`). A missed sample repeats dig → rate → tune **on the same grid**, up to `digTries` times, before the attempt gives up and `retry.difficulty` starts a new grid. A variant selects another dig strategy with `digStrategy` (Killer's wraps `digStage` with cage-aware settings; Greater-Than's digs signs, §8.3), registered with `SG.digger.registerStrategy(id, fn)`.
>
> **Exact, shallow and the stops (rating v3).** Each candidate has a *quality*: 0 for an exact one (a complete proof in the band that needs a technique of the tier, judged by `SG.difficulty.requiredOf` on the model the candidate was rated for), 0.5 for a shallow one (a complete proof in the band that does not), and for any other completely rated one the distance of its level and score from the band, never below 1,000; an unrated or unsolved candidate has none. The keeper (the closest candidate of the run, shared with the searches and the variant strategies) keeps a candidate only when its quality is strictly better, so an equal one keeps the first met, and it asks `closer(rating, model)` with the model of the candidate: Killer's layouts and Greater-Than's thinned signs are not the model of the requested board. The first exact sample ends the attempt's sampling (sample 1 always runs); an exact keeper ends the attempt (no other sample, no proposal, no rating of the local search); a local search that starts from a shallow candidate, or from a refinement of a proposal that landed in the band without needing the tier, keeps proposing moves until a neighbour is exact or the caps of ⑥ are used up, and every unique, completely rated neighbour is offered to the keeper before the search decides whether it follows the move, so a required neighbour with a lower score is kept even though the path refuses it. A refinement that ends exact, still above the band or incomplete is returned as it is. The cost is paid by the attempts that never meet a required puzzle: a shallow result in the band is no longer an early exit, and no new cap was added.
>
> **The shallow-search allowance (difficulty v3, R5).** The full ratings of a local search are counted from before its first downward refinement, in a record of its own: every rating it invokes counts (the refinement's, and one that throws, too), a call that is refused before the rater is invoked does not. The first *shallow* state of the search starts its allowance: `firstShallowAt` is 0 when the search starts from a shallow candidate, otherwise the one-based ordinal of the local rating that first shows one, even when the path then refuses it (a refinement that becomes shallow on its third rating gives 3, not 0); that identifying rating is not allowance work, and the count starts once and never restarts after the search leaves the band. From then on the search may make `SG.digger.SHALLOW_SEARCH_RATINGS` more full ratings, a whole number ≥ 0 or `Infinity` (no allowance beyond the caps of ⑥); the default of this source is **256**, selected by the measurement of R5 (below); the next *new* rating is refused before it is made, the search ends on the state it has and the keeper hands on its best verified candidate, with no extra solve. A repeated candidate judged from the memo is a proposal, not a rating. The setting is read each time a local search starts and is one common number: it limits only the local search, not the layout, dig or sample ratings and not the number of attempts, and there is no per-variant value. An exact state or an exact keeper ends the search first and reports no cap, and a cap of ⑥ that already stopped the loop keeps its own reason. A search ends up as `{ ratings, proposals, firstShallowAt (null: never shallow), shallowRatings, shallowLimit (null: Infinity), capHit (null, 'shallow', 'ratings' or 'proposals') }` in the `work` of its attempt. **The selected default (R5, task MS05r4).** The owner's rule was fixed beforehand: compare 64, 128, 256 and no extra cap (`Infinity`) on six cells, classic9 Normal, Master and Nightmare, Killer Hard and Expert and Jigsaw 9 Expert, with 12 requests each from the seed base 20260101 (288 requests), count only the generator's own exact flag with every error a miss, and choose the smallest finite value that loses no exact hit in any cell and lowers the median of full ratings in at least one, otherwise no extra cap. 64 and 128 lose hits in classic9 Nightmare (0 and 1 of 12 against 2); 256 loses none and lowers that cell's median (1,345 against 1,417), so the default is 256. A finite default changes which puzzle returns when a local search is cut short, never its validity or its exact flag. It acts only on the local continuation: the ordinary dig samples, which are a large part of the time of the G13 and mega requests, are not limited by it. The cells, the three closure requests measured at the four settings without selecting anything, and the evidence are in [docs/difficulty-v3/shallow-cap-2026-11-13.md](../docs/difficulty-v3/shallow-cap-2026-11-13.md).

#### ⑥ Outer loop: budgeted restart

The outer loop of the algorithm above (up to `maxAttempts` grids, by default the number for the size of the board (below), UI range 1–20; up to `digTries` dig samples per grid, then the local search) is the budgeted restart. Every attempt has deterministic caps (rating v2, report §5.7 (9)): at most 1,024 full ratings (the refinement's included) and 32,768 proposed moves (rejected and repeated ones count too), and every uniqueness check of a search candidate or a proposal removal is guarded at 200,000 search nodes; the guided objective's bounded solve is guarded at 100,000 budget checks. Counting work instead of time keeps a seed's puzzle the same on every machine. When every attempt is used, the best **completely rated** puzzle is returned together with the warning `difficulty.approximate` ("best" = exact first, then a shallow one, which also adds `difficulty.shallow`, then the closest level and the distance of the score from the band; of two equal ones the first met stays); a puzzle without a complete logical proof is never returned, and when no sample at all had one the run fails with `digger.noRatedPuzzle`. The 120 s active-time guard of the client and cancellation stay errors. `SG.digger.generate` reports the counters of every attempt as `work` (`{ ratings, proposals, maxRatings, maxProposals, localSearches }` per attempt, counters only and never a time), a fresh plain copy each time; an error that ends a generation carries a copy of the work done so far as a non-enumerable `work` property of the same error object, so its identity, its code and a cancellation stay what they were, and an error raised before any generation (a bad option of the pipeline) has none, which is unknown and not zero.

**The default number of attempts follows the board (difficulty v3, CV6-03 (c2); task MS05r5p).** One function, `SG.digger.defaultMaxAttempts(cellCount)`, gives 8 for 81 cells or fewer (classic4/6/9, diagonalX, hyper, jigsaw9, Killer, Greater-Than, Wordoku), 4 for 82 to 144 (mega12, butterfly) and 2 for more (flower 189, mega16 256, sohei 288, samurai 369). `SG.digger.generate` calls it with `model.cellCount`; `SG.pipeline.normalizeConfig` with `SG.variants.cellCountOf(def)`, the size read from the definition without building a model, drawing a random number or looking at a seed (digits squared, unless the definition declares its `cellCount`, as the four multi-grids do); the store takes it for the selected variant (§10.4, §14.3), so the three never hold the rule twice. A caller's `maxAttempts` always wins, whatever the board: a whole number from 1, and the control keeps 1–20. `SG.pipeline.DEFAULT_MAX_ATTEMPTS` (8) is only the reference of the 81-cell boards, never the default of another board. Why: since requiredness a request that meets no required puzzle runs every attempt, and on a large board one attempt costs seconds to a minute, so attempts beyond the first few cannot make a request fit its budget, they only turn a miss into the application's 120 s error; with the size default the request ends with the fallback and its warnings. It is a count and not a time, so a seed gives the same puzzle on every machine; it does not touch the shallow allowance (256), the caps of an attempt or the samples inside an attempt, and no board of up to 81 cells changes. A fallback is a miss for the final qualification, which alone decides what is offered ([qualification](../docs/difficulty-rating/qualification.md)); no level is hidden before it, and the browser's 120 s guard stays an error. `tests/soak.js` and `tests/qualify.js` never pass `maxAttempts`, so they measure these defaults.

**The target policy (difficulty v3, R6/R7; tasks MS06b, MS06c1 and MS06c2): active.** The policy gives each sample one target score inside the raw band of its level (R6), carried through every stage that compares candidates (R7). The rules are pure helpers in `SG.digger.targetPolicy` (an internal seam for the tests and the activation, like `removalObjective`, `createKeeper`, `keeperView` and `keptPuzzle`, see §13.13), fixed by `tests/suites/digger-target.test.js`, and generation calls them; a seed gives a different puzzle than before the activation, by design, so the 520 regression hashes were refreshed with it (§15.3). They rate nothing and never decide whether a candidate is required: the caller computes exactness on each candidate's own model. `targetFor(spec, rng)` is `null` when `spec.max` is `Infinity` (the top level keeps its unbounded digging) and otherwise `spec.min + rng.fork('target').next() × (spec.max − spec.min)`, a point of the raw band with no rounding or truncation; only the child stream `target` is drawn, so the random and guided parent streams do not move. A guided pool compares objectives of three kinds, `{ kind: 'refused', reason }`, `{ kind: 'bounded', score, tierUsed }` and `{ kind: 'proposal', priority }`; `preferObjective(candidate, incumbent, target)` is strict and defined only for two successful objectives of the same kind (a refusal never wins, a proposal is never read as a score, and the first successful candidate of a pool is its incumbent). Within a bounded pool it prefers `tierUsed`, then the score nearer a finite target, or the higher score when the target is `null`; a proposal maximises `priority` whatever the target; an exact tie keeps the first, seeded candidate. `applyBounded(current, winner, target)` decides whether the winner of a bounded pool replaces the current state:

| Current `tierUsed` | Winner `tierUsed` | Applied? |
|---|---|---|
| true | false | never |
| false | true | yes |
| false | false | yes: the search for the tier goes on |
| true | true, finite target | only if the distance to the target strictly shrinks |
| true | true, `null` target | yes: the unbounded digging is kept |

`preferRated(candidateQuality, candidateScore, currentQuality, currentScore, target)` is the local search's choice between two completely rated candidates: the lower R3 quality wins (0 exact, 0.5 shallow, then the distance of the level; `Infinity` unrated), an equal quality prefers the nearer finite target or, with none, the higher score, and an exact tie is false; every comparison is explicit, never `Infinity − Infinity`. `acceptReveal(current, next, spec, target, currentIsExact, nextIsExact)` takes two rating-like `{ solved, score }` values and decides one reveal of the downward refinement. It is false when either rating is unsolved or has a non-finite score. While `current.score >= spec.max` it keeps the existing rule, `next.score >= spec.min`. Otherwise it needs `current` in the band, both ratings exact, a finite target below `current.score`, `spec.min <= next.score < spec.max` and `abs(next.score − target) < abs(current.score − target)`: after the refinement crosses into the band with a required puzzle it goes on only while it is above the target, and only to a required, in-band, strictly nearer state, so a shallow, under-minimum or non-improving reveal is undone, and an in-band shallow state is not polished by refinement (the caller keeps searching locally instead). With the Expert band `{ min: 700, max: 1000 }` and target 850: 1100 to 920 is allowed, 920 (exact) to 880 (exact) is allowed, 880 to 900 is refused (not nearer, though 920 to 900 would have been), 920 to 840 shallow and 920 to 690 are refused, an exact 800 cannot start another reveal and a shallow 920 cannot be refined. The 64-rating allowance of a refinement, the rule that adds no clue to a model with signs and the `enterLocal`/`leaveLocal` accounting stay the caller's, unchanged.

**The draw and the route (task MS06c2).** In the sample loop of `generate`, after its early-stop and cap guards, every entered sample names its stream, `sampleRng = rng.fork('dig#A.T')`, and draws its own target once, `targetFor(spec, sampleRng)`: `null` for the open band without a random draw, otherwise one draw from the child `target` of that sample. The target goes to `digLevelSteps` as an own `target` property that is put last in the options of the sample, so it **replaces** an `options.target` of the outer caller, which the loop copies into every sample; neither options object is changed, and there is no generation-wide override. At the lower-level entry (`digLevel`, `digLevelSteps`) an own `target` property is honoured as it was given, an explicit zero or `null` included (a value that is not a finite number acts as none and the result says `null`), and only a *missing* property is initialized, once, from that entry's original stream and spec before the keeper view is made; the effective property is then given to the strategy in its resolved options. The direct `digStage` and `digStageSteps` calls do not initialize an absent target and consume a supplied one. The route is: the sample loop's target → `digLevelSteps` → the resolved options → the strategy (`cells` steers its guided stage by it; the registered `signs` strategy of Greater-Than receives the option and does not read it: its first-allowed sign pass compares no candidates) → the keeper view of the sample → `LevelPuzzle.target` and the `tune` verdict → `attemptBest.target` → the local search, which takes `target: attemptBest.target` (null included) and never draws from `search#A`, never reuses the caller's value or the last sample's, and gets the original keeper like every context (a view is never wrapped in a view) → the result, the wire and the soak row. A candidate of a variant's structure search is offered to the original keeper with no target, so a winner that comes from it has `null` even after later samples had finite ones. Equal quality still keeps the candidate met first whatever its target, and no target is compared across samples.

Where the target acts: **(1) the guided pass of the `cells` strategy.** A score-targeted pass first rates its own board once (one guarded logical solve, `assumeUniqueness: false`, the 100,000-check guard, counted once in the oracle counter; a board it cannot rate ends the pass on the board it has). Each pool then starts with its first successful objective as its incumbent, `preferObjective` decides between two successful ones, an all-refused pool goes on with the next, and the winner replaces the state only where `applyBounded` allows it (the table above). When it does not, no other member of the pool would be allowed either, and the pass ends there, on its current board and with its one end record: no further pool, rating or random draw, and no rejection record for the losers. A proposal pass has no state and no target and applies its highest priority, `STUCK_SCORE + unsolvedCount` included. Every `trace.keep` is still the number, the score or the priority. **(2) The downward refinement** of a proposal or a candidate above the band (never of a model with signs) keeps the rule that a state above the band takes any reveal that is solved and at least the band minimum; once a required state is in the band it goes on only while it is above the finite target, and accepts only a required, in-band reveal strictly nearer the target, inside the same 64-rating allowance; a shallow state in the band ends it and the search goes on from there. An exact state the keeper already holds does not stop a refinement that was entered. **(3) The local search** follows a neighbour that is unique, completely rated and below the band maximum when `preferRated` finds it better than its state (the R3 quality first, then the nearer finite target, else the higher score; an equal pair is not followed), so a required neighbour with a lower score is followed, and every unique, completely rated neighbour is still offered to the keeper before the search decides, the ones it refuses included. On Greater-Than the target acts only there and in the continuation: the first-allowed sign pass transports it and does not consume it, and refinement is not entered for models with inequalities. No new post-exact search, budget, cap, floor or sign rule was added, and none of them was relaxed.

**The target as data, nullable (task MS06c1).** The target is carried as data through every context. Every value below is a number (zero included) or `null`, never a midpoint of the band and never inferred from a score; a value that is not a finite number is `null`. The objective of a removal in the guided pass is already typed (`SG.digger.removalObjective(model, spec, proposal)`): `{ kind: 'refused', reason: 'ambiguous' | 'techniques' | 'tooHard' }`, `{ kind: 'bounded', score, tierUsed }` (`tierUsed` is true for a vacuous tier, Very Easy or an empty tier, and otherwise when the same restricted solve used a technique of `spec.tierTechniques`: it is not the requiredness of the puzzle and costs no solve) and `{ kind: 'proposal', priority }` (the score, or `STUCK_SCORE + unsolvedCount` when the techniques below the chain group do not finish the puzzle). The guided pass ranks them with `preferObjective` and `applyBounded` as above; a refusal keeps its reason in the dig log and the events, and `trace.keep` shows the number. Each sample and each search gets a **keeper view** (`SG.digger.keeperView(keeper, target)`): the shared keeper itself through its prototype, so `best`, `distance`, `unsound` and the model-aware `closer` are always live, with an `offer` that hands every candidate to the original keeper (which holds the only state and the only uniqueness check) with the target of its own context, a scalar captured when the view is made and not a variable another context could change while this one is suspended. The structure search of a variant keeps the original keeper, so its candidates have no target. The kept candidate (`SG.digger.createKeeper`) stores the scalar, `SG.digger.keptPuzzle` hands it on, the dig sample (`LevelPuzzle.target`) has the target of its call, a local search has the target of the candidate it continues, and `SG.digger.generate` returns the target of the sample, search or kept candidate that is the result (`GeneratedPuzzle.target`); equal quality still keeps the candidate met first, whatever its target, and no target is compared across samples. `SG.pipeline.run` returns it as `PuzzleResult.target`, which `toWire` always writes and `fromWire` reads (a wire or a cache entry from before it gives `null`; the wire version stays 1), the `tune` events carry a finite one (`target`, left out for none) and the soak row has the target of the selected result. A normal request in a finite band carries the target its sample drew; the target is `null` for an open band, for a result that comes from a variant's structure search and for a wire or cache entry written before the field. The tests that fix this are in `tests/suites/digger-target.test.js`: strict numeric equality at the strategy, the keeper, the continuation and the selected result, the real `signs` route of Greater-Than (seed 319) and the structure-search winner.

> **UX principle:** **never** hang indefinitely. Always return a valid puzzle (a unique solution is absolutely guaranteed), and if the difficulty is not matched exactly, tell the user clearly, with a "Retry with a different seed" button.

#### ⑦ Additional optimisations (task `T-6.3`)

Built (`js/ui/cache.js`, §14.3):
- **Persistent cache:** every generated puzzle is stored in `localStorage` (`sg.cache.v1`, LRU, 20 puzzles, schema-version checked). The same configuration with the same seed is served from it at once. The cache stores the puzzle only, never the trace nor the dig log; a puzzle served from the cache is shown immediately (the replay waits, with the note "Preparing the score tables: rebuilding the generation record…") and is **regenerated from the seed in the background** (an independent client, in a worker; where the page has none, after the press of "Prepare score tables"); its dig log then gives the puzzle its replay through `SG.app.attachTrace(trace, digLog)`, which opens on the finished puzzle. The regenerated puzzle must equal the cached one, otherwise the entry is dropped.
- **Background pre-generation:** once a puzzle is shown and the page has been idle for 1 s, the cache quietly pre-generates one or two puzzles with the same configuration and random seeds in a Web Worker (at most 2 per configuration, 4 in all; never while a generation runs; paused while the tab is hidden; skipped for configurations whose expected time is above 3 s; **absent in main-thread mode**). "Generate" with an empty seed field and "Generate random" hand them out instantly.

Not built: *progressive difficulty (warm start)* — generating a `normal` puzzle first and then digging on to `hard`/`master`. The guided dig of stage B covers the same need differently.

---

## 7. Generation Pipeline & Step Event System

> This section is where the core requirement is realised: **"the UI must walk the user through the steps of generating the game"**.

### 7.1 The seven canonical steps

`SG.pipeline.run(config, emit, budget)` always goes through the 7 steps. A variant may **add** events inside a step (Killer's cage repairs, Wordoku's word fallback…) but must not skip a step: a variant without a `postGenerate` hook still passes through step 4 and emits `extraStructure.none`.

| # | Step ID | Name (VI) | Name (EN) | What it does | Shown in the replay (§7.3) |
|---|---------|-----------|-----------|--------------|----------------------------|
| 1 | `configure` | Chuẩn bị cấu hình | Prepare the settings | Normalise the options, fix the `seed`, look up the variant | Not shown (technical work) |
| 2 | `buildModel` | Dựng mô hình lưới | Build the board model | `variant.build`: cells/units/peers; Jigsaw regions; multi-grid sub-grids; then the `difficultySpec` of the board | Position 0: the empty grid with its regions (also on screen while a run goes on) |
| 3 | `fullGrid` | Tạo một lời giải đầy đủ | Generate a full solution | Randomised DFS + propagation (`attempt` n of m) | Step "Full solution": the whole solution at once (no backtracking is shown) |
| 4 | `extraStructure` | Dựng cấu trúc phụ | Build the extra structure | `variant.postGenerate`: cages (Killer) / inequality signs (Greater-Than) / key word (Wordoku), or "nothing to do" | Step "Cages" (dashed cages and their sums) or "Signs" (every `<` `>` sign); no step for the other variants |
| 5 | `dig` | Đào lỗ và kiểm tra từng lỗ | Dig holes and check each one | §6.8: stage A (random dig), stage B (guided dig), and the uniqueness safety net | Runs of random removals (removed cells framed, cells put back framed dashed red), the removals the generator chose with its own check, the local search's adjustments, the signs of Greater-Than, rarely a restart; then the finished puzzle |
| 6 | `rate` | Chấm độ khó | Rate the difficulty | One full rating with the logical solver | Not shown as a step; the Summary tab has the Difficulty score table of the techniques used |
| 7 | `tune` | Hiệu chỉnh và hoàn tất | Tune and finish | The band check: `tune.hit`, or `tune.shallow` / `tune.miss` and a new sample; `tune.best` when every attempt is used | Not shown (a sample that missed the level is never replayed); the Summary tab has the cards |

**Steps 5–7 repeat per dig sample.** Rating and tuning are three steps of their own, not one: the digger's guided stage shares its rating objective with the rater, so "dig" is every removal plus the uniqueness check, "rate" the rating of the finished puzzle, "tune" the band check. When a sample misses the level (`tune.miss`, or `tune.shallow` when it is in the band but needs no technique of the tier), dig → rate → tune repeat **on the same grid** (`dig.start` carries `sample` / `maxSamples`; up to 12 samples per grid, 30 for coarse symmetries) — a grid is expensive and its next dig is cheap. When the samples of a grid are used up, `retry.difficulty` is emitted and steps 3–7 restart with a new grid ("Attempt 2 of 8"; the counter comes from `fullGrid.start` and `retry.difficulty`, the sample counter from `dig.start`; the events reach the trace and the `generate:progress` event of the app, while the page shows the empty board and the Cancel button until the puzzle is ready).

### 7.2 Event schema

```js
/**
 * @typedef {Object} GenerationEvent
 * @property {'step'|'micro'|'metric'|'retry'|'done'|'error'} type
 * @property {string} stepId          one of the 7 step ids, or 'pipeline' for `done` and for errors outside a step
 * @property {'start'|'progress'|'end'} phase
 * @property {number} t               ms since the start (active time: time given away between slices is not counted)
 * @property {number} seq             0, 1, 2, … in emission order (equals the array index of an uncapped trace)
 * @property {string} [i18nKey]       translation key of the description line
 * @property {Object} [i18nArgs]      parameters inserted into the translation, e.g. {cell:'R4C7', value:5}
 * @property {Object} [patch]         incremental change (delta) for redrawing the grid — do NOT send the whole grid
 * @property {Object} [metrics]       { backtracks, nodes, attempts, accepted, rejected, clues }
 */
```

An event is plain data (numbers, strings, booleans, null, arrays, plain objects): it survives `postMessage` and `structuredClone`, and never carries a function or a typed array.

**The event contract is `SG.events.VOCABULARY`**: a list of `{ type, stepId, phase, key, level, args, patch }` entries — every key the core and the variants emit, the lowest verbosity that emits it, the names that may appear in `i18nArgs`, and the patch fields it may carry. `SG.events.lookup(event)` finds the entry of an event and `vocabularyKeys()` lists the keys; the locale packs must translate every key (`i18n.test.js`), and `pipeline.test.js` and the variant suites check every emitted event against it. A variant adds the events of its own by pushing entries (Killer's `extraStructure.repair*`, Wordoku's `extraStructure.wordFallback`, …).

**The band verdicts of step 7.** `tune.hit` (the sample is exact), `tune.miss` (every other miss) and `tune.shallow` (a complete result inside the score band that does not need a technique of the level) carry `requested`, `achieved`, `score`, `scoreMin` and `scoreMax` (`null` for an open band), plus `hardestTechnique` for a hit and `sample` / `maxSamples` for a miss and a shallow. All three may also carry `required` (`true`, `false`, or `null` when the requiredness was not derived) and `target` (a finite number or `null`; left out when there is none); `SG.events.eventProblems` refuses any other value of these two names. The new names supplement the old argument lists, which are unchanged. The text of `tune.shallow` explains the need ("the puzzle does not need a "Hard" technique"), never "wanted Hard, got Hard". **Emitted since the required-exactness engine:** `tune.hit` carries `required: true` (a vacuous Very Easy included), `tune.shallow` carries `required: false` and `tune.miss` carries `required: null` — also for a solved result outside the band, whose requiredness is not derived. A `tune` event carries `target` when the sample's target is a finite number (zero included): every sample of a finite band draws one (§6.8), so the verdicts of a normal request show it. A `null` target (an open band, or an explicit `null` or a value that is not a finite number given to the lower-level dig entry) is left out. A shallow sample is retried like a miss (the `retry` line of the explanation highlights it) and the old argument lists are unchanged.

**Mandatory rule — send deltas, not snapshots.** A `patch` contains only the cells that changed, **at most 8 cells** (`MAX_PATCH_CELLS`):
`{ cells: [{ i: 34, value: 7, state: 'placed' }], highlight: [34], unhighlight: [12] }`.
Cell states: `empty`, `placed`, `given`, `backtracked`. A patch is applied in the order cells → unhighlight → highlight. Sending the entire 369-cell grid with every event would clog the `postMessage` channel for Samurai. The helpers `makePatch`, `applyPatch`, `invertPatch`, `mergePatches`, `splitPatch`, `createView` / `cloneView` (the plain state a patch is applied to), `capTrace` and `createTraceBuffer` live in `SG.events`.

**Three verbosity levels:**

| Level | Events emitted | Used when |
|-------|----------------|-----------|
| `off` | only `step` start/end + `done` + `error` | tests, batch generation, a pre-generation that needs no replay |
| `summary` | adds `step` progress events (the narration: config, model, techniques found, verdicts), `retry`, and a `metric` at most every 50 ms | the pipeline's default, so what the page gets (it sends no `trace`), and what a `full` request becomes above 300 cells |
| `full` | adds every `micro` event (each fill, backtrack, dig, rejection) | direct callers and the tests (applying its patches in order rebuilds the board exactly); the page's replay does not need it |

### 7.3 Replay engine in the UI layer

```
result ──► SG.replay (analysis: worker, or after "Prepare score tables") ──► analysis
                                                                               │
result + analysis ──► SG.player.buildViews (pure) ──► views { compact, details } ──► player ──► board
                                                                                      │ onPosition
                                                              step description (#step-detail)
controls: ⏮ first · ‹ previous · › next · ⏭ last · slider (#scrubber) · "Step k of N" (#player-time)
          · "Show all moves" (#replay-view-details) · status block (#replay-status, #btn-prepare-replay)
(the explanation of the step on show is in the Help dialog, §7.4)
```

- **Finished first, moved by hand.** After a generation the board shows the finished puzzle at once (`player.load(result)`: nothing is rated yet and the controls stay off). The page then prepares the analysis (below); when it arrives, `player.setAnalysis(analysis)` builds both views and shows the **last** position of the compact view. Nothing animates or plays by itself. ⏮ goes to position 0 (the empty grid), ‹ / › move one step, ⏭ returns to the finished puzzle, and the slider (with a translated `aria-valuetext` and a notch under it for every step) jumps anywhere. There is no Play/Pause button, no speed and no speed preference.
- **Two views of the same moves.** The **compact** view has the few steps `SG.replay` chose so that the score never goes down (a step at the end of every phase); the **details** view has one step per complete move, including the moves that lowered the score. The "Show all moves" checkbox (`player.setView('compact' | 'details')`) switches between them and keeps the place: the position is mapped to the step of the other view that shows the same board (the solution, the structure and the finished puzzle by role; a compact step to the details step of its last move; a details step to the compact step that covers it). The view is not remembered between puzzles.
- **Built from the analysis, not from the trace.** `SG.player.buildViews(result, analysis)` (pure, → `{ compact, details, signs, cellCount }`) reads the moves, ratings, views, transitions and proofs of the analysis of `SG.replay` (§7.5, which reads the dig log, §13.9) and the board model of the result; it never rates a board and never changes its input. Given a result without a log, the analysis is `SG.replay.fallback(result, reason)`: the full solution and the finished puzzle only. The page never rates a puzzle itself: the score tables are the ratings of the analysis.
- **Preparation** (`SG.player.createPreparation(options)`, a pure controller; `start(result)`, `press()`, `unavailable(result)`, `cancel()`, `state()`). Once the puzzle is shown it asks the client for `replay(result, { onProgress })` (§9.3): with a Web Worker it starts by itself and the status block shows "Preparing the score tables… k of N"; without one (the main thread, `file://`) nothing runs until the user presses "Prepare score tables" (`replay(result, { allowMain: true })`), the notice says that the page may pause, and the replay controls stay off until then. A puzzle served from the cache waits, with the note "Preparing the score tables: rebuilding the generation record…", for the log that `SG.app.attachTrace` hands over (below). A `budget.cancelled` rejection that is not the consent request means the job was replaced and ends in `idle`; any other failure (including a refused `busy` call) shows the fallback replay. States: `idle`, `waiting` (the client is not ready), `consent`, `trace`, `preparing`, `ready`, `unavailable`, `error`.
- **One step = one visible change of the board.** Technical work (the settings, the board model, the rating, the tuning, samples that missed the level) is not shown. Position 0 is the empty grid, position *k* shows step *k*. The title kind of a step comes from its role and the phases of the moves it covers:

| Kind | Title | What the board shows | Card (`phase` / line) |
|------|-------|----------------------|-----------------------|
| `solution` | Full solution | Every value of `result.solution` | `fullGrid` / `place` |
| `cages` | Cages | Killer: the cages and their sums appear | `extraStructure` / `build` |
| `signs` | Signs | Greater-Than: every sign appears | `extraStructure` / `build` |
| `clues` | Starting clues | Greater-Than: the grid is emptied and a few clues are kept (dotted frame) | `dig` / — |
| `random` | Random removals | Removals chosen at random: the cells cleared by the step are framed (`cell--dug`), the cells that were tried and put back are framed dashed red (`cell--kept`); one sentence per run of moves ("Clear 9 cells chosen at random: the puzzle can still be solved without their clues", "Try to clear 10 cells chosen at random: 8 cleared, 2 put back because …") | `dig` / `check` |
| `dig` | Removing clues | The same for a log whose removals are not in random order | `dig` / `check` |
| `guided` | Choosing clues to remove | Removals taken after the generator's own check compared candidates ("Compare 12 (4 rejected) and choose the removal preferred by the generator's check: …"); refused candidates are framed `cell--kept` | `dig` / `deeper` |
| `search` | Adjusting the puzzle | The local search of the top levels: clues cleared and restored while looking for a higher-scoring puzzle | `dig` / `deeper` |
| `signDig` | Removing signs | Greater-Than: signs tried; removed signs are drawn dashed and faded, signs that had to stay red | `dig` / `check` |
| `reveal` | More clues | Greater-Than: the puzzle came out too hard, so more clues are shown (dotted frame, `cell--revealed`) | `dig` / — |
| `mixed` | Combined changes | A compact step that covers moves of more than one phase | `dig` / the line of its last move |
| `restart` | Starting again | The safety net threw a dug puzzle away and digs again from the full grid (rare) | `dig` / `verify` |
| `final` | Finished puzzle | The clues of the result (and its signs), no frame | `dig` / `verify` |

A compact step that folds several moves says so in its sentence: "Within this step, the score reached {peak}." (the variant that adds how many clues were restored during refinement when the peak was above the chosen level, and "{n} positions within this step could not be rated" when some of them had no rating); a restart step says what the generation had reached before it, and a replay rebuilt from the final clues only (`analysis.rebuilt`) carries the note that it is not a record of the original order.

Example: Classic 9×9, *easy*, seed 3 replays in 7 steps (26 with every move shown): Full solution (score 0); Random removals ×3 (scores 15, 26, 44); Choosing clues to remove ×2 (46, 100); Finished puzzle, 34 clues (100). Killer, *hell 2*, seed 11 has 9 compact steps (38 with every move shown): Full solution, Cages (both 0), Random removals ×2 (27, 98), Choosing clues to remove ×4 (162, 517, 579, 3723) and the finished puzzle (3723). Larger boards and higher levels take more.

- **Steps are plain data:** `{ index, view, role, first, last, position, kind, phase, line, parts, key, args, meta, values, structure, marks, signs, metrics, context, table }`. `role` is `solution`, `structure`, `moves`, `restart` or `final`; `first` / `last` are the moves it covers and `position` the position of the analysis it shows; `parts` are the sentences (one per run of moves of one kind, each `{ key, args }`, composed by `describeStep` with the notes above) and `key` / `args` the title; `meta` is the one-line summary ("9 cleared · 72 clues left"). `values` is the board after the step (shared with the analysis, never mutated, so seeking needs no checkpoints or inverse patches), `structure` tells whether cages and signs are drawn, `marks` holds the frames `{ dug, kept, revealed, cause, causeRestore }` and `signs` the sign marks `{ hidden, removed, kept, cause }` by index into `buildViews().signs` — the cell marks `dug`, `kept`, `revealed` and the sign marks `hidden`, `removed`, `kept` are the **net** change of the step (a cell cleared and restored within it is not framed), while `cause`, `causeRestore` and `signs.cause` hold the one clue or sign that causes a score jump (the cell then leaves `dug` or `revealed`; a sign stays in `removed`, because it is still a removed constraint), `metrics` the running dig counters `{ attempts, accepted, rejected, clues }` over the moves up to the step (null for the solution, the structure and any fallback step), and `context` the frozen `{ moves, signs }` they share. The counters are cumulative and counted in what the board shows — cells, or signs for Greater-Than — so they add up with the step description. `table` is the **model of the score table** of the step: `{ state, status, reasons, counts, before, empties, score, change, transition, proof, position }` where `state` is `start` (position 0), `zero` (the solution, the structure and a restart: nothing to solve yet), `scored` (the techniques and empty cells of the board), `final` (the finished puzzle) or `status` (the board could not be rated: `status` and `reasons` say why); `before` is the counts of the previous board when it had a total, `change` the change of the Total (`transition.change`; null at the compact restart), and `proof` the first use of the new hardest technique. The step description draws every state (below). `viewAt(view, position)` is the board at a position of a view (`views.compact` or `views.details`); `describeStep`, `stepTitle`, `stepMeta` and `positionText` are the texts (`replay.*` keys).
- **One place drives the rest.** The player's `onPosition({ position, length, step, direction })` (`direction` is `'forward'`, `'backward'` or `'seek'`; `step` is null at position 0) is the only thing that moves the step description, and the app re-emits it as its `position` event, so nothing can be out of step with the board. The Help dialog reads the same position when it opens (`SG.app.getExplainContext()`, §7.4). `tableAt(position)` of the player instance gives the table model of the step at a position, which the step description turns into its score table (`js/ui/app.js` gives it to the step description as its `table` option; the page no longer rates a board itself).
- **Board.** The structure layers (`cages`, `ineq`; `SG.player.STRUCTURE_LAYERS`) are hidden until the step that builds them; the frames are highlight classes of `layer-overlay` ([§10.3](#103-animation-language)). Greater-Than draws every sign during the replay (`SG.greaterThan.withSigns(result.model, signs)`), marks them by index (`board.setSignMarks({ hidden, removed, kept, cause })`) and switches to the model of the result at the last position. A score jump that exactly one clue or sign causes (Q10 b) gets a **frame**: a solid accent frame on the cell (`cell--cause`, for a removed or a restored clue; the legend says which) or, for a sign, a separate rectangle in the sign layer around its gap (`rect.sign--cause`, `data-cause-sign`; the sign itself keeps its faded `sign--removed` look). A jump whose parts give nearly nothing alone, whose parts are mixed, that covers several moves or whose parts could not all be rated has no frame; no slider mark and no proof is ever drawn on the board. The other overlays (regions, windows, the Wordoku line) come from the model, not from the log. "Show solution" draws the solution only at the last position.
- **Step description** (`SG.stepView.create(rootEl, { table })`, `js/ui/step-view.js`), in the **Generation steps** tab under the replay controls (`#step-detail`): a heading "Step k – Title" with an **Explain** icon (opens the Help) and, for a first rated puzzle or a score jump, a **badge** in the heading (text, not colour only: "First rated puzzle", "Score jump ×12.5"), the one-line summary ("9 cells removed · 72 clues left"), the full sentence, the **tier line** or the **callout** below (see the next item), the **score table of the puzzle at that step** and the legend of the marks the steps use (removed in this step / tried, but put back / clue shown in this step / sign removed in this step / sign tried, but kept / the clue or sign that causes a score jump, whose wording says removing or restoring), at the right. Every step has a table, drawn by the `state` of its `table` model: `start` (position 0: the hint to use the replay controls and "No score yet: this is not a puzzle yet."), `zero` (nothing to solve yet: the line "No solving technique is needed yet.", only the Empty cells row with 0 and a Total of 0; a restart of the details view shows its drop, "0 (−551)"), `scored` and `final` (Technique section with uses — "(+n)" against the step shown before — points and share of the score per technique and a help icon on each name; Other section with the empty cells; a Total row that shows the change from the step before: "551 (+507)", "992 (unchanged)", "1,940 (−848)"; at the last step the same total as the Summary tab) and `status` (details view, a board that cannot be rated: the sentence of its status, a note that the rows are techniques the solver applied and not a validated rating, and those rows with their uses; no points, work, score, Total, tooltip or retry button, and the page never rates the board again — a missing final rating and a contradiction have sentences of their own). At position 0 it also shows a hint to use the replay controls. There is no list of steps: the user moves with the replay controls. The instance offers `setSteps`, `setPosition`, `clear`, `getPosition`, `refresh`, `destroy`; the constant `STEP_IDS` lists the seven pipeline steps, and `SG.stepView.chartModel` (technique order and labels) and `roundToTotal` (whole numbers that add up to a total) are shared with the result panel (§10.5).
- **Tiers, the badge and the jump callout** (`SG.stepView.explain(table, step)`, pure: the Total and its change, the badge, the tier line, the row label, the callout, the status sentences; the tests read it). The tier of a step comes from the transition between it and the step shown before in the same view: **first rated puzzle** (the first step with a score, once per view; no multiplier), **score jump** (a rise of at least ×3 **and** at least +100 points from a positive score, decided on the exact totals by `SG.replay.isJump`; the badge shows the ratio rounded down to one decimal in the format of the language, ×12.5 or ×12,5) and **new hardest technique** (one line, also on a flat or falling step of the details view, and a "New hardest technique" label on the row of the technique; a jump whose hardest technique was already used highlights the Total row instead). A jump has a **callout** (`div.step-callout`, after the score table): the heading "Score jump: 44 → 551 (+507)", two or three sentences — what the generator's solution now uses (or how its weighted work grew), that the largest technique weight counts in full as the P term, and the cause: the single clue or sign whose removal alone gives nearly the whole jump, parts that give almost nothing alone ("45 and 44 … 870"), a mixed move, one sign or one clue with no smaller move, the complete change in which the technique first appears (several moves), or no cause named — and a closed **Why?** (`details.step-why`) with one line each for P, W, R, E, V, the Total and the level, what the log records about the candidates the generator compared (and that its limited check could not finish the chosen board, when so), the restricted check ("In a separate check, this solver stopped before completing the board using only techniques with weights below P", shown only after a stuck check), the audit scope (only when the audit looked at rated boards only), the first use of the technique in the generator's solution (deduction *k* of *n*, the solver's own paragraph, every candidate removed and digit placed) and the scope line. Every sentence speaks of the generator's solution, never of what a puzzle needs in general; the numbers come from the analysis and are never recomputed by the page.
- **Cache and the main-mode gate.** A puzzle served from the cache has no dig log (it is never stored): the replay waits, in the `trace` state of the preparation, until the background regeneration hands the log over through `SG.app.attachTrace(trace, digLog)` ([§6.8 ⑦](#68--optimal-hole-digging-strategy)); the replay then continues like a fresh puzzle. The regeneration runs in a worker only: where the page has none (`file://`) it starts after the user's press of "Prepare score tables" (the app's `replay:consent` event, which `SG.cache` listens to) and on the page, and when no log can be had (no worker, a failure, a different puzzle) `SG.app.markTraceUnavailable()` shows the fallback replay.

### 7.4 Explanation content

Each step has a bilingual explanation card with 3 fixed parts — **What / Why / How**. The comments below quote the example Vietnamese UI copy (kept verbatim as product strings), each followed by an English rendering:

```js
SG.explain['dig'] = {                // js/core/explain-data.js — pure data, keys only, no DOM
  stepId: 'dig', index: 5, name: 'step.dig.name',
  what:  'explain.dig.what',   // vi: "Loại bỏ dần các số khỏi lời giải hoàn chỉnh." (en: "Gradually remove numbers from the complete solution.")
  why:   'explain.dig.why',    // vi: "Mỗi ô bị bỏ đi làm bài khó hơn, nhưng phải..." (en: "Every removed cell makes the puzzle harder, but it must...")
  how:   'explain.dig.how',    // vi: "Sau mỗi lần bỏ, ta kiểm tra bài có còn..." (en: "After every removal, we check whether the puzzle still...")
  pseudocode: [ { label, indent, key }, … ],   // one entry per line; `label` is what the UI highlights
  highlight: { 'dig.try': 'try', … },          // event i18nKey → pseudocode line label
  // Content that changes with the real-time context
  live: function (metrics, t) { return { key: 'explain.dig.live', args: { /* numbers read from metrics */ } }; }
};
```

The data module is `SG.explain` (core); the UI card that renders it is **`SG.explainCard`** (`js/ui/explain.js`) — the two names must not be confused. The cards live in the **Help dialog**, tab "How it works" (`js/ui/help.js`), not on the page: one collapsible entry (`<details class="disclosure help-step">`) per pipeline step, each with a compact card (`SG.explainCard.create(el, { compact: true })`, no kicker and no title, the entry's summary names the step): the three parts, the **pseudocode** (turning the site into an algorithm-learning tool) and, in the entry of the step on show, a "Now" line. The entry that the replay step on show belongs to (`fullGrid`, `extraStructure` or `dig`; `SG.app.getExplainContext()`, at position 0 the first of them) is marked "Now: replay step k of N", is open the first time the tab is drawn, and shows the pseudocode line of the replay step highlighted and, during the dig only, the counters ("Tried · accepted · rejected · clues left", cumulative and counted in cells, signs for Greater-Than, so they add up with the step description). The user opens and closes the entries freely (the open ones stay open when the language changes). The **Explain** icon of the step description (`#btn-explain`, `action { id: 'help', tab: 'generation' }`) opens the Help on that tab. The card re-renders on a language change keeping the step, the line and the metrics.

### 7.5 The replay analysis (`SG.replay`)

`js/engine/replay.js` is the data layer of the Generation Steps tab: it reads a finished `PuzzleResult` (`model`, `solution`, `givens`, `rating`, `config`, `digLog`) and never changes it, so the generation algorithm, every puzzle and every regression hash stay as they are. It is pure (no DOM, no clock, no random numbers, no state of its own) and runs after the result exists, on the main thread or in a worker. The page asks for it once the puzzle is shown and builds the steps of its replay from it with `SG.player.buildViews` (§7.3). The design, the decisions Q1–Q19 cited here and the research behind them are in [docs/generation-steps/report-detailed.md](../docs/generation-steps/report-detailed.md).

**Moves.** `SG.replay.moves(result)` → `{ ok, reason, moves, signs, structure, rebuilt, randomOrder, finishedAlive }` and never calls a solver. *A move is a complete change of the board derived from the log*: a symmetric group removed, an exchange (a removal and the clues the local search shows again), one sign, the starting clues, a group of clues shown again, or a restart. No position shows half of one. `moves[0]` is the full solution, the last move is the finished puzzle, and every move carries the board after it (`values`, 0 = empty) and the signs still on it (`alive`, null without signs). The log entries become moves like this:

| Log entries | Move (`kind`) |
|-------------|---------------|
| `accept` of a group in the random pass (or `fallback`, or before any pass) | `random`: the cells are removed |
| `accept` of a group in a guided pass, after at least one `keep` | `guided` |
| `accept` of a group in a guided pass with no `keep` since the previous move (the local search) | `searchRemove`; a `reveal` with `reason: 'search'` right after it joins it into one `exchange` (the removed and the restored cells together) |
| `reveal` with `reason: 'search'` that follows no `accept` | `searchAdd` |
| `reveal` without a reason (the downward refinement) | `reveal` |
| `clear` and the `reveal`s before the first sign pass (Greater-Than) | one `clues` move: the starting clues |
| `accept` with a `sign` | `sign`: the cells stay, the sign leaves `alive` |
| `restart` | `restart`: the full solution again |
| `keep`, `reject` | no move: they are summed in the `span` of the next accept (`rated`, `refused`, `chosen`, `others`, `nulls`, `max`, `rejected`, `rejectedSigns`, `reasons`; after the last accept of a pass, in its `trail`) |
| `pass`, `signs` | no move |

If the log is missing, an entry is unreadable (a cell outside the board, a sign that does not exist) or the last board is not `result.givens` (or the signs left are not the model's), `ok` is false with `reason` `noLog`, `unreadable` or `mismatch`, and `analyse` returns a conservative analysis: the solution, the structure step (Killer, Greater-Than) and the finished puzzle with the rating the generator recorded, without any solver call. Killer's rebuilt log (the layout search of a request at Expert or above leaves one random pass that removes, in order, every group without a clue) is recognised only by that exact shape and only for a level asked at Expert or above; its moves do not claim a random order (`randomOrder` is false).

**Ratings.** `SG.replay.rateBoard(model, values, options)` gives the canonical rating of one board: `SG.logicalSolver.solve` with every technique and `assumeUniqueness: true` under a guard of 1,000,000 checks (`rateNodes`), classified by `SG.difficulty.classify`; when the proof used a technique that assumes uniqueness (Unique Rectangle), the solutions are counted too (`SG.dfs.countSolutions`, a guard of 200,000 nodes, `countNodes`) and a score is published only for a count of 1. `SG.replay.prepare(result)` is an iterator that rates the board after every move, **once per distinct board** (a memo keyed by the board and the signs on it), and yields `{ done, total }` after each position, then explains the jumps (below) and yields `{ done: total, total }` again after each probe and each optional solve; `analyse` drives it to its end. The finished puzzle is put in the memo first with `result.rating` and is never rated again (its own rating can need more than 120,000 checks). A rating is `{ status, reasons, score, counts, empties, hardest, P, W, R, V, level, checks, memo }`; the statuses are:

| Status | Meaning | Score | Rows |
|--------|---------|-------|------|
| `rated` | a complete proof of a unique solution (by the proof or by the count) | yes | the proof's counts |
| `zero` | the solution, the structure step, a restart | 0 | none |
| `final` | the finished puzzle: `result.rating` | yes | the Summary's |
| `capped` | the guard ran out (an honest status, no retry) | no | none |
| `stuck` | the solver stopped before completing the board (`reasons` adds `ambiguous` when the count finds two solutions, `contradiction` when there are none) | no | the techniques it applied, as attempted |
| `ambiguous` | the count found at least two solutions | no | the attempted rows |
| `unverified` | the count ran out of its guard, or a fallback has no final rating from the generator (`missingRating`) | no | the attempted rows, none for `missingRating` |

`P`, `W`, `R` and `V` are the parts of the score (`SG.difficulty.breakdown`: the hardest weight, the work, the work term and the empty-cell term). `checks` is the work of the solve (`budget.nodes`): the subset techniques space their budget checks with a counter that lives across solves, so it can differ by a few between two runs of one board; every other field is a function of the result.

The **Analysis** is plain data (structured-clone safe, no text): `{ version, fallback, rebuilt, randomOrder, structure, signs, level, band, moves, ratings, chapter, views, transitions, proofs, stats }`, with `ratings[i]` belonging to `moves[i]`, `stats = { positions, fresh, memo, zero, checks }` and `views.details.steps` listing the solution, the structure (when there is one), one step per move and the finished puzzle (the example of the tests, Classic 9×9 Expert seed 3275974478, has 28 positions and 29 steps; 26 are rated, one comes from the memo, one is the solution). `views` and `transitions` are filled by `SG.replay.buildViews` (below); `proofs` is filled by the explanation of the jumps (below), `{}` without it. `SG.replay.toPlainWire(result)` / `fromPlainWire(data)` carry a result as JSON-safe data (the typed arrays of `SG.pipeline.toWire` become plain arrays); the `replay` suite reads the five slow golden results (G09 to G13) saved that way (`tests/fixtures/replay-wire.js`, §15.2).

#### Views, selection, audit and tiers

`SG.replay.buildViews(analysis)` → `{ chapter, views, transitions }` builds the two views of the replay and the transition between every two adjacent steps of each. It is pure (it reads `moves`, `ratings`, `structure`, `band` and `fallback`, returns new objects and calls no solver); `prepare`, `analyse` and `fallback` assign its result to their analysis, and the tests call it on synthetic analyses. The **compact** view is the default; the **details** view ("Show all moves") has one step per move.

A `ViewStep` is `{ role, first, last, position, phases, kinds, transition }`: `role` is `solution`, `structure`, `restart`, `moves` or `final`; `first … last` are the moves it covers, `position` (= `last`) the move whose board it shows, `phases` and `kinds` the distinct phases and kinds of those moves in order of first appearance (empty for the solution, structure and final steps), and `transition` the index into `analysis.transitions` (null for the first step). The **details** view is the solution, the structure step (cages or signs, when there is one), one step per move and the finished puzzle, which repeats the last position; its scores really go down (an exchange, a refinement) and its status steps have no score; a restart is a step of role `restart` showing the full solution again, so the drop to 0 is visible and both digs are shown. A conservative fallback (see **Moves** above) has the solution, the structure step and the finished puzzle in both views, built without a selection and without a duplicate of the final position; the transition into its final step uses the real difference.

**The compact selection** (`SG.replay.select(entries, options)`, pure, on `entries[i] = { score, phase, kind }`, one per move; the constants `MIN_GAIN = 10`, `R_MIN = 1.3`, `R_MAX = 3`, `R_EXP = 1/6` are in `SG.replay.CONSTANTS`, and `f10(x) = max(x, 10)`):

1. **Chapter.** `s` (also `analysis.chapter`) is the index of the last `restart` move, or 0; `e` is the last position at or after `s` that has a score (normally the finished puzzle, which keeps `result.rating`). An unrated tail is never attached to an earlier board.
2. **Eligible positions.** `s`, `e` and every rated `i` between them whose score is not above the score of any later rated position up to `e`. The others are folded into the step that contains them, which is what makes the compact view never go down (the step tells the peak it folded, `folded.peak`).
3. **Ratio.** `R = clamp((f10(score(e)) / 10) ^ R_EXP, R_MIN, R_MAX)`; the example (925) gives 2.13.
4. **Cuts, with a rank** (a position's rank is the largest rule that cuts it): `s` (4) and `e` (3); a **rise** (3): for consecutive eligible `a < b` with `f10(score(b)) >= R × f10(score(a))`, both; a **phase end** (2): the last eligible position of every maximal run of moves of one `phase` in `(s, e]` (a run with none gives no cut); a **fill** (1): walking the eligible positions after `s` with `last` the latest cut, `i` when `f10(score(i)) >= R × f10(score(last))` and `score(i) − score(last) >= MIN_GAIN`.
5. **Small steps.** While two consecutive cuts differ by less than `MIN_GAIN`: drop the later one if its rank is 1, else the earlier one if its rank is 1 and it is not `s`, else keep both (a flat step between two protected cuts, such as a phase end).
6. **Audit** (below).
7. **Steps shown.** The solution; the structure step; when `s > 0`, the "Starting again" step (`role: 'restart'`, `first: 1`, `last: s`; its board is the full solution, and the abandoned dig is folded into it: `folded.restart = { peak, moves }`); one `moves` step per cut after `s`, ending on its board; and the finished puzzle (`role: 'final'`, the position of `e` again).

```text
select(entries):
  s = lastIndexOfKind(entries, 'restart') or 0
  e = last finite-scored index >= s; if none: return { s, e: -1, ratio: R_MIN, cuts: [] }
  eligible = [i in s..e : i == s or i == e or (scored(i) and score(i) <= min score of scored j in (i, e])]
  R = min(R_MAX, max(R_MIN, (f10(score(e)) / 10) ^ R_EXP))
  rank = { s: 4 }; rank[e] = max(rank[e], 3)
  for consecutive a < b in eligible: if f10(score(b)) >= R * f10(score(a)): rank a and b at least 3
  for each maximal run of one phase in (s, e]: p = its last eligible position; if p: rank p at least 2
  last = s
  for i in eligible, i > s:
    if i in rank: last = i
    else if f10(score(i)) >= R * f10(score(last)) and score(i) - score(last) >= MIN_GAIN: rank[i] = 1; last = i
  repeat until stable: for consecutive cuts a < b with score(b) - score(a) < MIN_GAIN:
    if rank[b] == 1: drop b   else if rank[a] == 1 and a != s: drop a
  return { s, e, ratio: R, cuts: sorted positions with their rank }
```

For the example (Classic 9×9 Expert, seed 3275974478) the cuts are 7 (a fill: 23 ≥ 2.13 × 10), 22 (the end of the random run, 44), 25 and 26 (the rise 47 → 593) and 27 (the end, 925); the guided moves 23 to 25 fold into the step that ends at 25, since none of them is a rise, a phase end or a fill. The compact scores are `0, 23, 44, 47, 593, 925, 925` (7 steps) and the details view has 29 steps. (Since MS08a, when the skyscraper joined the Hard tier, the example is another puzzle; since MS08b, when the two-string kite joined it, its jump is the Two-String Kite step 47 → 593 (it was the Simple Colouring step 47 → 621), caused by R2C4, and its local search ends in an exchange.)

**The audit** (`SG.replay.audit(entries, positions)` → `{ positions, edges }`) makes sure that a jump left in the compact view cannot be removed with the moves the generator recorded (whole moves only; the page never claims that no easier board exists). For every pair of consecutive cut positions `a < b` that is a jump (below) and spans two moves or more: the *nodes* are the rated positions `j` between them with `score(a) <= score(j) <= score(b)`; an edge `u → v` is allowed when `score(u) <= score(v)` and *calm* when it is not a jump, with `ratio(u, v) = f10(score(v)) / f10(score(u))`. A **bridge** is a path of calm edges, with the fewest inner nodes and then the smallest largest ratio: its nodes are inserted as steps. Without a bridge, the path over allowed edges with the smallest largest ratio (then the fewest nodes) is inserted when it lowers the largest ratio, its new edges are audited again, and the jump that remains is kept. Each audited edge is recorded as `{ from, to, scope, inserted, remaining }`; the `scope` is `complete` when every position inside the edge has a score and `ratedOnly` when some has none (such a position is never a bridge nor proof that none exists). With the example vector of scores `0, 23, 44, 100, 200, 400, 800, 992` the selection gives `0, 23, 44, 100, 400, 992`, whose edge 100 → 400 (×4, +300) is a jump, and the audit inserts 200. On the 107 requests of the research no edge needed an insertion and every scope was `complete`. A transition of the compact view carries the `scope` of the narrowest record that contains it (`auditScope`), else null.

**Transitions and tiers.** `analysis.transitions` lists the transitions of the compact view first, then those of the details view; `transition = { view, step, from, to, role, before, after, change, tier, ratio, newHardest, folded, auditScope, cause, choice }`: it leads from step `step − 1` to step `step` of its view (`from` and `to` are their positions; `role` is `final` for the finished-puzzle step), `cause` and `choice` are `null` except on a jump, where `prepare` fills them in (see **Explaining a jump** below; `buildViews` itself leaves them `null`). `before` and `after` are the `Terms` of the two positions, `{ P, W, E, V, R, total, level, hardest, counts }` (the rating's parts; all numbers 0 for a zero rating); `change` is `after.total − before.total`, or null when a total is missing and for the introductory structure step (a details restart keeps its real drop to 0; the final duplicate has change 0). `folded = { peak, aboveBand, revealed, unscored, restart }` says what the step folds: the largest score inside it when above its end (`peak`, and `aboveBand` when it reaches the top of the chosen level's band), the clues restored by its refinement moves (`revealed`), the positions without a score (`unscored`) and, for the compact "Starting again" step only, the abandoned dig. The **tier**, on the exact integer totals and in this order:

| Tier | Rule |
|------|------|
| `first` | `before.total` is 0, `after.total` is above 0, and the view has no `first` yet (once per view: in the details view the first rated board after a restart gets none) |
| `jump` | `before.total` is above 0, `after.total − before.total >= 100` (`JUMP_GAIN`) **and** `after.total >= 3 × before.total` (`JUMP_RATIO`): `SG.replay.isJump(before, after)` |
| `newPeak` | `before.total` is above 0 and the hardest technique of the end board was not used by the board before (`!(before.counts[after.hardest] > 0)`: a count of 0 is not a use), whether the score rises, stays or falls |
| none | otherwise, also when a total is missing (a status step) |

`newHardest` is the condition of `newPeak` on its own, so a jump can carry it too (the "New hardest technique" row label); a jump without it keeps its hardest technique and the weighted sum of uses grew. For a jump, `ratio = SG.replay.badgeRatio(before.total, after.total)` = `Math.floor(10 × after / before) / 10` (551 / 44 → 12.5, 300 / 100 → 3.0, 1000 / 300 → 3.3): one decimal rounded down, so it never overstates and is never below 3.0; the tier itself is decided on the totals, never on the written ratio. The transition belongs to its view: the same end position can be a jump in one view and not in the other (Greater-Than Expert 42, move 111: 119 → 957 in the compact view, 962 → 957 in the details view). The `phaseCode(step, structure)` of a step (`S/Sol`, `S/Cag`, `S/Sig`, `S/Restart`, `D/Dig`, `G/Choose`, `X/Adjust`, `A/Restore`, `C/Clues`, `I/Signs`, `IM/Signs`, `F/Final`; letters joined with `+` and `/Mixed` for several phases, e.g. `G+A/Mixed`) names it in the tables of the tests.

#### Explaining a jump

A jump is explained from the generator's own solution and its limited check, never as a mathematical necessity. After the ratings, `prepare` fills, for every transition of tier `jump` (in both views), `cause` and `choice`, and `analysis.proofs[position]` for the end position of each; the pair `(from, to)` is explained once inside one analysis and the views share the result, and a proof belongs to its end position. `options.explain === false` skips all of it (`cause` and `choice` stay `null`, `proofs` stays `{}`; the moves, ratings, views and tiers are the same), and `options.probeNodes` (default `RATE_NODES`) is the guard of every optional solve. Each probe, first-use solve and restricted solve is one unit of work followed by a progress report. **Optional work never undoes a rating**: each unit runs under `try … catch`, and a failure (a guard, an error) leaves that piece `unavailable` or `null`; `prepare` never throws because of it.

**The cause** says which part of the move carries the jump. With `before` and `after` the totals of the transition:

| Class | When | Data |
|-------|------|------|
| `multi-move` | the step covers several moves | `n` moves; `k` / `kMove` (the first move of the step, 1-based within it, whose rated board uses the new hardest technique) stay `null` when `before.counts` already used it, `verifiedOnly` when an earlier position of the step has no score; no frame |
| `single-sign` | one sign move | `sign`; frame `{ kind: 'sign', sign }` |
| `single-cell` | one move with one change | frame `{ kind: 'cell', cell, action }` |
| `single-culprit` | 2 to 4 changes (`PROBE_MAX_CELLS`), exactly one share at or above `CULPRIT_SHARE` (0.9) | `culprit = { cell, action, score, otherScore }` (`otherScore`: the score of all the other changes together); the same frame |
| `together` | 2 to 4 changes, every share at most `TOGETHER_SHARE` (0.1) | no frame |
| `mixed` | 2 to 4 changes, any other split (two shares of 0.9 or more included) | `largest = { cell, action, score }` (ties: the lower cell); no frame |
| `unavailable` | no change, more than 4 changes, or a probe that is not rated | `changes` only; no frame |

The *changes* of a move are its removed clues (ascending) and then its restored clues (ascending), `changes = [{ cell, action: 'remove' | 'restore' }]`. For 2 to 4 changes the **partial boards** are rated: the board before the move with every proper non-empty subset of the changes applied (the single changes first, in that order, then the other subsets by bit mask), each by `rateBoard` under `probeNodes`; a subset that removes a clue must also pass the independent count of the solutions even when its logical proof claims uniqueness (a pure restoration on the certified board before the move keeps it unique). They are probes only: never a move, a step, a rating of the analysis nor a memo entry. `singles[i].score` is the score with the change `i` alone, and `SG.replay.classifyShares(before, after, singles)` → `{ class, index }` takes the share of a change as `max(0, ln(score / before) / ln(after / before))`. A **frame** is given only when exactly one clue or sign causes the jump (`single-culprit`, `single-cell`, `single-sign`); two shares of 0.9 or more give `mixed` and no frame. In the example (Classic 9×9 Expert, seed 3275974478) the step 47 → 593 removes R2C4 and R8C6: R2C4 alone scores 593 and R8C6 alone 47, so R2C4 (share 1) is the culprit and is framed; without it the removal of R8C6 stays at 47 (`otherScore`).

**The choice** is what the log records about the candidates compared before the move: `{ kind, count, chosen, at, rated, refused, others, nulls, max }`. `combined` (`count` moves in the step); `none` for a rebuilt Killer log and for a `reveal`, `searchAdd` or `clues` move; `guided` for one guided move, from its span (`chosen` is the chosen candidate's logged score, `at` its log offset, then the numbers of candidates rated, refused, scored others, unscored and the largest other score); `random` when the order of the dig was random, otherwise `checked`; `checked` also for an `exchange`, a `searchRemove` and a `sign` move (the log holds no comparable scores for them). A `null` `chosen` is not 0, a time-out or a failed uniqueness test: it is the dig's objective for a board its limited check did not finish, ranked above every finished one. The example: 5 candidates, 3 refused, 1 other of at most 48, chosen 593 at offset 67.

**The proof** of an end position is `{ firstUse, restricted }`. `SG.replay.firstUse(model, values, technique, options)` solves the end board with every technique and the steps kept and returns the first deduction that uses the new hardest technique: `{ k, n, technique, i18nKey, i18nArgs, eliminations, placements }`, the `k`-th of `n` deductions with every effect; it is `null` when the board is not solved within `options.rateNodes` (an unfinished proof is not the generator's solution), when the technique is not used and on an error (the example: 26 of 61, Two-String Kite, candidate 3 removed from R1C9). `SG.replay.restrictedCheck(model, values, w, options)` solves it again with the techniques that weigh less than `w` (the weight of the new hardest technique) and says `'stuck'`, `'complete'` or `'unavailable'`; the page may say that *this solver* stops without the new technique only after `'stuck'`, never that no easier proof exists.
---

## 8. Variant-Specific Algorithms

### 8.1 Jigsaw — generating irregular regions

**Requirement:** partition the `D×D` grid into `D` connected regions (connected polyominoes), each with exactly `D` cells.

**Algorithm: seeded growth with backtracking.**

```
1. Place D seeds spread evenly (one seed per row, in distinct columns, one in each 3×3 box of the classic layout).
2. Repeat until every cell is assigned:
     - Pick the currently smallest region (prefer the region most starved of cells).
     - Take the set of border cells that are adjacent to that region and not yet in any region.
     - Exclude the cells that, if taken, would disconnect the remaining free space
       into pieces whose size is not divisible by D  (flood-fill check, `isFeasible`).
     - Choose randomly with weights: prefer cells with few free neighbours
       (reduces the risk of creating "islands").
     - If no valid cell remains → undo the last move and try its next candidate;
       beyond maxBacktracks (60 undone moves) → discard the attempt and draw new seeds
       (at most 200 attempts, then the error `jigsaw.layoutFailed`).
3. Validation: every region has exactly D cells, is connected, is not a bare row or column, and the layout
   has a solution (run generateFullGrid with a small node budget — 4000 nodes, small restarts —
   and a plain DFS of 5000 nodes). If it has no solution → drop it like a dead end.
```

Everything is driven by the rng and by node counts only, never by the clock, so a seed gives the same layout on every machine (invariant I4). The layout depends on the seed, so a jigsaw puzzle is never "the empty board of the variant": `model.layout.cellRegion` holds the region number (0–8, in reading order of each region's first cell), and a thick border belongs on every edge between two different regions.

**Aesthetic parameter:** `compactness` (0–1) controls the weight that favours cells touching the region on many sides. A high value → rounded regions; a low value → winding regions (prettier, but the puzzle is harder). Default `0.6`.

> ⚠️ Not every Jigsaw layout has a solution. The validation step at (3) is **mandatory**.

### 8.2 Killer — generating cages

```
Input: a grid that already has a complete solution.
1. Partition all the cells into connected cages:
     - The target size is drawn at random from a difficulty-dependent distribution:
         veryEasy/easy : {2:45%, 3:40%, 4:15%}       → small cages, easy to reason about
         normal        : {2:30%, 3:35%, 4:25%, 5:10%}
         hard … nightmare   : {2:15%, 3:30%, 4:30%, 5:20%, 6:5%}   (expert and master included)
         hell1/2  : the nightmare shares, tilted towards larger cages by +2 on the cage-size slider
                         (clamp(cageSize + 2, −2, 2): +2 at the default position)
     - Constraint: the values within a cage must be PAIRWISE DIFFERENT
```
